# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.0.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- **#1015 review round: two test-infrastructure defects, both inside the test setup added to end exactly that class of silence.** virgil found them; both are one-liners with measured remedies. **(1) The tampered-token test failed roughly 1 run in 15.** A 43-character base64url HS256 signature carries 258 bits for 256 bits of payload, so the final character's low two bits are PADDING — flipping an `A` to a `B` there decodes to identical bytes and the token still verifies. Confirmed independently: final characters are drawn from exactly 16 values, ~6.9% of signatures end in `A`, and **275 of 275** such flips produced byte-identical output. The tamper now targets character 0, whose six bits are all significant (**0 no-op tampers in 5000**), and the test additionally asserts the signature bytes actually changed before asserting the rejection — so a future technique that silently fails to mutate reddens loudly instead of passing vacuously. **The flake's real cost was not its own test.** It corrupted a DIFFERENT measurement: this changelog's mutation table claimed the domain-separation mutant reddened two tests, and it reddens one — the second was the flake, caught on the single run the table was built from. Reproduced deliberately: with the old tamper restored alongside that mutant, 20 runs gave nineteen `1 failed` and one `2 failed`. A single-run mutation measurement is exactly where a 6.9% flake lands hardest, and it landed in the artifact offered as evidence of suite strength. **(2) The vitest glob did not match `.tsx`.** `include: ["src/**/*.test.ts"]` silently drops component tests, and an uncollected file is indistinguishable from a passing one — a deliberately failing `.test.tsx` under the old pattern produced `25 passed, rc 0`. That is the same silence the runner was added to end, reproduced inside the fix for it. Widened to `{ts,tsx}`, and — because a canary cannot protect a pattern that stops matching it, since the canary simply stops running — the guard is an assertion about the PATTERN itself (`vitest-config.test.ts`), which survives its own subject vanishing. Verified in both directions: narrowing the glob now fails loudly rather than quietly dropping four tests. A real `.test.tsx` accompanies it, covering the closed-gate cases where `GoogleLoginButton` returns `null` outright — no DOM needed, so `environment: "node"` stays honest and jsdom remains a separate decision. Also taken from the same review: the button now uses the design system's `Button` with `asChild` rather than a hand-rolled near-copy of its outline variant that had dropped the focus-visible ring, and the login card gains `space-y-3` so two sign-in controls do not render flush.

- **#1015: `GET /auth/google` — the sign-in entry point, built to the storage boundary and refusing there.** Chris opened the preview expecting web sign-up and got the Telegram login: the credential path, the provisioning door, the post-signup screens and the routing all shipped, and nobody built sign-in. This is that front door. It starts the OIDC flow, verifies what Google returns against the published JWKS with `iss` and `aud` pinned, checks the nonce **inside the ID token's claims** rather than trusting it from the exchange, and hands back a verified subject keyed on **`sub`, never email** (D32 — an email is mutable and reassignable, so keying identity on it is an account-takeover primitive). **It mints nothing** — no user, no session, and explicitly no tenant, which stays an act at the provisioning door. **One OAuth client, not two.** The recommendation here was originally to create a separate sign-in client; Chris challenged it and was right. The stated reason was that a shared client would show a Drive consent screen at sign-up — but scopes are per *authorization request*, not per client, so that was a request-level property asserted as a client-level one. The existing Drive client already requests `userinfo.email` and already runs a `response_type=code` browser redirect flow, so it was never cleanly "the storage client". Sign-in adds one redirect URI to it and requests `openid email profile` only, with `access_type=online` and `prompt=select_account` — deliberately not Drive's `offline`/`consent`, which exist to guarantee a refresh token and would re-prompt a returning user on every login. **The state and nonce live in a short-lived signed httpOnly cookie rather than `oauth_states`**, and that is a ruling rather than a workaround: `07-security-model.md` §2 gives one table four purposes, three of which bind a row to an existing user, workspace or credential owner and genuinely need server state, while `signin` is the one purpose that is anonymous by construction — so CSRF state plus an ID-token nonce is all it must carry, with nothing to sweep. Its key is **HMAC-derived from `JWT_SECRET` rather than being it**, so a session token can never be presented as state or the reverse; pinned by a test that signs a session-shaped token with the raw secret and asserts the state reader rejects it. The cookie is `SameSite=Lax` **because the callback is a top-level GET redirected from `accounts.google.com`** and `Strict` would withhold it on exactly that navigation, failing every flow with a state mismatch.

- **#1015: the boundary is a single greppable constant, and the button cannot render while it stands.** `SUBJECT_STORAGE_AVAILABLE` is `false` because there is nowhere to write an OIDC subject: `user_identities` is migration 053 and unadopted, the live `users` table has no email, provider or subject column, and `users.telegram_user_id` is still `NOT NULL` so a Google-only row cannot be inserted at all — all of which need a migration, which is behind the #840 renumber lock. `googleSigninAvailable()` gates on it together with configuration and **the request's origin matching the configured redirect base**. That last one solves the preview problem without asking anything of anyone: Google matches redirect URIs exactly and forbids wildcards, so a per-branch registration would be a manual console edit on every PR and would rot the first time someone forgot — instead the button simply does not render where the flow cannot complete, which is virgil's own rule that a button which 404s is worse than no button. Both routes answer **404 rather than 403** when unavailable, because 403 tells an unauthenticated prober that Google sign-in exists here and is switched off. The callback answers **501** at the boundary — nothing failed, the step is not implemented — and deliberately **does not log the verified subject**, an OIDC `sub` being a stable personal identifier and a log being the durable storage we just said we do not have, in the place nobody is looking after it.

- **#1015 / #1016: `landing/` gets a test runner, because "8 checks green" was covering none of it.** There were zero test files under `landing/`, no framework, no `test` script, and no CI workflow referencing it — the check named "Test" runs `pytest tests/ --cov=src`, backend only. So every frontend-touching PR reported green from a run that never executed a line of the code in it. Adds vitest (node environment: everything under test is server-side logic and a DOM would be a dependency for nothing) and 25 tests. Nineteen cover the #1015 gates and the state cookie. **Six pin `hasTenant()`, which is #1016's cross-tenant predicate** — navi verified that fix by measurement, real server and forged tokens across seven routes with a positive control, and nothing pinned it afterwards; verified-once and stays-verified are different properties and only the second survives an edit. Includes the case a refactor reintroduces: chat id `0` is falsy, so a truthiness check reports a real tenanted session as tenant-less. Mutation-verified rather than asserted — truthiness in `hasTenant` reddens one, comparing hostname instead of origin reddens one, and removing the state key's domain separation reddens **one** (virgil's review re-measured this; an earlier revision of this entry said two, and the over-count has an instructive cause — see the review-round entry above). **CI does not run these**, and wiring it is a decision rather than something to assume: named here rather than left for someone to discover the same way this gap was found.

- **Preview bypass works, and the doc now records the failure mode that made it look like it did not.** `documentation/operations/fetching-preview-deployments.md` said there was no working bypass for this project. That was measured honestly and is now false — Protection Bypass for Automation is enabled, and the header form returns real app HTML (`/login` at `200`, ~14 KB, correct `<title>`). **The correction worth keeping is why the original measurement failed**: a `VERCEL_AUTOMATION_BYPASS_SECRET` was already present in the fleet environment from an earlier setup and was stale, so a correctly-formed command with a correct-looking credential returned `302` — byte-for-byte what "no bypass exists" looks like, and indistinguishable from it. **A second way to hold a stale value bites even after the secret is fixed: a running process keeps the environment it started with**, so rotating the fleet `.env` does not reach a live session — observed directly, this session's fingerprint differed from the file's while writing the correction. The doc gives a fingerprint comparison as the way to tell, since neither value may be printed. **Both failure directions are now covered in the verification snippet, because a status code decides neither**: a blocked fetch can return `200` (Vercel's own login page), and a working fetch can return `3xx` (the app redirecting an unauthenticated request to `/login`) — so the check reads the redirect target first and the body second. The fingerprint snippet was corrected after being run: the file side originally kept a trailing newline the process side strips, which made two identical secrets fingerprint differently and would have reported a false "stale". No credential value appears in the doc, this entry, or the commit — variable name only.

- **The user_id-keyed tenant provisioning door: `SettingsService.provision_personal` (tenant-anchor doc §9).** A web-only (Google) user has no `telegram_chat_id`, so `get_or_create`'s identity key does not exist for them — handed `None` it was CREATE-ALWAYS (the `= NULL` lookup can never match, so every call minted a fresh tenant); it now refuses `None` with a `ValueError` naming the right door. The new door mints the user's personal NULL-chat tenant **and its owner membership in one serialized transaction**: `FOR UPDATE` on the user row is the per-user serialization point, because the unique constraint that deduplicates chat tenants is NULLS-DISTINCT and therefore protects NULL-chat rows not at all — the schema-level guard is gone for exactly these rows, so dedup is the door's job. Contract v1: at most one personal tenant per user, enforced by serialization, revisited when multi-workspace arrives with the target schema. Refusal for a nonexistent user is the new `TenantProvisioningError("unknown_user")` — a separate type, deliberately not a new `TenantResolutionError` reason, because the resolution vocabulary is a closed contract shared across the two tiers and a mint precondition is not an identity failing to resolve. `get_all_active` now structurally excludes NULL-chat tenants (`telegram_chat_id IS NOT NULL`): the sweep is definitionally the Telegram posting sweep, and flag hygiene alone cannot keep a chat-less tenant out of it — the `onboarding_completed OR active_instagram_account_id` branch would re-admit one the moment an Instagram account is connected. The concurrency tests are adversarial rather than confirmatory, made deterministic by a race-window seam the suite replaces with a barrier: **remove the `FOR UPDATE` and the concurrent-mint test fails on a measured double mint; remove the sweep exclusion and the scheduler test fails while its positive control (a real chat tenant with identical flags IS swept) stays green** — both mutations run, observed red, and restored. **No migration file and no model change ship here, deliberately**: the schema-parity gate proved the model relax is coupled to the DDL, so the model stays at production truth (`nullable=False`) and the door's integration suite applies the relax to its test schema as the same `ALTER` the future migration will carry — model flip and DDL land together in that migration's PR (sequenced behind the #840 renumber per the tenant-anchor ruling), keeping the parity gate meaningful in both worlds. Deployed ahead of that DDL the door is inert-and-loud — nothing calls it yet, and a poked mint hits the live NOT NULL and raises, minting nothing (the mint commit is deliberately raw so a failure raises instead of being swallowed by the wrapper). The `_new_chat_settings` bootstrap helper joins the telegram-ratchet baseline's six existing entries for this repository — a visible baseline edit, the ratchet's own designed mechanism.

- **Preview deployments show the web sign-up surface, and how to fetch a protected preview is written down.** `webSignupEnabled()` now resolves as: an explicit `WEB_SIGNUP_ENABLED` wins in either direction, and with it unset the surface is ON for preview deployments and OFF everywhere else — **production included**, since `VERCEL_ENV` is set by Vercel itself and reads `production` there. Verified by real boots across all four combinations: preview+unset ON, production+unset off, preview+explicit-false off, neither-set off. The point is that reviewing these screens becomes a URL rather than a request to someone with dashboard access. **The companion problem is not solved and the doc says so rather than implying otherwise**: preview deployments sit behind Vercel deployment protection, so an agent cannot verify a frontend change by looking at it. Measured — a storydump preview returns `302` with and without a bypass credential, in both the query-parameter and header forms, and the same credential and forms fail against another project on the same account, so the request is not malformed: the credential in the fleet environment does not authorise this project. **Following the redirect returns `200`, and that `200` is Vercel's own `Login – Vercel` page, not the app** — so a check that reads only the status code reports success on a blocked fetch, and `documentation/operations/fetching-preview-deployments.md` therefore asserts on content. Closing it needs Protection Bypass for Automation enabled on the storydump project in the Vercel dashboard, which cannot be done from a headless environment: there is no Vercel API token in the fleet environment and the CLI has no stored credentials — `vercel whoami` starts an interactive device-authorisation flow. The doc names the variable that must hold the value and nothing else; the value is a credential and belongs in the fleet environment, never in a commit, PR, issue, doc, or message.

- **Web sign-up surfaces: the first-run and tenant-less screens, behind `WEB_SIGNUP_ENABLED` (off by default).** Adds `/welcome`, `/auth/error` and a no-tenant dashboard empty state, and re-points the dashboard route gate at tenancy rather than Telegram. **The state these serve could not previously occur**: every user until now arrived pre-provisioned through a bot, so nothing in the product had ever had to render an account with no tenant behind it — `middleware.ts` redirected `/dashboard/*` to `/instances` whenever `activeChatId === null`, which is permanently true for a user with no Telegram, so such a user could never reach the dashboard at all. That was not a missing empty state but a gate whose null-check happened to encode *has-Telegram*; it now asks whether a tenant exists, and those two questions coincided only because every tenant was born from a Telegram group. **`/welcome` deliberately does not ask the user to create a workspace**: tenant minting is lazy, so sign-up creates a user and nothing else, and the tenant is minted later at the first tenant-scoped action through a provisioning door — the screen orients and hands off rather than provisioning. Telegram appears on it as one optional connected service among three, never as a step or a prerequisite. `/auth/error` renders three failures that are **not interchangeable** — expired state, already-linked-elsewhere, and an email claim colliding with another user's account; the third says *why* accounts are never merged, because that state is otherwise experienced as a bug rather than as the account-takeover defence it is. **What is deliberately absent, because the dependencies do not exist and a stub that looks right is worse than a gap:** no Google sign-in button (needs `GET /auth/google`); no tenant minting anywhere — `ChatSettingsRepository.get_or_create` is **not called and must not be**, since it is keyed on `telegram_chat_id` and so for a web tenant never finds a row and is create-always, minting a fresh tenant per request; and no `/link/telegram`, `/join/[token]` or workspace-create screen, each blocked on backend state that is not adopted. Call sites needing the door are marked `TENANT_DOOR_REQUIRED` and render as unavailable. **The gate covers every route under `/dashboard`, and where it lives was measured rather than reasoned.** A tenant-less session is admitted to `/dashboard` only; every deeper route redirects there in middleware. A guard in the dashboard layout is NOT sufficient and that is a measurement: Next.js renders layout and page segments in parallel, so a layout that returns without rendering `{children}` hides the output while the page still executes its own fetches — measured on a warm route at one backend call per request with the layout guard already in place, and at zero after the redirect moved into middleware. This matters because those pages fetch with a null chat id, and `get_by_chat_id(None)` compiles to `IS NULL` with no `ORDER BY` on `.first()`; today no NULL-chat row can exist so the lookup returns None and `require_by_chat_id` refuses typed, but relaxing that column turns the same call into an arbitrary tenant-less row — and `require_by_chat_id` only tests whether the RESULT is None, so it would not raise. Found by navi in review. **The session contract is consumed through exactly one predicate**, `hasTenant()` in `landing/src/lib/web-signup.ts`, which fails toward tenant-less for anything it does not recognise, so the credential contract lands in a single edit. That file also records the open disagreement rather than papering it: the credential design names a tenant (`sd1.{user_uuid}.{tenant_uuid}...`) while a freshly signed-up user has none, so no credential currently expresses the exact user these screens serve — unresolved here, and nothing was built that needs it resolved. Flag name is server-side on purpose: an auth-adjacent flag must never reach the browser bundle as `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`. Verified by real dev boots in both flag states with forged sessions of each shape — flag on, tenant-less reaches `/dashboard` 200 with the empty state; flag off reproduces today's `307 → /instances` and the login page keeps both its Telegram copy and its "active Storydump bot" authorization claim. **The first run of that verification was invalid and was re-taken**: the reviewing shell carries `DEV_AUTH_BYPASS=true`, which makes middleware return early and `getSession()` hand back a mock session with `activeChatId: 0`, so every gate read as open; every figure was re-measured with it unset. A session *with* a tenant 500s locally, and that reproduces identically on clean `main` — the absent local backend, not this change.

- **#1015: the web-signup credential path — a BFF→backend credential for a user with no Telegram identity.** The bot token is currently a crypto root in two places (`landing/src/lib/auth.ts`), and the backend credential is a Telegram `chat_id` signed with it, so a user who never touches Telegram has nothing to present. This adds `generate_web_token`/`validate_web_token` as a THIRD branch of the `_validate_auth` cascade — the existing `initData` and URL-token branches are untouched — carrying the platform-neutral subjects that already exist in the live 045-era schema: `users.id` and `chat_settings.id`, both UUID primary keys. Signed with its own `WEB_TOKEN_SECRET`, never `TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN`, because a web session must not be invalidated by a bot-token rotation nor mintable from a bot-token compromise. An unset secret REFUSES rather than signing with an empty key, so an unconfigured deployment accepts nothing instead of accepting everything. **TWO SHAPES, because a user exists before they own anything:** `sd1b.{user_uuid}.{tenant_uuid}.{ts}.{nonce}.{sig}` BOUND and `sd1u.{user_uuid}.{ts}.{nonce}.{sig}` UNBOUND. Tenants are minted lazily at an explicit provisioning door (#842), so a user between sign-in and first workspace has no tenant to name — and that door is itself an authenticated call, so a credential that could not exist without a tenant could never reach the door that mints one (the alternative design, minting a credential only for a user who already has a tenant, is circular for exactly that reason). This is not a new concept here: `helpers.py:109` already distinguishes bound initData (group launch) from unbound (DM launch) and already treats an unbound tenant claim as untrusted; the web credential gets the same two shapes rather than a third idea. **The tenant slot is ABSENT in the unbound shape, never empty, and an unbound result OMITS the key rather than carrying a falsy one** — a caller handed `""` and passing it on as a tenant id is the failure this forecloses structurally instead of guarding against. The shape tag rides in the prefix (not a version bump: these coexist permanently, so `sd2` would wrongly imply supersession) and the parser dispatches on an exact string before touching a field, never on field count — a shape is declared by the minter, not inferred from what survived transit. The prefix is inside the signed payload, so the shapes are not interconvertible in either direction, both pinned by tests. **The result dict deliberately shares no key with the Telegram validators** (`user_uuid`/`chat_settings_id`, not `user_id`/`chat_id`): reusing `user_id` for a UUID string would hand it to `is_active_member`, whose repository compares it against a `BigInteger` column, whereas under distinct names a Telegram-shaped caller reads `None` and fails closed. **The credential authenticates but is refused 403 at `_validate_request`, deliberately and explicitly.** It would fail closed on its own — a web result carries no `user_id`, so `is_active_member(None, ...)` returns False — but that is two unrelated defaults lining up rather than a decision, and on the way there the tenant would be resolved from the request's untrusted `chat_id` while the one the credential names is discarded. Route handlers still take `body.chat_id` and query by it, so admitting a web token today would authorize against one tenant and serve another; wiring them is the next PR. **The refusal keys on `user_uuid`, NOT the tenant key that looks like the natural marker** — `_validate_request` WRITES `chat_settings_id` into `user_info` on its success path, and `validate_init_data`'s result is not always a fresh dict, so a reused one carries the key from an earlier call and the refusal fires on a legitimate Telegram caller; measured, it turned an oauth-route 400 into a 403. #587's nonce is in the format from birth and is NOT yet enforced — there is no nonce store, the token still replays within its TTL exactly as the Telegram credentials do, and a test pins that so nobody reads the field as protection it has not got; the point is that adding the store becomes a validator change rather than a format migration. **The cascade and the refusal are COVERED rather than merely correct-looking** (rajan's review): each of the three branches, the short-circuit that keeps a later validator unconsulted once an earlier one succeeds, the 401 carrying ALL THREE failure reasons, the 401 detail leaking none of them to the caller, the 403 refusal, and that the refusal fires BEFORE any tenant is resolved — the last being why it is an explicit refusal and not a happy accident of `is_active_member(None, ...)` returning False. **Including a regression test for the reused-dict bug the code comment records**: a comment naming a bug you hit, with nothing pinning it, is the shape that regresses, because the next reader believes the case is handled and has nothing that fails when it stops being. It reproduces the MECHANISM rather than the symptom — the mock returns ONE dict object across two `_validate_request` calls, so the first call's mutation is the second call's input. Mutation-verified: reverting the discriminator to `chat_settings_id` reddens three of the eight. **That mutation also surfaced a second reason for the choice that nobody had articulated** — an UNBOUND web credential carries no tenant key AT ALL, so `chat_settings_id` would not catch it either and it would sail past the refusal into the chat-keyed path. `user_uuid` is on both shapes; the tenant key is on only one.

- **#1015: the null-chat-id guard, ahead of the DDL that arms it — and the model nullability flip DEFERRED, because a gate said so.** Web signup needs both columns demoted from identity to an OPTIONAL PROVIDER/BINDING attribute — `users.id` and `chat_settings.id` were always the keys, and every one of the seven content foreign keys already points at them, so nothing re-keys and nothing cascades. **The models are NOT flipped here, and that is a correction found by running the suite rather than a scoping choice.** `TestSchemaParity` replays the migration corpus and diffs it against the SQLAlchemy models; with the DDL unnumbered and therefore not in the corpus, flipping the models alone made them disagree with the schema and reddened that gate. The gate is right — model and corpus must move together — so the flip ships in the same PR as the numbered migration. Both columns now carry a lockstep tripwire pinning the CURRENT state (`test_chat_settings.py`, and a new `test_user_telegram_identity.py` for the column that had no nullability test at all), so moving either side alone reddens: ahead of the DDL via schema parity, behind it because the ORM would keep refusing the insert the database now permits, which is what would have made the DDL cosmetic. **The guard is the half that is not SQL, and it ships here because the DDL is what arms it.** Dropping `NOT NULL` makes rows with a null binding exist, and `ChatSettingsRepository.get_by_chat_id` filters with `column == telegram_chat_id`; the tempting argument that `= NULL` matches nothing is true of raw SQL and false here, because the ORM compiles `== None` to `IS NULL` (measured on this project's SQLAlchemy 2.0.49), which matches EVERY unbound row while `.first()` returns an arbitrary one — another tenant's. `require_by_chat_id`'s refusal cannot catch it: it raises only when the RESULT is None, and a null input now finds a row. The check is therefore on the INPUT and raises `ValueError` rather than `TenantResolutionError`, because a null chat id is a caller bug that should surface as a loud 500 rather than blend into the ordinary 403 an unknown chat gets. The reasoning lives in the docstring so the next reader re-deriving the raw-SQL argument does not talk themselves out of it. Its test is mutation-verified: with the guard deleted, `require_by_chat_id(None)` DID NOT RAISE and returned the foreign tenant. `tests/src/models/test_chat_settings.py`'s `test_telegram_chat_id_not_nullable` is RE-POINTED rather than deleted — the fact is still worth pinning in the other direction, since restoring `NOT NULL` would make every web workspace unrepresentable — and its missing twin for `users` is added, that column having had no nullability test at all.

- **#1015: the DDL is written and DELIBERATELY NOT NUMBERED, because there is no slot for it and that needs a ruling.** `scripts/migrations/proposed/relax_telegram_identity_not_null.sql` holds the two `DROP NOT NULL` statements and is inert — `discover_migrations` ignores anything not matching `NNN_*.sql`, verified by running it (63 migrations discovered, max version 63, the proposed file absent). It **has not been run against any database**; the behaviour it relies on was measured on a throwaway PostgreSQL 16 container built from 006's DDL — three NULL rows coexist, a duplicate real chat id is still rejected, and both indexes (the UNIQUE constraint's and the redundant plain `idx_chat_settings_telegram_id` at 006:43) are byte-identical before and after, so it is a catalog-only change with no table rewrite. **Why unnumbered:** `051_schema_move_public_to_legacy.sql` carries `-- runner:schema-move` and is the corpus's lineage boundary, so a file numbered 064 executes AFTER the cutover against the TARGET `users` — which carries no telegram columns at all (053:83) — and the ALTER would ERROR rather than merely target the wrong schema. Below the boundary, `legacy_lineage_max` puts the legacy lineage's end at 050 and every slot 001-050 is taken, with `_FILENAME_RE` admitting integers only. Renumbering 051+ is not available either, each of 052-063 being under a contiguous-prefix contract. **The open question is therefore how a NEW legacy-lineage migration reaches a production database sitting at 045 that must not be cut over yet** — a corpus-owner decision, not this PR's to make. **A second, independent reason to carry no number: inserting into the legacy band requires a FULL-BAND RENUMBER — which is exactly what PR #840 does (new 050, everything above shifted by one, the schema move 051→052) — and #840 is holding that lock as a DRAFT reading CONFLICTING, last moved 2026-08-22. Two concurrent renumbers of one band is a hard lineage failure rather than a merge conflict. The file is inert either way, so nothing here is blocked on that being resolved.**

- **#942: the target composition root serves `/health`, so its deploys can complete.** `railway.toml` sets `healthcheckPath = "/health"` for both services; the LEGACY root serves it from `src/main.py` and the target root shipped without it. Railway probed a port nothing listened on, timed out, and marked three consecutive deploys FAILED (02:31 / 02:33 / 02:35 UTC) while the worker itself was healthy — so the last SUCCESSFUL deploy, which predates `WORKER_IMPL=target`, kept serving legacy against a database whose tables the M.3 cutover had moved to the `legacy` schema. `healthcheckPath` gates DEPLOY COMPLETION rather than liveness, so the target worker never took over at all. The listener now binds **before the first database connection**, which is the same rule `main.py` already records from the previous occurrence of this trap — Railway times the socket, not the program, and the target root's startup does N+1 Neon connections plus a clock election plus a Telegram probe before it would otherwise be ready. **200 does not mean here what it means for legacy, and the difference is derived rather than copied.** Legacy reports per-loop staleness and 503s on a stale loop because its loops can go quiet while the process lives; the target root is fail-fast instead — `supervise()` turns any supervised task death into `WorkerTaskDied` and `main()` turns that into `SystemExit(1)`, so a dead task takes the process with it and the socket stops answering, which is a stronger signal than a 503 could be. The one failure this endpoint can see that the supervisor cannot is a worker that is ALIVE but STUCK: a task blocked inside its loop has not exited, `supervise` never fires, and the counters simply stop moving. That is the only condition that 503s, it is the one a restart actually repairs, and it uses the same 2x-expected-interval rule legacy applies in `services/core/loops/heartbeat.py` rather than a new number. **Deliberately NOT a threshold on `consecutive_failures`** — those are documented as "what a liveness check reads" (`scheduler.Clock`, `outbox.OutboxPoller`) and are reported in the body, but a database blip would raise them on a worker that recovers and 503-ing would spend `restartPolicyMaxRetries` restarting through an outage no restart fixes; they are diagnostic, not a gate. **No startup-grace constant, and its absence is derived**: legacy needs 120s because it reports tick staleness that reads stale before the first tick, whereas this endpoint's staleness clock starts at the first observation of a STARTED clock, so a booting worker is healthy by construction and there is no window to paper over. Legacy's server is deliberately left untouched and the ~12 lines of HTTP framing are duplicated rather than extracted: legacy is the root currently serving production, and a shared surface is not worth touching it for two callers. The load-bearing test drives the real `run()` with an engine whose first `connect()` never returns and asserts `/health` is already answering — it fails against the pre-fix tree because nothing binds the port, verified by reverting `src/worker.py` alone.

- **#997 completed: 063 carries adoption evidence, and the evidence is a documentation gap fixed on its own merits.** `runner adopt` still could not run — `_load_manifest` refused 063, so first contact died before opening a connection. Measured end to end against a copy-on-write clone of production: with the corpus as-is `adopt` fails naming 063 and writes nothing; with 063 paired it **completes** — 43 adopted, 7 asserted, 13 pending. That also retires a claim in #1006's own body, that fixing 063 would not unblock first contact because 057/059/060 are the next wall: #1004 was corrected by its filer (adopt short-circuits on the first false probe, so none of the nine raising probes is reached) and the branch confirms the correction on real production shape rather than a fixture. **Why the four eliminated candidates stayed eliminated and one did not.** Probing `prosrc` is dead *in principle*, not by preference: a `LANGUAGE plpgsql` body is stored by Postgres as opaque text with no parsed catalog form — unlike a SQL-language function's `prosqlbody` — so there is no semantic surface and the predicate could only ever match a FORM. Bounding the adopt window is dead on the wrongly-true `050`, which is no longer a prediction: adopting against the production clone recorded 050 where a synthetic at-49 fixture left it pending, because both its postconditions are absence-shaped and production satisfies them without ever having run it. `asserted` is refused in code above the floor. What changed is the merit question `COMMENT` was parked on: **`fn_clock_tick` is `SECURITY DEFINER`, owned by `svc_clock` with `EXECUTE` granted to `svc_worker`, and runs the five scheduled legs that produce due work — and it carried no comment at all, while a single nullable timestamp column (062) carries one.** **An earlier revision of this entry, of the comment itself, and of the ruling that authorised it said `fn_clock_tick` was *the sole producer of every scheduled job* and *executable only by `svc_worker`*. Both were false and rajan caught them in review, one `grep` and one catalog read from the schema they were about to enter permanently: **two** services `INSERT INTO jobs` directly — `media_sync` and `work_loop` (a first count of three was itself wrong: the third hit is a DOCSTRING in `scheduler.py` quoting `fn_clock_tick`'s own recurring leg, caught by rajan reading the file rather than trusting the grep; the ORM `Job` model is never constructed and `reschedule_job` is an UPDATE, so raw SQL is the only insert path) — and five roles hold `EXECUTE` on the live migrated branch (owner, grantee, a role with owner membership, and two superuser-class). A third claim — that each leg is bounded by the running remainder so none can starve the ones after it — was wrong in the opposite direction: leg 1 is capped by `p_max` itself and can consume the whole budget, leaving `rem = 0` for legs 2-5. The shipped comment now states only what was checked against the catalog and the source, and says outright that the function is NOT the only writer of `jobs`. The merit argument survives without the false premise, which is why the change still ships.** That is a documentation gap independent of any probe, so the comment is warranted whether or not adoption evidence needs it, and the probe is a beneficiary rather than the reason — which is precisely the inversion (#1006's durable finding: *adoption evidence must not be manufactured by the thing being adopted*) that killed the earlier `COMMENT`-marker attempt. **The probe tests the comment's PRESENCE, never its text**, because matching the wording would re-create `prosrc` form-matching inside the option that replaced it and would let a later reword break adoption silently. It is a pure catalog read over `pg_proc`/`obj_description` fed an oid from its own join, so it returns false rather than RAISING on a database its migration has not reached — adopt treats a raising probe as a hard failure, so a probe naming the object directly (`::regprocedure`) would take down first contact. Editing 063 rather than adding a 064 is forced, not preferred: the manifest check is per-file, so a later file cannot carry an earlier one's evidence — and the checksum rule binds an **applied** file, which 063 is nowhere (production: ledger absent, 0 applied, measured). `07-security-model.md` §9 advertises the comment as it already advertises 062's, and the `advertised_ddl` manifest block moves with it. The `PARKED` exemption in `TestEveryMigrationCarriesAdoptionEvidence` is deleted, as that test instructs when an exemption is spent; it asserts in both directions, so leaving it would have reddened.

- **#942: the deployed worker entrypoint reaches the composition root — behind an explicit gate, so arming is a decision and never an accident.** Before this, both Procfile entrypoints reached zero target modules, re-confirmed at `1ae62bb` by two independent methods (the closure instrument; navi's repo-wide literal-text sweep) — six W-track merges had wired everything into `src.worker` and `src.worker` had no importer. Now `python -m src.main` imports the root EAGERLY and dispatches on `WORKER_IMPL`: unset or `legacy` runs the legacy scheduler+bot byte-identically; `target` runs `src.worker.main`; anything else — including present-but-empty — refuses at boot with exit 2 naming the valid spellings, because an operator who typo'd the arm must get a crash loop they notice, never a legacy worker they believe is the target. The gate CONTRACT (constants + resolver) lives in `src/worker_impl.py`, a stdlib-only leaf, because it has a second consumer with the opposite import budget: the instrument labels with these terms and must not need the ~780-module legacy closure — with its import-time settings floor — to read four strings (and under #989's per-interpreter rewrite, importing `src.main` for them would silently re-couple the orchestrator to exactly the env floor that rewrite decouples). The deployed-axis movement this makes (`worker: 0 → 20` target modules) is IMPORT-reachability, and the instrument now says so itself rather than leaving it to prose: the worker axis prints its gate (variable, default, what this run's env selects, IMPORTABLE-NOT-SERVING) with the terms imported from `src.worker_impl` (an earlier revision of this sentence said `src.main`, written before the leaf extraction this same entry describes) — a respelling there reddens exactly two tests here at every scope measured, pinned by mutation. The eager import is load-bearing three ways: the closure is how reachability is measured and a lazy import is invisible to it (#979, relied on in reverse — lazifying reddens six tests at full-file scope: three by the designed detection path, three structurally, because the mutant removes the attribute the dispatch tests monkeypatch); an import failure in the target tier surfaces at the next legacy boot and every CI run instead of at the window; and M.3 step 4 becomes a config flip with instant rollback (the migration runner's dormant-`preDeployCommand` shape, second instance in this repo). Boot safety is measured, not argued: the added closure needs no env key at import time beyond legacy's own floor — pinned by importing with the `TARGET_*` family stripped. The root row of the instrument also stops lying under the new order: with the root pre-imported by a deployed entrypoint its differential collapses to zero (#986's mechanism at full scale), and printing "0 of 32" would render the exact commit that moved the deployed axis forward as the root regressing to nothing — it now names the pre-import and points at the axis that carries the measurement. The differential-discrimination test's specimen moved from `src.main` (whose "imports no target" premise this change retires — and whose assertion would have stayed green anyway, measured after `src.worker` in probe order, a docstring going false over a passing assert) to a legacy loop module that adds ~300 novel modules and zero target hits. **What this does NOT claim, in the instrument's own words: the #942 blocker is not cleared.** Nothing serves target code until a human sets `WORKER_IMPL=target` on the service, and that flip also presupposes the applied schema and `TARGET_*` config per the M.3 runbook. #985 and #989 both landed mid-PR and the rebase composed rather than fought them: the gate facts now print inside #985's clearing banner (one banner, message keyed on what is known — the by-hand instruction survives as the fallback for a movement the gate cannot account for), the `root_pre_imported` splint was deleted because #989's per-interpreter `measure()` makes the root row honest by construction (the planned expiry, taken), and the differential class's no-target specimen moved off `src.main` — whose premise this gate retires, with the assertion staying green over a false docstring in probe order — to a legacy loop module adding ~300 novel modules and zero target hits. Mutations verified per test at full-file scope, counts corrected after rajan's independent reproduction (an earlier revision stated the selection-scoped trio as the complete red set): lazify the import — six red, the closure pin plus both label tests by the designed path plus the three dispatch tests structurally (their monkeypatch target vanishes); respell the operator-facing variable in the leaf — exactly the two label-literal tests red at every scope, the symbolic contract tests correctly indifferent. 120 green across the gate, instrument, and neighboring worker/scheduler suites.

- **#997 half: 062 carries adoption evidence again; 063 cannot, and that is a ruling rather than an omission.** `runner adopt` — the operations doc's first-contact command — failed before opening a connection, because `_load_manifest` refuses a file above the floor that has neither a manifest entry nor `runner:postcondition` lines to derive one from, and 062/063 carried none. The manifest stops at 049 by design; every file from 050 to 061 carries its own. **062's three probes are DERIVED from its own DDL** — the new `ig_accounts.last_reauth_prompt_at` column, the partial `ix_ig_accounts_reauth_due` index, and `fn_clock_tick`'s return shape gaining `o_reauth_jobs` — so they record what the migration does rather than deciding anything.

  **Catalog reads only, and that is a production-safety property rather than a style choice.** Adopt treats a probe that ERRORS as a hard failure, never as false, so a probe that names an object directly takes down first contact on a database the migration has not reached yet. Measured across the whole corpus against a database lacking the target objects: **nine derived postconditions raise** — `fn_safe_tz` (052), `post_intent_transitions` (055), the `archive` schema and three tables (057), `fn_claim_job` (059), `session_tokens` (060) — while catalog lookups answer false on the same database. That is a lower bound, not a total: Postgres roles are cluster-scoped, so a fixture that provisions the service roles masks the role-dependent probes, and 059's reads true here only for that reason. Filed separately; this entry does not fix it.

  **063 is PARKED, and all four candidate answers were eliminated — which is a finding about the question rather than a reason to pick the least-bad one.** Its whole delta is one clause INSIDE `fn_clock_tick`'s body, signature and return shape byte-identical to 062's, so no catalog surface distinguishes a database carrying it from one that stopped at 062. **A** (probe `pg_proc.prosrc`) matches a FORM — a later reformat breaks adoption silently. **B** (a `COMMENT` marker probed via `obj_description`) was ruled, built, and **withdrawn on evidence**: `advertised_ddl` pins the target lineage as a prefix of the DDL written in the design docs and ALREADY models comments — 062's own is at `07-security-model.md:228` — so it refuses an UNADVERTISED one, and landing B would mean amending the specification to carry a token that exists to be matched by an adoption probe. **C** (`asserted`) is refused in code above the floor. **D** (bound the adopt window) misses the wrongly-true `050`, which sits inside the bound.

  **The durable finding is the principle that killed A and B alike: adoption evidence must not be manufactured by the thing being adopted.** A invents evidence out of the file's own source text; B invents it by adding DDL whose only purpose is to be probed. Both make the migration testify about itself. The open question — whether a comment recording that `fn_clock_tick` is provider-restricted and fails closed for new providers is something the design docs should carry **on its own merits** — belongs to whoever owns those documents. If yes, advertising it is documentation the runner happens to be able to read and the inversion dissolves; if no, B stays dead.

  **Parking it costs nothing on the critical path**, which is why it is parked rather than forced: fixing 063 does not unblock first contact, since the probes in 057/059/060 are the next wall regardless (#1004).

  **The exemption cannot outlive its cause in silence.** The new gate asserts the unpaired set is EXACTLY the parked one, so a new file landing bare goes red naming it — the #997 defect again — and 063 acquiring evidence ALSO goes red, telling the reader to delete the exemption rather than debug the line. Both directions verified by mutation.

  **The true direction was already gated and is not duplicated**: `test_lineage_lane.py` replays the corpus through one unbounded `apply_pending` across the schema move, and `_run_postconditions` raises unless every line reads true where its file just applied. What that lane cannot say is what a probe does on a database its file has NOT reached — the direction production meets first — so `TestTheDerivedAdoptionProbesReadBothWays` asserts exactly that, reading the predicates out of the file rather than restating them.

- **The waitlist confirmation told the public that a Drive folder was "all you'll need", omitting the Instagram Professional conversion the API requires (#998)** — the panel a visitor sees immediately after signing up read *"While you wait — get your Google Drive folder ready with your story content. That's all you'll need to get started."* A Drive folder is one of **three** things, and the omitted one is the only one a user cannot do later, in the moment, or inside the product: `instagram_login_oauth.py:47-48` requests the Business scope family, which Instagram does not grant to a Personal account, and the Mini App's Instagram step is an OAuth authorize (`onboarding/app.js:156`) that cannot convert an account type. A reader who believed this arrived at the wizard unable to finish it.
  - **Named all three rather than adding the missing one.** Fixing only the Instagram omission would have left "all you'll need" false about Telegram — trading a visible falsehood for a quieter one, which is the failure mode this class keeps producing. The panel now reads *"While you wait, get your side ready: a Google Drive folder with your story content, your Instagram switched to a Professional account, and Telegram. That's the whole list."*
  - **The "how" is a link, not more words.** "Professional account" links to `/setup/instagram`, which already teaches the conversion correctly. A reader who needs the detail gets it in one click; the panel stays a waitlist pitch and grows by three words. It also gives `/setup` its first inbound link from anywhere on the site.
  - **Swept for the claim rather than the string, and it is one instance.** Checked every metadata description, the site-wide tagline, all three blog-post descriptions, all eleven landing components, the three surviving setup pages, and the Mini App and bot copy. `hero.tsx`'s *"No password required"* and *"No credit card required"* are auth and billing claims and both hold. `/setup` and `/setup/instagram` already stated the requirement correctly — this was the one surface that contradicted them, and it was the one a signup actually shows.
- **#512: `ChatSettingsRepository.get_by_id` becomes tenant-scoped, like every other by-id read.** It was the last by-id repository read that took no scope parameter, so its callers could not state which tenant they were acting for and the method could not check. It now takes the acting scope and follows the F.1/#841 contract the rest of the data-access layer already follows.

  **The rule here is identity rather than a filter, and that is a property of the table.** A `chat_settings` row IS its own tenant — there is no `chat_settings_id` column to filter on — so `_apply_tenant_filter` cannot express the boundary and the entitlement is "the acting tenant is the row being asked for". Absent context raises rather than widening; `SYSTEM_SCOPE` reads any tenant, for callers dereferencing a `chat_settings_id` foreign key off a row they already hold; any other scope reads only itself. A scope that is not entitled gets `None` — **deliberately the same answer as a row that does not exist**, which is the convention `QueueRepository.delete` already states for the write side. The refusal happens before the session is touched, so a refused call never checks out a connection.

  **Parameter naming follows the other nine repositories rather than local convenience.** `chat_settings_id` means the ACTING scope, as in `MediaRepository.get_by_id(media_id, chat_settings_id=...)`; the row being requested is `settings_id`. Reusing `chat_settings_id` for the target would have left one name meaning the scope in nine repositories and the target in the tenth.

  **Every caller was enumerated rather than assumed.** Three service-tier call sites and two service methods (`get_settings_by_id`, `require_settings_by_id`) thread the scope through to their own four callers. Three of those are self-scoped — they ask for the tenant they are already acting as. One is a genuine foreign-key dereference (resolving the tenant that owns a queue item) and passes `SYSTEM_SCOPE`, marked rather than hidden.

  **The instance-manage callback now derives entitlement instead of inheriting it from its payload.** The picker only ever offers instances the user belongs to, but the id arrives from the client, so membership is re-derived through the same repository the picker builds its list from — the two cannot drift into offering one set and honouring another. An inactive membership is not entitlement: `get_membership` returns rows regardless of `is_active`, unlike the picker's `get_for_user(active_only=True)`, so the flag is checked explicitly rather than assumed out of the query. A user without an active membership gets the same answer as a tenant that does not exist.

  **The #841 SYSTEM_SCOPE census moves 48 → 49**, which is the mechanism working as designed: the pin is held by equality precisely so a new deliberate cross-tenant site lands in review next to the +1 that admits it. The one new site is the foreign-key dereference above.

  **Mutation-verified, four mutants, each naming the tests it reddened:** dropping the entitlement rule reddens `test_another_tenant_reads_as_absent_and_never_queries` alone; removing the fail-closed guard so absent context widens reddens `test_absent_context_raises_rather_than_widening` alone; removing the handler's entitlement derivation reddens three; checking membership existence while ignoring `is_active` reddens exactly the one test that covers it. The unentitled-read case stubs the database to RETURN a row, so a `None` can only come from the rule and not from an empty result, and asserts the session was never touched — a repository that queried and found nothing would otherwise pass it identically.

  Baseline-checked rather than counted: `tests/src/repositories/`, `tests/src/services/` and `tests/src/api/` go from 7 failures to 7, with an empty name-diff against a clean-`main` baseline. Those 7 are pre-existing `test_telegram_service` rate-limiter and saturation failures unrelated to this change.

- **#986: every entrypoint is now measured in its own interpreter, so measurement ORDER stops deciding the answer — and the same run confirms #942's zeros under an instrument that can no longer manufacture them.** The reported root axis had moved 19 → 18 with nothing becoming unreachable: `closure_for` is differential and the entrypoints shared one process, so a deployed entrypoint reaching FURTHER subtracted from the root's own delta. **Progress reported as loss.**

  **The ruling widened the fix, because measuring first showed the defect was not the root's.** Same module, same process, twice: `src.worker` gives **19 target hits and then 0**. Differential under-reports *any* entrypoint whose target modules something earlier imported — the root was merely where it surfaced, being measured last and reaching the most. So this was never *the root wants cumulative while the deployed want differential*: **a shared interpreter cannot answer either question honestly**, and choosing semantics per axis only moves which axis is wrong. Measure-the-root-first was rejected on the same evidence — it makes a deployed entrypoint that genuinely reaches target code report **0**, trading the root's correctness for the axis #942 actually rests on. Reporting both numbers, or disclosing the exclusion, were rejected because **a labelled wrong number is still read** — the `live_locks` shape from the same night.

  **Isolation removes the SHARING, not the subtraction.** The differential stays: dropping it re-opens #972, where `src.main` reported 14 target hits while importing none — the direction nobody re-checks. In a fresh interpreter it is a cheap safety net instead of the load-bearing mechanism, and its pin still passes. **A failed probe RAISES (`MeasurementFailed`) and never returns zero**, which is the property the whole change exists for: a probe that failed and reported *"0 target modules"* would be indistinguishable from the finding #942 rests on. A missing `src.worker` stays a `ModuleNotFoundError` — *this commit predates the composition root* is a finding the caller owns, not a broken probe. Mutation-verified: making a failure return zeros reddens exactly that test.

  **And a third round found that the probes could escape `--root` entirely (astrid, pre-existing).** An editable install puts a finder on the child's path through a file `site.py` reads — a PEP 660 `__editable__*.pth` or an `.egg-link`. When the measured tree **lacks a submodule**, `PathFinder` misses, falls through to that finder, and the **real repo backfills it**: the instrument answers about a different tree than the one it was pointed at, silently. **The blast radius is the historical case exactly** — "a tree missing submodules" is what measuring a commit that predates W1 *means*, so every backward-looking comparison this instrument exists to support was the case it broke on, while the forward-looking runs we happened to take were the ones it got right.

  **It is environment-dependent, and that IS the finding**: red in a PEP 660 venv, green under setuptools-develop — instrument correctness was resting on which pip mode happened to be installed. The child now runs `-S -E`, so `site.py` never executes and neither finder is ever installed, with site-packages re-added **by hand** — that restores third-party dependencies without restoring the path injection, because the injection lived in the files `site.py` reads. Both halves were measured: `-S` alone leaves the child unable to import `src` at all, and the child cannot compute its own site-packages under `-S` (that is the very `site.py` that makes a venv a venv), so the parent resolves it and passes it in. The child also now calls **`assert_root`** — the parent asserted the tree it was pointed at while the child, which does the actual importing, asserted nothing: the same guard missing one layer down, and reusing the parent's predicate rather than forking a second.

  **Verified by mechanism rather than by colour, because the escape does not reproduce in this environment** — and a test that passes here for want of the bug firing is decoration. What is asserted instead: `-S -E` reach the real argv (a flag that does not reach `subprocess.run` is a comment), a child launched that way carries **no editable finder** on `meta_path`, dependencies still import so the instrument can work at all, and the child **calls** the assertion. **Two defects in my own verification surfaced doing this and are worth recording**: the first mutation silently did not apply, because `ruff` had reflowed the argv and a non-matching `str.replace` is a no-op — the harness now aborts unless the mutation changes the file; and the assertion test matched `assert_root` in the probe's **import line**, so deleting the call left it green. Both are the cannot-fail class, inside the verification of a fix for the cannot-fail class. 28 passed.

  **Review found the recursion alive one axis over, and that is the finding worth keeping (astrid, #989).** This change closed *no-answer-is-loud* on the TARGET axis — a failed probe raises rather than returning 0, because 0 is indistinguishable from #942's result. She found the same class on the **root** axis: `measure()` routed to the predates-W1 finding on a **substring** test, so a root that merely fails to import — a typo'd submodule, merge damage — printed *"no composition root exists"*. **The instrument manufacturing #942's literal headline out of a broken root.** Root-exists and root-absent are different states with different remaining work, and the standard has to hold on every axis rather than the one where the bug was first noticed. Fixed by matching the missing module name **exactly** (the LAST `No module named '<X>'`, since an import chain reports the innermost failure last): `src.worker` missing is the finding, `src.worker.anything` missing is a failure. **Her three-file construction is kept AS the regression test rather than re-derived** — she built the break, so her break is what must stay red — with a companion test that a genuinely absent root is *still* the finding, since over-refusing would destroy the before half of the before/after this instrument exists to produce.

  **And a hung import was a silent fourth state** beside reaches / does-not-reach / raises: `subprocess.run` carried no timeout, so an entrypoint blocking at import hung the instrument indefinitely. **Something that can neither answer nor fail is not loud.** Bounded at 120s — generous, so a slow import is never reported as a broken one — with `TimeoutExpired` routed into the existing raise-never-default machinery. Both mutation-verified: reverting to the substring check reddens exactly the two misroute tests, and dropping the timeout reddens the test asserting the knob actually reaches `subprocess.run` rather than merely existing. **The confirmed measurement is unaffected** — the misroute fires only on a nonzero-exit probe, and the discriminating run enumerated 19 target modules by name under the root, which a failed import cannot produce. 23 passed.

  **Both flagged musts landed.** The positive control is re-asserted under isolation rather than assumed — it reads cumulative `sys.modules`, which was right in a shared process and had to be re-checked once every measurement got its own — and `closure_for`'s docstring, which stated the method as sequential-one-process and said that method decides the answer, is corrected in the same commit rather than left to ship a false claim about itself (#942 §1 was retracted over exactly that distinction). **The discriminator run is in the PR body**: same three entrypoints, isolated instrument, `0 / 0 / 19` — unchanged, so the zeros are confirmed rather than artifacts, and the closure sizes moving (`src.worker` +22 → +487) is the visible evidence isolation took effect. 17 passed.

  **Round five: the backstop could fire, but not on the escape it was added for (astrid, #989).** With `-S -E` removed to simulate a future reopening, her probe returned a **silent wrong measurement — 483 modules, 19 target hits, read from the real repo** — and the in-child `assert_root` stayed green. Mechanism: `assert_root` validates the `src` **anchor**, which the measured tree's own `src/__init__.py` satisfies, while the escape happens at **submodule** resolution, where the editable finder backfills after the anchor check has already passed. **Different objects, so the guard could never see the failure** — the cannot-fail shape for the sixth time on this PR, now inside the belt of the fix. The child now runs a **provenance sweep after `closure_for`**: every loaded `src` module must resolve under the root it was told to measure. That makes the reopened-escape scenario loud, covers the purelib-shadow case the anchor check was carrying alone, and turns the belt from a check on the package into a check on the measurement.

  **Two declared deviations from the recipe as reviewed, both because the literal form reproduces the class it closes.** Containment is `pathlib`, not a string prefix: `/x/root-two/src/worker.py` starts with `/x/root`, so a prefix test is satisfied by a **sibling checkout** — which is precisely the condition that produced the original wrong measurement this instrument exists to prevent. And the sweep is a named function taking an injectable module map rather than an inline block, because **the escape does not reproduce in this install mode** (egg-link, not PEP 660) and an inline block is reachable only through the environment-dependent path — while a test calling it against the live `sys.modules` would fire on whatever `src.*` the suite had already imported and pass without exercising the predicate at all. The seam is pinned shut: one test asserts the default map is the live one, so the injected path cannot become the only path that works.

  **Verified by mechanism, since green here is the expected result and is not evidence.** Six mutants, each reddening a named test: neutering the sweep reddens the three firing tests and leaves both negative controls green; **reverting to the reviewed string-prefix form reddens exactly the sibling test**, which is what earns the deviation; stripping the call from `_PROBE` while leaving the import reddens exactly the call pin — the mutation that slipped the equivalent pin last round; inverting or unscoping the containment reddens the negative control **and four real-subprocess tests**, which is independent evidence the child genuinely executes the sweep rather than merely carrying its text. End-to-end in the real child configuration: the sweep raises, the child exits nonzero, and `_missing_module` returns `None`, so the parent routes it to **`MeasurementFailed`** rather than mistaking it for the predates-W1 finding. **Bound stated rather than left to be discovered**: the sweep sees file-backed modules, so a namespace package carries no `__file__` and is invisible to it — pinned by a test so it stays a known gap. 34 passed.
  **Round six: rebased onto `1ae62bb`, and the change had falsified two of its own claims.** The `closure +N` footer still told the reader the figure *"moves with Procfile order"* — the surface a reader of the tool actually sees, the same class as the `closure_for` docstring corrected above, caught in the docstring and missed in the output. Reversing the two Procfile lines now leaves **every figure identical**, which is also an independent control on the isolation by a different mechanism than the closure sizes: under a shared interpreter that swap had to move both deployed numbers. A test docstring naming *"19 then 19"* went stale in the same rebase and is now number-free, since the assertion compares the two reads and never pinned either. **And the re-measurement against current `main` is in the PR body: the deployed zeros held at `0 / 0`, while the root moved `19 of 31` → `20 of 32` — entirely #984's `media_sync`, with the unreached count unchanged at 12.** 34 passed.


- **main went red at #984's merge, and the failing assertion was a pin doing exactly what it said it would.** `test_wiring_the_seam_does_not_pretend_the_executor_exists` was written on #982 while `sync_media_source` was still parked, and declared itself a pin that expires once W6 lands. #984 wrote the executor fourteen hours later, the kind went live, and the assertion reported a correct un-park as a defect. **Confirmed as an expiry rather than assumed**: `git log -S` puts the guard in `711c71a` (#982/#987), and #984's own diff adds both kinds to the expected-live set — it asserts the un-park it is accused of. The opposite story, that #984 un-parked something it should not have, would have needed the opposite fix.

  **The guard is re-pointed, not deleted, because the property is real** — a wired seam must not pretend an executor exists — and a guard removed on the day it finally fires is the worse failure. What is replaced is how it measures. The new form is **differential**: the registry with the seam against the registry without it, so it names no kind as built or unbuilt and cannot expire the next time an executor lands. It pins the SEAM'S CONTRACT (which kinds this door gates, in both directions) rather than the BUILD STATE, which is what moved under the old one. Mutation-verified, since green here is the expected result and is not evidence: making the seam un-park a kind it does not gate, dropping the gate entirely, and leaving an unbuilt reason still blaming the seam each redden a named assertion — and the un-park mutant was re-cut after the first attempt reddened on a `SyntaxError`, which is not the guard biting.

  **Folded in, same collision zone:** `WorkerDeps.drive` was declared twice — the documented one from #982 and a bare duplicate from #984, a merge artifact between two PRs touching the same dataclass. Behaviour was identical (Python keeps the last), which is why it survived review; the hazard is that the surviving declaration is the undocumented one, so any later change to the documented default would be silently overridden. The documented field stays.
- **#940: the ambiguous-publish class was over-broad, so every error Instagram raised on a rejected call stranded its queue row forever.** `is_container_confirmed_failed` tested only container `status_code` (`ERROR`/`EXPIRED`), and #549's claim-before-publish hold is that predicate's `else` branch — a row left in `publishing`, excluded from every stale sweep, never retried, with no history row. Everything raised off a *rejected response* — token revoked/corrupt/expired (`190`), OAuth (`102`/`104`), unsupported media (`9004`), an unparseable non-200 body — fell into it. **Measured on production: 6 of 158 container-creating attempts on the busiest chat stranded, the oldest for 29 days.**

  **The predicate is now `is_publish_definitively_failed`, and it reads both registers Instagram answers in.** A container status of `ERROR`/`EXPIRED` arrives in a 200 body, so it has no HTTP status and keeps its own test. A **4xx** is Instagram receiving the request and refusing it — whether that refusal landed on `media_publish` (rejected) or on an earlier call (never attempted), the story was not created either way. **The line is 4xx, deliberately not "any non-2xx"**: a 5xx is the classic non-idempotent-write ambiguity, where the server may have created the story and then failed to say so. A 5xx, a transport failure with no response at all, and an HTTP 200 carrying no story id all stay ambiguous and keep the hold — **the default remains "we do not know"**, because this predicate is the release gate on an anti-duplicate guarantee and a wrong release posts a story twice.

  **The argument was already in the codebase, applied to one error class.** `telegram_autopost`'s `RateLimitError` special case says it outright — Instagram enforces the quota at the publish call, so a rejection there means the story was never created, and stranding the row would be *"the ambiguous 'held for review' dead-end"*. That reasoning is not specific to quota. The special case is now the general rule, and as a side effect the scheduler's auto-approve path stops stranding rate-limited rows, which it did while autopost did not.

  **`http_status` is stamped at the boundary, not at each raise site.** `_check_response_errors` returns early on 200, so "Instagram answered with this status and refused" is a property of its whole body; it now catches and stamps on the way out rather than asking seven raise sites to remember a keyword — the eighth is the one that forgets. All eight call sites inherit it, including the three in `backfill_downloader`, where it is inert until something consults the predicate.

  **Mutation-verified, four mutants, each naming which tests it reddened**: reverting the predicate to container-status-only reddens 9 (all eight 4xx cases plus the unparseable body); widening 4xx to any-non-2xx reddens exactly `test_a_5xx_stays_ambiguous`; removing the stamp reddens 11; and hardcoding the stamp to `400` reddens 3 — which is the only thing `test_the_stamp_reaches_a_raise_site_added_later` exists to catch, and why it uses 422. Restored control 17 passed; 224 passed across `test_instagram_api`, `test_telegram_autopost` and `test_scheduler`.

  **This is the code half only. It prevents the next strand; it does not resolve the six that exist** — their containers are 8 to 29 days dead against Instagram's 24-hour story window, so neither the container-status door nor the story-history door can still answer. Those need a disposition decision, tracked on #940, and are deliberately not folded into this change.

- **`/setup/meta-developer` and `/setup/google-drive` are deleted (#810, #802)** — #810 filed the Meta Developer page's Step 5 as documenting Facebook-Page OAuth scopes (`pages_show_list`, `pages_read_engagement`) one click after the funnel says no Facebook Page is needed, and proposed either swapping in the Instagram Login scopes or adding a legacy-flow caveat. **Neither is available, and the reason is that the scope list is downstream of the page's premise.** Swapping the scopes yields a page titled "Meta Developer App Setup" walking a reader through building an app **nothing consults** — under *both* branches of `setup.py`'s `if settings.INSTAGRAM_APP_ID:`, since that branch changes which dialog and which scopes, never *whose app* — and it removes the one contradiction that made the page findable as wrong, which is a worse state than the visible one. A caveat is honest but writes careful copy onto a page every option on #282's menu deletes.
  - **Deletion cannot pre-empt the product call, because it is the intersection of every option on that menu.** A (correct the funnel now), B (A plus an explicit waitlist frame), C (delete the dead pages, defer the rewrite) and D (collapse `/setup` into the Mini App wizard) all remove both pages; they differ only on what replaces the frame around them, which is untouched here.
  - **Both are additionally unfollowable, not merely unaccepted.** `/setup/google-drive` published `https://storydump-production.up.railway.app/auth/google-drive/callback` for the reader to register as their authorized redirect URI. That host returns Railway's **edge** 404 — `{"status":"error","code":404,"message":"Application not found"}` — identically on `/`, `/health`, the callback path and a deliberately bogus path, rather than FastAPI's `{"detail":"Not Found"}`, so nothing is attached to that hostname. A reader could not finish the page even in principle.
  - **Both URLs answer with a temporary redirect to `/setup`, not a 404 and not a 308.** Temporary is the point: what replaces this part of the funnel is open, and a permanent redirect is cached by the browser in a way that is awkward to take back. There is no index equity to consolidate — #805 noindexed both — so the redirect exists for the reader with a bookmark or an inbound link, not for search.

- **The setup funnel's own navigation into two dead pages is closed (#802)** — #805 withheld `/setup/meta-developer` and `/setup/google-drive` from search, on the finding that the product accepts no tenant-supplied credentials. Checked against the live site, the search door was shut and **every other door was still open**: `/setup/instagram`'s primary filled CTA read "Next: Meta Developer", `setup-nav.tsx` listed both pages in the persistent nav rendered on every setup page, `/setup/media-organize` linked back to the Drive page, and an indexed blog article deep-linked it *and* reproduced its Cloud-project recipe inline. A reader landing on the indexed `/setup` reached a page the product cannot honour in two clicks, neither of which was a search. The funnel now runs Overview → Instagram → Organize Media → Connect Telegram, and no surviving page links to either dead page.
  - **`/setup` drops two prerequisites and its time estimate.** "Meta Developer account with an app configured" and "Google Cloud project with Drive API enabled" are not prerequisites — `INSTAGRAM_APP_ID`, `FACEBOOK_APP_ID` and `GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID` are all deployment-level and no surface takes a tenant-supplied one. "Estimated time: 30-60 minutes (mostly waiting for Meta approvals)" is **removed rather than re-timed**: the wait it priced no longer exists, and that figure is the baseline #282's own target is denominated against, so replacing it with a number would pre-empt the measurement rather than report it. The same clause is dropped from the page's search description.
  - **`/setup/connect` loses two glosses out of four.** "Connect Instagram — authorize your Meta App (have your App ID and Secret ready)" and "Connect Google Drive — authorize your Google Cloud project (have your Client ID and Secret ready)" told the reader to arrive holding credentials the wizard has no field for. The **step names are kept and are verified real** — `stepOrder` in the Mini App is `welcome, instagram, gdrive, media-folder, indexing, schedule, summary` — so only the false half is cut. What the two steps actually do is copy that belongs with the funnel's rewrite, not here.
  - **The blog article stops teaching the bring-your-own-credentials model.** `google-drive-instagram-integration` told the reader the setup "requires a Google Cloud project with the Drive API enabled and OAuth credentials" and listed the four steps to build one. That is the same falsehood as the withheld page, on a surface that is **indexed** — so the noindex never reached it. It now says Drive is connected from inside Storydump, and keeps the one claim that was true and worth keeping: the `drive.readonly` scope, verified as the sole entry in `GoogleDriveOAuth.REQUIRED_SCOPES`, which can read files and never modify or delete them.
  - **No product decision is made here and none is pre-empted.** Both pages remain live, reachable by direct URL, and unchanged; deleting them, rewriting `/setup/connect`'s wizard walkthrough, and deciding whether the funnel is self-serve or waitlist-gated all stay with #282. Every edit removes a claim that is false under all four options on that issue's menu; none writes a claim that any of them could discard.

- **#977: `_sql_ident_char` was named for SQL's definition of an identifier character and implemented a narrower one — the name is now `_is_word_char_for_this_query`, which names the scope it actually has.** It handles unquoted `[A-Za-z0-9_]`, which is every alias `LOCKS_SQL` uses. **Three shapes still truncate the projection, measured on this parser rather than presumed** — `rows$from$history` (`$` is legal in an unquoted identifier), a quoted `"rows from history"` (the DELIMITER decides the boundary there, not the character class), and a bare `"from"` alias. In each, a `live_locks` planted afterwards goes invisible. They are stated **at the definition**, where a reader meets the function, rather than left to a docstring nobody opens.

  **The name shrank rather than the implementation growing, and that direction is the decision.** Earning the old name means implementing SQL identifier rules — `$`, Unicode in unquoted identifiers, and quoted identifiers where the delimiter rules — i.e. a SQL tokenizer living inside a test helper, to parse a seven-line query it fully controls. That is more surface than the thing it guards, and a gate whose job is to have no holes should not grow a bigger place for holes to live. **A visible name that over-claims is worse than an inline expression**: the next reader trusts it and stops checking, which is the same failure as a gate that reads as protection and does not protect. The naming instinct was right — that is exactly why the over-claim bit.

  **The new test guards the PRECONDITION rather than pinning the limitation**, and the distinction is the point: `test_every_alias_stays_inside_this_parsers_domain` asserts every alias in `LOCKS_SQL` stays inside the handled domain, so a future alias in an unhandled shape fails loudly instead of silently shrinking the gate's field of view. A test asserting the three shapes truncate would have pinned the defect — which is what `test_the_negative_control_names_a_module_that_cannot_exist` did on #972 before it was retired for that reason. Verified firing on all three shapes; the `isalnum()` mutation reddens it and the boundary test together. Why this was a follow-up rather than a block: none of the three is reachable by any current alias, where the underscore hole was reachable by the file's own naming convention. 14 passed.

- **#982: the Drive read leg ships as ONE seam for W6 and W5b — and its page bound is announced rather than absorbed.** No target-side Drive adapter existed; W6 (`sync_media_source` / `first_ingest_chunk` listing) and W5b (`media_fetch` bytes) both need the same door, so it is once-owned rather than built twice into two copies that drift. `drive_adapter.py` ships the **seam plus a scripted stub** on the #862 posture — nothing reaches a real Google endpoint before M.3, and building the real httpx door now would create an untestable-until-M.3 path that reads as coverage. The deferral is stated in the module header, not in a ticket.

  **The listing leg's hazard is that truncation looks exactly like paging**, which is astrid's finding from her own W6 code — *data loss wearing a bound's clothing*. A caller handed a full page cannot tell *"that is all"* from *"that is the first N"*.
