So exhaustion is carried **structurally**: `next_page_token is None` is the only statement that a listing is complete, and **no count means it** — not a full page, not a short one. `page_size` bounds ONE PAGE and never the traversal; capping a page always hands back a token, so a bound can be resumed rather than swallowed. There is deliberately **no `max_total` parameter**: a total cap is the one shape this protocol cannot announce, since it would have to either claim exhaustion falsely or return a token it will not honour — a caller needing one bounds its own loop, where the truncation is its decision and visible in its code. **Mutation-verified**: making the cap absorb the remainder reddens exactly the two tests that guard it.

  **Five findings from the consumer review (astrid, #987), all taken — she reviewed as this seam's first caller, which is the review I could not do for myself.** **The surface is now `async` and takes the source's config**, and both were design defects rather than slips. The real door is egress-floor httpx and `egress.request` is a coroutine, so a sync stub set a contract the real implementation could only keep through an event-loop bridge — **the stub would have defined the shape of a door it never has to open.** And `list_files` took a bare reference, which cannot express D37's `{v, folder_ref, root_name?}`: a source scoped to a **subfolder** would have been listed from the drive root — quietly, and looking like a correct listing whether it came back full or empty. The whole config mapping is passed rather than destructured, so a later key reaches the door without a signature change, and the stub records it so a consumer can assert `root_name` arrived.

  **And the second review round found the half of that fix I had not made: expressible is not required.** Passing the config mapping fixed the ability to **carry** `folder_ref`; it added no refusal to **accept its absence**. So the wrong-answer-that-looks-right did not go away — it moved from *inexpressible* to *omittable*. Same destination, quieter road: a subfolder source with no `folder_ref` still lists from the drive root and still looks correct whether it comes back full or empty. `ck_sources_config_v` cannot help, since it validates only that `v` is a number. **This was my own diagnosis from earlier the same night surviving my own fix** — the recursion this repo saw twice already, where correcting a scope leaves the correction with a scope nobody checked.

  **The refusal is a module-level contract function, not the stub's private behaviour, and the placement is the finding rather than the check.** A guard living only inside `StubDriveAdapter` would let a consumer go green against an obligation the real door never inherited — **exactly the failure the async fix closed one layer over**, a stand-in defining a shape the real implementation is not bound by. `validate_source_config` is therefore what every implementation calls first, named in the header as part of the seam contract, and a test fails if someone later inlines it back into the stub. It refuses only an absent `folder_ref` — `root_name` stays optional and `v` remains the database's business — because the failure being closed is an **absent location**, not a malformed document. Terminal rather than retryable: retrying a shapeless config cannot fix it. Mutation-verified — disabling the guard reddens exactly its three tests, and a companion test guards against over-refusing, since a guard that rejected sources without a subfolder would break every source that does not have one. 22 passed.

  **The blocking one is the night's cleanest instance of act-bound beating recall-bound.** 063 was unregistered with the three stream-keepers — and astrid had written the recipe for exactly this three hours earlier, for exactly the next person to land a migration. It reached nobody: **a note needs its reader to know it applies, at the moment they are extending their own reasoning.** The three guards *did* fire, because they trigger on the ACT of adding a migration, and each named its own remedy in its failure text — append to the lineage list **at the end** (arm (b) is an ordered prefix, so a file may only ever be appended), add the block to the advertised `07` §9 stream with a manifest entry, and bump the normative count 20 → 21. **The documentation did not work and the guards did**, which is worth remembering the next time a recurrence looks like it wants a written warning. Also: the park pin now states its own expiry (#984 makes wired-seam mean live-executor, so its red under that merge is the expiry rather than a regression, and whoever rebases second should delete it rather than debug it), and 063's comment points at the test that exists rather than a file that never did. 18 + 16 passed, both keepers green.

  **The seam parks W6 loudly and does not pretend to be the executor.** With `WorkerDeps.drive` unset the two kinds park naming the seam and the build-path item, which is distinguishable from the generic unbuilt reason — *an executor that does not exist needs building; an absent seam needs wiring*, and a silent park would be indistinguishable from a kind nobody has started. **Wiring the seam does NOT un-park them**, and that is pinned: the executor is still W6's to write, and telling someone their blocker cleared when it has not is worse than parking. Errors are typed on the `meta_adapter` pattern (retryable / terminal / auth / lost-response), with `DriveLostResponse` deliberately **not** a `DriveError` subclass — *"we do not know"* must not be catchable as *"it failed"*. Every stub outcome is independently selectable, because a stub whose error paths cannot be reached only ever proves the happy one. 15 passed; work-loop suite 16 passed; the schema-derived registry-completeness gate still green.

- **#982 prerequisite (disclosed on #978): the credential-refresh leg gets a provider guard, so a Drive credential cannot be minted into an Instagram-shaped refresh.** 062's leg selected due credentials on `state`/`next_refresh_at` with **no provider filter**, and `ig_refresh` then builds IG-shaped params against `graph.instagram.com` unconditionally. **Latent, not broken — and it goes live on someone else's commit.** Safe today by construction only: `store_credential` takes no provider argument, binds `PROVIDER = "ig_login"`, and is the **one** INSERT site into `oauth_credentials` in the tree (verified, not quoted). But `ck_credentials_provider` already admits `'gdrive'`, so the row is insertable the moment a Drive credential writer lands — and an armed gdrive row would have its token posted to Instagram's host, take a definitive 400, and be wrongly `mark_dead`-ed. Both D31 flips, permanent until reconnect. It ships **ahead of** the adapter so whoever lands that writer finds it already done.

  **A new migration rather than an edit to 062, and the rule decides it rather than convenience.** The runner keys on SHA256 of file bytes — *"an applied file that no longer matches its recorded checksum is a hard failure everywhere: fix forward"*. Production carries neither `fn_clock_tick` nor `oauth_credentials` today, so an edit would have been safe **here** — but "safe in the environments I can see" is not the rule, and 062 set the precedent by dropping and recreating the function 059 created. The transformation was applied programmatically and diffed: **one line added**, the rest of the function carried forward byte-identical. 062's transient `GRANT CREATE ON SCHEMA` bracket is carried with it — `ALTER FUNCTION … OWNER TO` needs the incoming owner to hold CREATE, and 062 records having failed exactly that way without it.

  **The gate is falsifiable and carries its own positive control.** `TestTheRefreshLegIsProviderGuarded` arms one `ig_login` and one `gdrive` credential and drives a single tick: the ig_login row **must still mint** (without that, a guard refusing *every* provider would satisfy the real assertion while silently breaking the refresh leg), and the gdrive row must not. **Mutation-verified**: deleting `AND provider = 'ig_login'` turns it red, restoring it green. The test writes the credential directly rather than through `store_credential`, stated at the helper — `store_credential` cannot produce a gdrive row, which is the very construction the guard replaces, so a test routed through it could not reach the state under test. Owner column is provider-correct because the schema enforces it: `ck_credentials_one_owner` takes exactly one of account/source, and a Drive credential is source-owned by construction (D37). 9 passed on the lifecycle gate.

- **#974: `fork_a_attribution.py` shipped a `live_locks` column with no liveness filter — and it was returning the RIGHT number, which is the defect.** Measured read-only on production 2026-08-21: `media_posting_locks` holds **496 rows, 0 of them expired**, because `cleanup_expired_locks` deletes expired rows. So the filtered and unfiltered counts agree exactly and the wrong label was invisible. **The name asserted a property the query never checked, and it held only because a reaper outside this script kept it holding** — it would have gone wrong silently the first time that reaper lagged, over-stating the stake.

  **"Rename it" and "add the filter" are each wrong ALONE, in opposite directions, so the count is decomposed instead.** `total_locks` would be honest about the query while leaving the section header — *"locks that dropping history would strand"* — over-stating, since an expired lock strands nothing. A bare `locked_until > now()` would drop the **20** `permanent_reject` rows, which store `locked_until = NULL` and are in force *forever*: under-stating the stake by exactly the locks that can never expire. The query now reports `all_rows` / `in_force` / `permanent` / `expired`, each named for what it counts, and scopes `backed_by_history` to the in-force-or-permanent population so the two stay comparable. Measured after the change: **496 = 476 in force + 20 permanent + 0 expired**.

  **The vocabulary is `fork_e_lock_cost.py`'s (#967), deliberately not a second one** — that script is the authority on Fork E's population and a rival predicate here is the fork this repo keeps paying for; the section header now names it as the authority. `all_rows` is reported alongside so a reader can SEE the filtered and unfiltered figures agree rather than trust that they do.

  **The check is inverted into a drift gate rather than pinned to a count**, because a count pins today's data: `TestALivenessNameIsBackedByALivenessFilter` parses `LOCKS_SQL` and fails any alias claiming a lifecycle property (`live`, `in_force`, `permanent`, `expired`, `active`) whose expression never reads `locked_until`, and any unfiltered `count(*)` wearing such a name. It carries its own positive control — `test_the_gate_would_have_caught_the_defect` restores the pre-#974 shape and asserts both checks go red — so the gate cannot pass by being vacuous. **The gate then needed two fixes of its own, both found in review and both the same mechanism one scope narrower each time.** Its parser ended the projection at the FIRST `FROM` — the one inside `backed_by_history`'s subquery — so that column and everything after it were outside its view; a planted `count(*) AS live_locks`, the exact defect it exists to catch, passed both checks. Bounding at the **depth-0** `FROM` fixed that, and then the bound itself tested word-ness with Python's `str.isalnum()`, which does not count `_` — so an alias like `rows_from_history` carried a `from` that read as bounded, truncating at `rows_`. **Every alias in this query uses underscores**, so that was the file's own naming convention walking into the hole. The predicate now borrows **SQL's** definition of an identifier character rather than the host language's, named `_sql_ident_char` so the definition is visible instead of implied. **When you add a boundary, ask what the boundary itself is bounded by.** Both are pinned and mutation-verified: reverting either reddens exactly its own test. Read-only measurement script; nothing in `src/`, and no production write of any kind.

- **#942 review follow-up: the reachability script was right by accident — `closure_for` computed target hits from the FULL `sys.modules` rather than from `after - before`, so an earlier entrypoint's target modules were attributed to every later one.** Demonstrated rather than argued: with `src.worker` measured first, `src.main` reported **14 target hits while importing none of them**. `0 / 0 / 14` survived only because `Procfile` happens to list `worker` and `web` before the root is measured — an ordering this script does not control. **The failure direction is the dangerous one**: a deployed entrypoint appearing to reach target code reads as the composition-root blocker RESOLVED, which is the answer everyone wants and therefore the one nobody re-checks. Hits are now differential, and `TestTheHitsAreDifferentialNotCumulative` pins it — mutation-verified, reverting the one line reddens exactly that test.

  **The controls from the manual #942 run are ported rather than reinvented, because a control is what catches this class without a reviewer finding it by hand.** Positive `src.config.settings` present in every closure; negative a fabricated `src.services.target.__NONEXISTENT__` that never matches. They pin **opposite** failure directions — one catches a walker that has stopped matching, the other one that has started matching everything. Both now run on every invocation, print their verdict, and set the exit code, since a control whose result is invisible is decoration. **The positive control reads the CUMULATIVE closure while hits read the differential one, and that asymmetry is deliberate**: `settings` is imported once per process, so against the differential set the control would go false for every entrypoint after the first — a control that fails on correct behaviour teaches its reader to ignore it.

  **The method is documented at the source, because it decides the answer.** Measurement is sequential in one process, so each entrypoint is measured given what came before it; a fresh process per entrypoint answers a different question and returns different numbers — measured, `src.api.app` is **+381 sequentially and +1155 fresh**, a 3× swing on one commit. #942 §1 was retracted over exactly this distinction.

  **And `closure +N` is now labelled NON-COMPARABLE at the point of output rather than dropped.** It counts every module including stdlib and third-party, so it moves with the interpreter and installed set, and it is sequential, so it also moves with `Procfile` order — the issue's 843/378 does not reproduce here in either method and cannot be expected to. The figure is still useful *within* a run and so is kept, but printed beside a statement that only the reach counts and the module names transfer between runs, so it cannot quietly invite comparison with a figure from another environment. Load-bearing numbers unchanged and re-verified: **28 on disk, 0 / 0 deployed, 14 of 28 for `src.worker`**, now identical in either measurement order.

- **#969: the clock stops losing ~1 in 10 ticks under the 3.10 floor — Postgres now parses its own jsonb timestamp rendering.** PG strips trailing fractional zeros rendering `timestamptz` into jsonb; CPython's `fromisoformat` before 3.11 is width-sensitive; the W1 `plan_slot` adapter parsed in Python, and its own R8 backoff swallowed the `ValueError` — a stranding clock, not an outage, invisible locally on 3.11. The adapter's resolve query now casts the payload string server-side (`CAST(CAST(:slot AS text) AS timestamptz)` — the inner cast pins `$1`'s inferred type, because a bare cast makes PG infer timestamptz and asyncpg then refuses the string; measured, not guessed), so no interpreter parsing rule is encoded anywhere. The previously ~90%-passes-by-luck soak assertion is deterministic by construction: a widths gate drives every fraction shape PG can render — including the incident's exact 5-digit form — through the real claim path. Sibling sweep: 10 `fromisoformat` sites in `src/`; exactly one was the jsonb class (this one); two parse Python-written `isoformat()` (0-or-6 digits, floor-safe by construction); the rest parse provider timestamps with stable formats. Production Python measured from the Railway build logs: **3.12** on both services — so the defect was CI-floor-only today, and the three-way skew (floor/CI 3.10, dev 3.11, prod 3.12) is recorded on #969 for an owner call rather than papered over.

### Added

- **#942 W6: the media-sync executors — the last clock-minted lane unparks, and a source can no longer strand out of the due-scan.** Leg 4 nulls `next_sync_at` at mint and only the sync executor re-arms it, so until now every minted sync job parked forever with the alive job as the source's sole carrier. `sync_media_source` + `first_ingest_chunk` land as two executors over one shared body: read the source (own transaction) → the **#982 drive door** (`list_changes(config, checkpoint) → (items, checkpoint')`, `01` :76's port signature, duck-typed on `deps.drive` — production composes `None` and both kinds park loudly with the seam named, the `media_fetch` posture) → upsert into `media_items` riding the schema's own `uq_media_dedup (workspace_id, content_hash)` (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, never a pre-check, so two syncs offering the same bytes cannot race a duplicate in) → persist the checkpoint → **chain a `first_ingest_chunk` while a page token remains** (the `05` 200-file chunk contract is documented as the DOOR's paging contract, deliberately not enforced by truncation — dropping a tail the door already returned is data loss wearing a bound's clothing) → the LAST page stamps `last_sync_success_at`, flips `error → active` (`02` :463: the sync IS the probe, clearing `alerted_at`), and re-arms the `05` baseline (6h jittered). Failure routes three ways, all gate-proven: classified persistent (`DriveSourceGone`/`DriveCredentialDead`) → `state='error'` + one `alerted_at`-deduped notification through `prompts.push_bindings` + the job SUCCEEDS; anything else raises onto the lane's ladder with the source still active and `next_sync_at` NULL (the alive job carries the re-arm); a demand sync on an `error` source that succeeds recovers it. Gate: 6 scenarios on the replayed schema including the full chain (3 pages → 2 chained chunks → last re-arms), with the re-arm revert-checked (nulling it reddens two scenarios — the baseline and the chunk chain, whose last-chunk re-arm assert guards it independently). `first_ingest_chunk` gains its first producer (the sync itself — `02` §5 :1161's "sync job (chains)" was declared and unimplemented, the reauth-prompt shape one lane over).

- **#942 W5d+W5e: the credential lifecycle — a credential can no longer die silently.** The clock was already minting `refresh_credential` jobs into PARKED lanes (work scheduled with nothing able to execute it), with a dated consequence: every credential silently reaching `mark_dead` at ~60d. Two pins pulled beyond the missing executor: **`store_credential` never armed `next_refresh_at`** — a reconnect-stored credential was invisible to the refresh leg forever, so the executor alone would not have saved it (now arms `now()`; the first mint re-arms the cadence) — and **`reauth_prompt` had no producer anywhere** (`02` §5 :1165 declared a clock leg; nothing implemented it), so migration 062 adds fn_clock_tick leg (5): reauth_required accounts, 7-day cadence (`05`'s number, deliberately not a parameter), marker stamped at mint symmetric with legs 2-4, still-open-job guard, return shape gains `o_reauth_jobs` (DROP+CREATE — 059's transient CREATE-grant bracket re-confirmed empirically, plus the column-scoped `UPDATE (last_reauth_prompt_at)` grant in 057's style). The refresh executor is three phases — load+commit, provider call, write+commit — on its own tenant-GUC sessions (`deliver_outbox`'s shape; the egress floor structurally refuses a provider call inside an open transaction, so the discipline is loud, not latent). D31 routes three ways and each is gate-proven on the replayed schema: definitive auth-class rejection (400/401/403) → both flips in one transaction, and the 062 leg mints the prompt next tick (single producer, no inline mint); transient (429/5xx, and a 200 with no token) → the lane's ladder retries and the liveness edge is never taken; undecryptable → the flips `load_credential` already committed stand and the job is DONE, not a retry loop. The prompt executor re-checks account state at execution (a reconnected account gets no stale prompt), routes through `prompts.push_bindings` — extracted so the W3 sweep and W5e share ONE spelling of "where can we say this" — and enqueues `notification` outbox rows (retry-once class). The token appears in no raise, log, or error detail on any path, pinned by a str+repr substring test. Gate: 8 scenarios including the full arc (armed store → tick mints → rejection → both flips → next tick prompts → notification lands on the binding) with an active-account negative beside every mint and the dedup's own positive control; classification units mutation-verified (dropping 400 from the rejected set reddens exactly the 400 case). Found and fixed along the way: the gate's `_tick` commits first, because psycopg2's implicit transaction froze `now()` before the insert and the leg correctly read a fresh credential as not-yet-due — measured at 106ms.

- **#942 W4: the Telegram webhook ingress route — the deployed half of L.8, stopping at the resolution seam.** `webhook_ingress` has supplied `verify_secret_token`, `fingerprint` and `admit` since #865 and nothing had ever mounted them. This mounts them at `POST /webhooks/telegram`.

  **It can land ahead of the #854 ruling because `admit()` takes no tenant** — its signature is `(conn, channel, external_ref, payload, principal)` and it keys on `(channel, principal, external_ref)`. Resolution happens afterwards, when something goes to *act* on the admitted command, so the idempotency boundary sits upstream of the tenant boundary and this half has no dependency on the other. That was verified from the signature rather than inferred from the plan.

  **The design is one ordering rule: a delivery that cannot be executed is refused BEFORE admission, never after.** Admission is irreversible — the `command_dedup` key persists, so a later redelivery of the same update is a `DeliveryReplayed` and is correctly never executed. Admitting a delivery nothing can dispatch therefore does not defer the command, it **destroys** it, wearing the shape of successful deduplication: the failure `AdmissionConflict`'s own docstring calls the invisible one. This is W1's parking discipline translated rather than departed from — W1 parks an executor-less job by rescheduling it **alive** and declines the irreversible step (finalization); the irreversible step here is admission, so the route declines that, and the provider's redelivery is the park cadence. The rejected alternative was admit-then-park behind a 200: it wires admission more visibly and the loss is unreachable in production, but only because nobody has registered the webhook — a property of an operational fact rather than of the code, and the reason to land early is that the code should be right before that fact changes. **Pinned by `test_an_unwired_route_NEVER_REACHES_ADMIT`, which is the load-bearing test**: the 503 alone cannot distinguish "refused" from "admitted then refused".

  **Dormant by two independent facts, not one.** `verify_secret_token` refuses when the expected value is absent, so a deployment that has not set `TARGET_TELEGRAM_WEBHOOK_SECRET_TOKEN` refuses every delivery at the door. Arming requires setting the secret **and** registering the webhook. Neither is done here. `dispatch` receives the open connection so #854 can make admission and effect one transaction — committing admission and then calling a dispatcher on its own connection would leave a committed key with no effect, re-creating the same loss one layer down.

  **The setting is `TARGET_`-prefixed deliberately.** It pairs with `TARGET_TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN` — a different bot from `TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN` — and `Settings` reads whatever the ambient environment holds under a bare name, the hazard its own class docstring records. The route's rate budget is likewise **declared rather than inherited**, with the tension written down: the secret is the real authentication control, but the endpoint is reachable unauthenticated, while slowapi keys on IP and every real delivery arrives from a small set of Telegram addresses, so one bucket serves the whole bot. The number is not validated against measured delivery rates — there are none, the route being dormant — and the comment names the M.2 rehearsal as what would validate it.

- **#942: `target_reachability.py` now prints its own bound next to any non-zero DEPLOYED figure, and the message is keyed on the fact it names.** Mounting the route puts `src.services.target.webhook_ingress` into the `web` closure, so the deployed axis reads non-zero for the first time since #942 was filed — **on `web` only; `worker` still reaches zero, and the blocker is NOT cleared.** That movement is in the flattering direction, which makes it the reading nobody re-checks.

  **A caveat kept in prose degrades the moment someone re-derives the number.** The #942 body carries an amendment saying so, but an amendment does not travel with a figure that someone re-runs this script for and pastes into a new comment, and a new number outranks an old amendment for whoever reads it next. A label the print path emits cannot be separated from the figure — including by the author of this script months later, which is the case that actually matters.

  **The first version of that label was itself the bug it exists to prevent, and review caught it.** It fired on "any entrypoint non-zero" and printed one fixed conclusion — that this is not the blocker clearing, because the blocker clears when the **worker** entrypoint reaches the tier. But `worker` is itself a Procfile entrypoint and therefore a key in the same dict, so on the day it *did* move the banner would have listed it as non-zero underneath a headline denying the blocker was clearing: a caveat outliving its premise at the exact moment it mattered, and worse than the prose it replaced because a script carries more authority than a comment. The clearing entrypoint is now a named constant that the predicate and the message both read, and the clearing branch prints a different block — that the instrument establishes import-reachability only and **cannot** confirm the blocker is cleared, so someone has to find the call site by hand. Both branches are pinned, plus a test that the constant names a real Procfile entrypoint, since a constant naming one that does not exist makes the clearing branch unreachable and leaves the label able only ever to deny.

- **#942 W3: prompt production — the approval card, designed first-principles under two rulings.** Chris ruled Fork 1 (*"post now, even if back end mechanics do flow it through a scheduler or queue"*) and ruled parity out as a design constraint (*"i dont care all that much about parity … best from first principles in the next state"*), so the card is a design rather than a port, with one floor: the four callbacks production actually uses (autopost · skip · posted · reject, per the 30-day observed-use measurement) all survive. The ruled card: **Post now** (iff the workspace can publish by API — `workspaces.api_publishing_enabled`'s first production reader) · **Posted myself** (the matrix's manual-mode path) · **Skip** · **Reject**, plus the zero-backend Instagram deeplink. Callback tokens are versioned (`v1:<action>:<intent>`, inside Telegram's 64-byte bound); the inbound half that parses them is W4, gated on #854.

  `prompts.prompt_intent` implements the `02` §4 edge exactly — `scheduled → prompt_pending` commits with its cards in ONE transaction, one card per active push binding, and **no surface means no transition** (an intent in `prompt_pending` with no card anywhere would be unreachable by any tap). `prompts.sweep_due_prompts` is the three-leg backstop: due `scheduled` intents gain cards; `prompt_pending` intents with a SENT card advance to `awaiting_approval`; intents whose cards ALL failed move to `failed` — every transition guard-checked, races benign. The advance/fail edges implement the spec's **first disjunct only** ("delivered on ≥ 1 binding"): the "or workspace has web access" half has no referent in schema or code today and arrives with X.2 — a stated narrowing, not an exact-match claim. The `plan_slot` adapter prompts on the same beat it mints (fast path); the supervised `PromptSweeper` runs the backstop on its own cadence with `prompts[sweeps= prompted= advanced=]` riding the status line, the same absence-is-failure control the sender sweeper carries. Deliberate deferrals, stated: auto-mode is a policy at `awaiting_approval` (`auto_reapprove_returning`), not a skip-prompt path — `scheduled → approved` is not a seeded edge, so nothing here fakes it; photo previews wait on the media-bytes seam (W5b); reject stays a single tap until W4 owns the confirm flow.

- **#943 M1-02: Fork D is not a fork — the shipped schema already answers it, and minting rule W is retired.** `channel_bindings` is UNIQUE on `(channel, external_ref)` and carries **no** uniqueness on `workspace_id`, so **N channels per tenant is the design** and `telegram_group`/`telegram_dm` are two **channel kinds, not two tenants**. The transform was written against minting rule W — one workspace per `chat_settings` row — which the target **refuses**: measured on a scratch build of 052+053, a workspace minted for a chat with no active memberships fails at commit on `trg_workspaces_owner_at_insert`, while the same build **commits** one workspace carrying two bindings (positive control first, so a pass cannot come from everything failing). Production is exactly that shape — group `-1003688539654` has 3 active memberships, DM `7668871620` has **0** — so W is inexpressible on the actual corpus rather than merely one of two readings. **The 1,095-row quarantine dissolves**: it was the cost of a mapping the target does not admit, not a cost to weigh. **The replacement is derived rather than asserted** — a DM binds to the workspace of the user it is with, via `telegram_chat_id` → `users.telegram_user_id` → active membership → that group's workspace; measured, **0** DM users belong to more than one group (no tiebreak needed), **1 of 1** DM chats resolves to exactly one group, **0** resolve to none. The DM user's role is `member`, **not** `owner`, so a rule phrased as ownership would fail here. Four conditions that would make it wrong are named with their current counts, and the G-IDS consequence the retired rule was carrying silently is stated: `workspaces.id := chat_settings.id` is well-defined only because the **group** chat mints the workspace. **The postcondition block needed no edit** — it was restated as a partition earlier the same day and already allows N — which is exactly why the sweep mattered: the sentence was already right while §3.4, §4.2, the §2 file table and the fork list still encoded 1:1. Verified that none of 054–061 alters `ck_bindings_channel`, `uq_binding_external` or `ct_workspaces_owner_at_insert` (058 only enables RLS), so 053 remains the authority. **The sweep was not exhaustive the first time, and the miss was live code** (rajan): `scripts/m1_preflight.py` still declared `fork="D"`, so the battery printed `D  live on this data` on every run — anyone running preflight before this migration would have read D as unruled, contradicting the closure. Fixed at the source: the `fork=` declarations, the check **name** `fork_c_and_d_collapse_counts` → `fork_c_collapse_counts` with its two cross-references and a test docstring, and the `A (volume conditional on D)` label → `A`. Verified by importing the module and asserting `D` is absent from the declared fork set, rather than by re-reading the diff. Three further sites the review did not name were found by sweeping the class rather than patching the list: §3.3's "the total `chats` count is Fork D's number", the preserved §3.4 register entry still speaking in the **present tense** (now fenced as SUPERSEDED, since a retained block making false current claims is the same defect), and §4.8's ambiguous `C/D-conditional` label. **And the central claim is sharpened rather than restated**: rajan's fourth probe minted a workspace for a DM-shaped chat with a *synthetic* owner and it committed, so the DDL trigger alone does not refuse rule W — the refusal is the trigger **jointly with** the pre-existing reconciliation postcondition `count(workspace_members) = count(active legacy memberships)`, and neither half is sufficient alone. Also recorded: the >1-group ambiguity case is **structurally untestable today** (exactly one group chat exists), which is a lower floor than "0 observed"; and D13 was ratified 2026-08-10, eleven days before this correction, with `053` already citing it — the register posed as open a question already settled in prose, which is a process defect named here and not fixed here. **Third pass (astrid), and the count went 5 → 9 → 11 with every sweeper believing they were done — so eleven is an empirical floor, not a figure of speech.** Two more sites: §6's rung-4 note still read "regardless of which way Fork D is ruled", and §3.4's C-menu addendum still called a DM-rooted tenant "Fork D's territory" — the latter **contradicting the §3.3 correction made in this same PR**, so the fix and the stale claim disagreed inside one file. **And the SUPERSEDED fence itself over-scoped, which is the finding worth keeping**: it opened a marker and never closed one, so it labelled the live restatement and live C-menu content as retired. The remedy landed in the same failure class as the defect, inverted — before, text asserting something retired read as live; after, text asserting something live read as retired. Both are false statements about status. Fixed structurally rather than by wording: the retired paragraph now sits **inside** the blockquote, so the boundary is the quote itself and cannot drift from what it claims to cover. Documentation plus one measurement script — no SQL, no `src/`, production untouched.

- **#943 Fork E: the cost of dropping the legacy locks is at most 27 days of slightly-too-eager suggestions, and it decays to zero on 2026-09-17.** `scripts/fork_e_lock_cost.py` prices the fork read-only, importing `observed_use.py`'s identity + `default_transaction_read_only` contract rather than restating it. It prices; it does not rule.

  **`chat_settings.repost_ttl_days` is NULL for both tenants — and NULL is BOUNDED.** The column is nullable with no DB default, so "unset" was a live possibility rather than a missing datapoint, and it turned out to be the actual state. `MediaLockService._resolve_ttl` falls through to `DEFAULT_REPOST_TTL_DAYS = 30` on NULL (also on no chat id, and on chat-not-found); the constant is hardcoded and not env-overridable. The script **asserts that constant against `src/config/defaults.py`** rather than quoting it, because a printed constant is one that can go stale against the source it claims — and here it carries the entire bounded-vs-unbounded answer. The unbounded case is real but is a different lock: `permanent_reject` stores `locked_until = NULL`, which the model documents as permanent; there are 20, and Fork E does not touch them.

  **The setting is a proxy; the cost is already recorded.** `media_posting_locks.locked_until` is a STORED column written as `locked_at + ttl` at creation, so the setting governs locks made from now on while `locked_until` governs the ones that exist — the population the fork is about. Changing the setting today would not move a single existing expiry. Both are reported and the stored column is the one that describes the cost. They agree here by two independent routes: all 226 `recent_post` locks were built with exactly 30 days.

  **Measured: 226 `recent_post` locks in force, 0 expired, 0 permanent. 17% would have expired within 7 days anyway, 100% within 30, and the last on 2026-09-17 — 27.0 days out.** That is a ceiling rather than an estimate, since no lock in the table survives past it, and every early re-offer sits behind an approval prompt.

  **Two corrections recorded rather than quietly fixed.** The Fork A measurement reported "502 live locks" from a query with no liveness filter — it counted every row and the label was wrong; the real in-force figure for Fork E's population is 226. And 226 does not contradict the 233 on the issue: **no expired rows exist in any bucket**, because `cleanup_expired_locks` deletes them, so the population shrinks on its own between measurements and a later re-derivation should expect a smaller number still.

- **#943 Fork A: the epoch gate has almost nothing to bite on, and the measurement that says so is read-only.** The quarantine question is *which* Instagram account a `posting_history` row went out through; the proposed mechanical answer was a gate keyed on each account's `created_at`. `scripts/fork_a_attribution.py` measures whether that gate separates anything. It reports a shape and rules nothing, and it is read-only the way `observed_use.py` is read-only — by **importing** that module's identity and `default_transaction_read_only` contract rather than restating it, so there is one audited copy of "did you only read?".

  **Measured on production: the gate separates 1 of 1,586 Instagram-bearing rows.** Both accounts' rows were created on 2026-06-02, nine hours apart — but Instagram posting starts 2026-01-17, nearly five months earlier. So **950 rows predate BOTH account rows** (no signal in either direction), 1 falls between them (attributable to the first account by the sound direction), and 635 postdate both (ambiguous on time alone).

  **The verdict is computed over three buckets, not two, because the label is quotable.** The dispatch framed this as one cut, which assumes both accounts predate the history; on this data they do not, and a two-bucket verdict reads `PARTIAL` while the gate does essentially nothing. `no_signal` / `separable` / `ambiguous` are counted separately, `separable == 0` is its own verdict (`NOT-AT-ALL`, never `PARTIAL`), and every `PARTIAL` is required by test to carry its own denominator so a bare label cannot reach a ruling stripped of the fraction that makes it meaningful.

  **Two negatives are measured rather than inherited from the issue.** `posting_history` carries no attribution column — the full column inventory is printed, so that is a measurement. And a column-NAME inventory cannot see attribution hiding in column CONTENT: Instagram media ids are commonly `<media>_<owner>`, so the ids are probed against the real account ids. Result is a clean null on all 1,586 rows — zero match either account, zero contain an underscore, zero carry a permalink. That is what makes "no other signal" a finding instead of an assumption.

  **What it cannot settle, stated because the sound direction runs one way only.** A row posted before an account existed cannot have gone out through it. The converse does not hold — a row postdating both accounts could have gone out through either, since both are `is_active`. So the 950 are *unattributed*, not *attributed to the first account*, and the script says so where the numbers are printed rather than in a footnote.

- **#942: the cutover blocker's measurement becomes a script, because the hand-run version silently measured a different repository.** The finding was established in prose and re-run by hand two days later; an editable install had **another storydump checkout on `sys.path`**, so a run launched from outside the repo imported that tree instead and returned **byte-identical numbers for two different commits**. That identity was the only tell, and a stale-tree read fails in the reassuring direction — it reports *no change*. `scripts/target_reachability.py` now **asserts `src` resolves under the root it was told to measure and refuses otherwise**.

  **Re-measured at `13bc6cf`: the finding stands, with zero movement on the deployed axis.** Both `Procfile` entrypoints still reach **0 of 28** target modules, exactly as at `ec6b1d7`. What changed is off that axis: `src.worker` (W1/W2, #958) reaches **14 of 28**, and **nothing in the `Procfile` or `railway.toml` runs it** — the tier gained an ignition that nothing turns. Reported separately from the deployed count because "the root exists but is not deployed" and "the root does not exist" are different states with different remaining work, and the deployed axis renders both as zero. The instrument survives a commit predating W1 by reporting the missing root as the finding rather than crashing on it.

  **FC-7.3 re-derived per item: still 12 of 14 ABSENT.** The tier gained 8 public callables (96 → 104) and zero parity items. Item detection is substring-over-identifier, and the script **prints evidence with no headline count**, deliberately: an automated first pass scored 5 present, of which three were name-shape artifacts (`IntentNotApproved` matching "approve", `resume_unresolved` matching "pause/resume", `resolve_cancel` matching "cancel") — the exact trap the issue's own method note warns about, sprung on an identifier instead of a docstring. A headline is the part that gets quoted without its evidence, so there isn't one.

  **Entrypoints are parsed from the `Procfile`, not hardcoded** — a third deployed process is the event that would make a fixed pair silently incomplete. Absence is counted against the modules the tree actually defines, so 0-of-0 and 0-of-28 stay distinguishable.

- **#841 item 4 (partial): 22 tenant params in `src/repositories/` become keyword-only, so a stale positional is an immediate `TypeError` rather than a silently mis-bound argument.** Measured on alex's predicate — AST over `def`s carrying a `chat_settings_id` parameter, split on `args` vs `kwonlyargs` — the layer moves from **72 positional / 31 keyword-only** to **50 / 53**. The `origin/main` figures reproduce the #841 burn-down comment exactly, so this is the same denominator rather than a rival one. The hazard is not hypothetical: #961 inserted a tenant param *third*, ahead of `allowed_from`, and alex established the fail-closed guard does **not** refuse a mis-bound positional (`require_tenant_context({"processing"})` passes, because a non-empty set is truthy). Keyword-only is what makes that unwritable. **Pin-neutral, verified rather than inherited**: the `SYSTEM_SCOPE` census reads **48 on this branch and 48 on `main`**, so the F.1 equality pin does not move and this can run alongside the other burn-down items. **Four methods deliberately deferred** — `history.get_by_id`, `lock.get_by_id`, `category_mix.get_current_mix` and `get_current_mix_as_dict` — together with the 46 params in the four files #961 owned; 50 positional remain and item 4 is **not** complete. `src/services/` (48), `src/api/` (2) and `cli/` (0) are out of scope for item 4 by ari's ruling, not by omission.

  **Changing a signature is half the change; the callers are the other half, and finding them is where this went wrong twice.** A first sweep matched call sites by method *name* and converted one on the wrong class — `get_by_id` is defined by seven unrelated repository classes and only one converted. That was replaced with a sounder mechanism: convert the signatures, run the suite, and let Python's own call binding name the callers via `TypeError`. It is type-accurate by construction — and it is **blind through a `Mock()`**. A bare `unittest.mock.Mock` accepts any call signature without raising, so a call site whose only test double is a bare mock cannot produce the `TypeError` the mechanism keys on. Review found **12 such call sites**, two of them live production paths that would have raised at runtime and never at test time: the dashboard audit-log endpoint (`get_for_instance`) and the Telegram `instance_manage` callback (`get_by_id`). The other ten are the Google Drive OAuth refresh and disconnect paths (`get_token_for_chat` ×5, `delete_tokens_for_chat`), the setup-state probe, `create_membership`'s `IntegrityError` recovery branch, `deactivate`, and `deactivate_for_chat` on the membership handler. All twelve now pass `chat_settings_id=` by keyword.

  **The durable point is that both mechanisms were sound and both were incomplete, and neither incompleteness was visible from inside the mechanism.** Name-matching fails on identity (wrong class); `TypeError` discovery fails on reachability (mocked callee). What resolved it is not a third mechanism but a **two-stage** one that separates recall from precision and reports its own residue: stage 1 name-matches every call passing more positionals than the *narrowest* same-named signature allows — deliberately receiver-blind, so it is a superset (`min`, not `max`; a name shared with a wider signature would otherwise hide a real miss); stage 2 resolves each receiver to a class and returns **CONFIRMED / DISCARDED / UNRESOLVED**. The failure was never name-matching as such — it was name-matching used to *drive edits*. Over-broad is safe as a denominator and fatal as an edit list. On this diff stage 1 returned 83 candidates, stage 2 confirmed 11 automatically and left 25 unresolved, all 25 triaged by hand to one more confirmation and 24 discards. **Its own blind spot is named rather than assumed absent**: any call reaching a converted method without naming it literally — `getattr`, a bound-method reference, a `**kwargs` splat — is invisible to it. Each was probed with a positive control proving the probe was live (48 `getattr` calls, 81 repository attribute references and 30 splats exist in the tree), and each returned zero *for converted methods*. Two mechanisms with different blind spots converging on the same 12 is the evidence here; neither alone is.

- **#957: `schema_parity.py` now states what it deliberately skips, not only what it compares.** A scoped green tick whose scope is invisible gets read under time pressure as *"the schemas match"*; it means *"the schemas match on the dimensions below, by design"* — and during M.3 someone will read it the first way. Measured against `main`, the excluded dimensions hold **152 divergences**, every one expected: **65 column defaults** (SQLAlchemy `default=` is applied in Python and emits no DDL `DEFAULT`, so the migration side carries `uuid_generate_v4()`/`CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` where the models side carries nothing — both correct), **87 non-unique indexes**, and **0 triggers** (identical today, listed so a future divergence is a known blind spot rather than a discovery). **The index decomposition took two corrections and the docstring names both false summaries as false.** "All 87 are naming" was my own filing; "the 33 non-naming are all partial-vs-unconditional" was my correction of it, and rajan's review corrected that in turn by building both schemas and extracting the indexes himself. The measured answer is **three** categories: **54** naming (27 pairs), **18** partial-vs-unconditional (9 pairs — the migrations hand-write predicates where `index=True` takes none), and **15 orphans** present on one side with no counterpart at all. All 15 orphans are migration-only, from three causes: 5 multi-column, 5 single-column-with-predicate (neither expressible via per-column `index=True`), and 5 plain indexes the models simply do not declare. Orphans are called out separately because a differently-shaped pair is a deliberate divergence while an orphan is an absence, and a reader chasing "why is this excluded" needs to know which they are looking at. The real reason for the exclusion is simpler and covers all 87: **a non-unique index carries no correctness guarantee** — it is a query-planning object, and unique indexes are compared precisely because uniqueness constrains data. Documentation only: the parsed module is **AST-identical** to `main` once the docstring is removed, and `test_migration_gate.py` is unchanged at 11 passed. The docstring also refuses the obvious follow-on — adding any excluded dimension would report its full population as a permanent failure, and the natural next step is to weaken or delete the gate.

- **#956: two `migration=` labels in the uniqueness gate pointed at the wrong file.** `users_telegram_user_id_key` claimed `001` (which adds a category column to `media_items`); the table and its implicit unique constraint come from `scripts/setup_database.sql` — also a category error, since the source is not a migration at all. `idx_media_items_file_path_legacy_unique` claimed `044`; it is created in `014_multi_tenant_chat_settings_fk.sql`. The field is decorative — re-verified, `.migration` is never read anywhere in `src/`, `scripts/` or `tests/` — so its only consumer is a human debugging a failing rule, and a wrong pointer ends that search where an absent one would have prompted a look. Label-only: the parsed module is **AST-identical** to `main` once `migration=` is normalised away, and the suite is unchanged at 30 passed. The third same-shaped label (`chat_settings_telegram_chat_id_key` → `006`) was checked and is **correct** — `006_chat_settings.sql` does create that table.
- **#942 W2: the Telegram outbox transport — and a dead credential is loud, as a requirement rather than a hope.** `src/channels/telegram_transport.py` is `deliver()`'s injected seam done for real: httpx through the L.0 egress floor (`api.telegram.org` was already in its allowlist), the `{v, text, reply_markup?}` payload contract W3's prompt production will emit, and the bot token redacted from every raise path — the request URL embeds it, so every exception out of the HTTP layer is re-raised scrubbed. The module is adapter-named on purpose: the FC-2 ratchet reads `telegram_*` stems as adapter-side by declaration, so the committed baseline gains exactly one line on `telegram_modules` and the core segment stays untouched.

  The shitpost-alpha lesson (2026-08-21: a fleet's outbound dead for an unknown period because a rejected token had no loud surface) ships as behavior: `getMe` probes the credential at worker startup and a rejected token parks the channel with a DEAD-TOKEN reason that re-warns on every park; a mid-run 401/403 is its own named error class (`TelegramAuthDead`), counted as `auth_failures` in the status line and logged loudly once; a definitive refusal stays distinct from a lost response, which keeps the outbox's ambiguity machinery honest. A transport that sends nothing is now distinguishable from one with nothing to send.

  The sender cycle: `ensure_sender_jobs` mints one `deliver_outbox` job per Telegram binding holding pending rows (idempotent on the live `tg:<binding>` serialization key — the sweep never mints for a parked channel, the same rule that keeps the clock's recurring set inside the live registry), the interactive lane claims it, the bounded hold runs poller ticks under the lease until the queue drains or the hold elapses, the job finalizes, and the next sweep re-mints only while pending rows exist. Gate-proven end to end on the replayed schema: sweep-once/dedup, two cards drained to the binding's own `external_ref`, rows `sent` with refs, a drained outbox mints nothing, zero stranded leases, and the dead-credential path marking the row `ambiguous` with the worker surviving. Also fixed on the way: the `wait_for(stop.wait(), …)` idiom leaked an unawaited `Event.wait` under shutdown-cancel races (caught by `filterwarnings = error`); all three wait sites now use `asyncio.timeout`, and the raceful pair ran 3× green under the fix.

- **#942 W1: the target worker exists — `python -m src.worker`, the composition root the tier was measured missing, proven idling on a branch of production.** The `02` §5 registry becomes a dispatch table with the denominator **derived from `ck_jobs_kind`** rather than restated: every kind is an adapter or a `Parked(reason)`, and a kind a future migration adds fails the suite until the registry says run-or-park. **Parking is the load-bearing negative**: `fn_clock_tick` already mints kinds nothing can run (measured on #790), and a claimed executor-less kind is rescheduled *alive* — logged with its reason, counted, attempt restored — never stranded on a lease, never finalized dead, never dropped. A seam the deployment lacks (no transport → W2, no `media_fetch` → W5b, no provider poll → W5a, no `CLOUDINARY_*`) parks its dependent kind the same way with the missing seam named, so the W1 live set is exactly `{plan_slot, reap_expired}` and the clock's recurring singletons are asserted to stay inside the live set — a clock that mints what the registry parks would manufacture parked work on its own cadence.

  `compose()` assembles the graph without touching the network (its properties are unit-tested facts), `run()` binds the election and per-lane claim connections, wires `LeaseHeartbeat` (closing #903, pool shape and escalation included), applies the GUC invariant per job through `unit_of_work.apply_gucs`, reports a 60-second status line plus a final summary from the observables, and stops cleanly on SIGTERM. `TARGET_DATABASE_URL` is the branch-soak/deploy door (platform URL in, asyncpg-safe URL out — Neon's libpq-only `sslmode`/`channel_binding` rewritten). Verified past the gates on a **Neon branch of production**: the real runner ran `adopt` (43 probe-adopted, 7 asserted — the #787-inert first-contact world exercised on prod-shaped data for the first time) then `apply` through the 051 move, and the worker idled 12 minutes — clock elected, one intent minted unaided, the parked kind cycling on its cadence across two separate processes, zero stranded leases, TERM to clean stop in 287ms. The integration gate (`tests/scripts/test_w1_worker_gate.py`) pins all of it, including the one defect it caught on the way in: jsonb hands the clock's `slot_at` back as an ISO string and the adapter owns the parse.

- **#841 F.1 burn-down items 1+3: the queue's write side stops accepting a UUID as authorization, and the SYSTEM_SCOPE census falls 64 → 48.** #846 landed the mechanism and the four gate obligations; what it left was a burn-down of deliberate cross-tenant sites. Two of its items are here. The security content is item 3: `QueueRepository`'s six by-identity mutators — `delete`, `update_status`, `transition`, `mark_publishing`, `update_scheduled_time`, `set_telegram_message` — took **no tenant context at all**, so knowing a row's UUID was sufficient to delete, reschedule or re-status another tenant's queue item. The read side had been scoped since #597; the write side had not, which is the fail-open shape F.1 exists to extinguish, sitting behind the reads that were already fixed.

  **The ownership rule was consolidated rather than copied.** `MediaRepository._write_allowed` and `._get_for_write` were already the right pattern and already model-agnostic in everything but their home; a second copy in `QueueRepository` would have been two rules that drift. They move to `tenant_scope.write_allowed` and `BaseRepository._get_for_write`, with no alias left behind, and `MediaRepository`'s eight call sites rewired. The generic resolver keeps the `#908` read-close the original inherited from `get_by_id` — dropping it silently changes session lifetime for every mutator routed through it, which is what the media suite caught.

  **`transition` needed the rule in a second language, and the twins are pinned against each other.** Its guarantee is that the status guard and the write are ONE statement — a pre-read to check the owner would reopen exactly the TOCTOU it exists to close — so it carries `BaseRepository._owned_or_null` in its own WHERE clause instead. Two expressions of one rule is a fork risk, so `test_the_sql_clause_and_the_python_predicate_agree` runs both over the same five ownership cases with the SQL side **evaluated by Postgres against real rows**: a clause compared against a hand-written Python re-statement of itself would agree by construction and prove nothing. The two methods make opposite trades for opposite reasons, and each says so — fetch-then-check can LOG a refused cross-tenant write and a filtered read cannot (a filter returns None for a foreign row and a missing one alike), while the conditional UPDATE gives up the log line to keep its atomicity.

  **Item 1 narrowed 12 of 17 telegram sites, and the other five are reported rather than forced.** The narrowable ones are all one shape: a *second-hop read* — "fetch the media item belonging to this queue row" — where the row was already ownership-checked at the handler door and its own stamp is the scope its dependents should be read under. Passing SYSTEM_SCOPE there granted the whole estate in order to fetch one row's own media. `tenant_scope.scope_of_row` names that: a stamped row scopes to its stamp; an unstamped one (legacy single-tenant data predating the #412 backfill) resolves to SYSTEM_SCOPE **and is logged at WARNING**, because that residual is a property of the DATA, not of the source, and a source census would report it as zero while it is not. `None` is refused rather than widened — returning SYSTEM_SCOPE for an absent row would rebuild the fail-open default one layer up.

  **The five that stay are the `#895` ownership door, and narrowing them would be a regression.** `telegram_accounts` (×2) and `telegram_service` resolve a row cross-tenant *and then* apply `caller_may_act_on_queue_row` — the handler IS the ownership check, and the pattern exists because the gate cannot resolve a short prefix. `telegram_notification` and `telegram_utils.validate_queue_item` are entry-point fetches with no prior row to draw scope from. All five share one prerequisite, and this change **narrows what that prerequisite is rather than clearing it**. `_apply_tenant_filter` is still strict `column == value`, so a narrowed read makes a legacy unstamped row invisible — but `_owned_or_null`, added here, now expresses owned-OR-NULL in SQL on any model, and `_apply_tenant_filter` has exactly **one** production caller. So the remaining work is wiring an owned-OR-NULL scope through the read filter, not inventing one; the honest statement is that it is a change to the read path (≈50 tests assert on that filter directly) deliberately kept out of a write-side PR, **not** a structural impossibility. Recorded precisely because a deferral reason outlives the defect it describes: an earlier draft of this entry claimed the rule was inexpressible, which this change's own helper falsifies.

  **Six live crash paths on legacy data, found by the sweep and fixed.** `chat_settings_id=str(row.chat_settings_id) if row.chat_settings_id else None` appears nine times; three are `HistoryCreateParams` ownership stamps where `None` is the correct value for an unowned row, and **six are repository calls that raise `TenantContextError` on any unstamped row** — measured, not inferred: `increment_times_posted`, `update_metadata` and `update_cloud_info` were each called with `chat_settings_id=None` and each raised. Fail-closed, so loud rather than dangerous, but a live defect on pre-backfill data. They become `scope_of_row`, which is the vocabulary that idiom was reaching for.

  **The pin moves 64 → 48 in this diff, which is the ratchet working as built.** `test_system_scope_inventory_is_pinned_exactly` is equality-pinned, so burn-down progress is red until the constant moves in the PR that earns it. The two new gate classes are mutation-proven: reverting the fetch-then-mutate ownership check, and separately deleting `transition`'s WHERE clause, each turn `TestQueueMutatorsRefuseAForeignTenant` red. That test carries its own positive control — the owner's writes still succeed — without which a method that refused *everyone* would pass it.
- **#790 M.1a: the observed-use measurement — the M.3 parity bar's own definition, derived from production instead of asserted.** FC-7 clause 3 sets the bar at the commands *"in production use"*; nobody had measured production use, so the bar was an inventory of what **exists**, judged against a definition about what is **used**. `scripts/observed_use.py` closes that gap in one read-only query — `GROUP BY interaction_type, interaction_name` with counts and `max(created_at)`, reported together because a command used 400 times whose last use was in March means something different from one used 40 times last week. It lands beside `m1_preflight.py` as the same kind of instrument: a read-only profile query the owner rules on, which rules nothing itself.

  **A zero is classified, not counted.** `log_command`/`log_callback` are hand-placed at each call site — there is no middleware — so the set of names the table *can* hold is a property of the code, not of the product surface. The script splits the dispatch surface into RECORDED (a count is a real count), SILENT (no code path writes this name, so its zero means nothing) and RENAMED (recorded under a different string than its dispatch key, so a string-equality join to the dispatch table manufactures false zeros — `sap` records as `switch_account_from_post`, and `schedule_confirm` as `schedule_action`). `handle_removed_command` does not log, so all twelve retired commands went dark **at retirement** rather than when demand ended; `RETIRED_ON` carries those dates so a fossil count is never read as current demand.

  **The surface is derived from source, never listed in the module** — the rule `m1_preflight` states as *"a second enumeration of the same predicates is exactly the fork that would drift"*. A hand-kept copy of the dispatch table goes stale the first time someone adds a callback, and the reader who then trusts a zero is hitting the exact defect the script exists to expose. The keys are read out of `telegram_service.py` with `ast` at run time, and SILENT is **computed** as dispatch-minus-writable, so a newly added key with no log call is silent by default rather than by anyone remembering. Only what is genuinely not derivable is declared — the retirement dates (git history) and the renamed handlers (verified at the call sites) — and a call site whose name is a variable rather than a literal is reported as UNRESOLVED instead of dropped, because a silently missing name is what turns a real zero into a false one. The derivation immediately earned itself: it caught `schedule_confirm` logging under another name, which a read of the handler had missed.

  **The instrument is checked against an independent one before any zero is trusted.** `posting_history` is written by a different code path; a manual card action lands a row in both. The pair check matches `posted`/`skipped`/`rejected` against their callbacks at 216/214, 1050/1044 and 15/15, and monthly agreement runs 96.5–100% across all eight months of production — so a zero for a RECORDED name is a real zero, and this table is not vouching for itself.

  **Read-only is enforced and then confirmed.** The server is given `default_transaction_read_only=on`, the session is set read-only client-side, and the script **reads the setting back**, refusing to proceed unless the server agrees. Intent is not enforcement; the assertion is what makes "did you only read?" answerable by something other than the script's own say-so. Identity is asserted the same way — the resolved host must equal the one production endpoint — and the DSN is taken from the consumer's own config, never reconstructed and never echoed.
- **#654 clause 2: every legacy uniqueness rule now has to prove what it REFUSES *and* what it PERMITS — because schema parity can only show that two sides agree, never that they are right.** The issue asks for two things and **the first was already served**: `scripts/schema_parity.py` diffs two live schema signatures and `TestSchemaParity` runs it against a runner-replayed database and a `create_all` one, in CI, behind a real `postgres:15` service with `REQUIRE_TEST_DATABASE=1` and no marker filter — derived from live catalogs, not pinned to a count, and already carrying its own can-it-fail control. That is recorded on the issue rather than left to be rediscovered. **The second clause — "no test asserts that the 021/015/040 constraints behave as intended" — was untouched**: two of the three named constraints had zero tests and the third had two asserting the ORM constraint's *name*. The gap is not redundant with parity, and 021 is the proof: it exists because `unique_permanent_lock_per_media` was previously an unconditional index that collided a permanent lock with an ordinary one on the same media item. The fix is a **predicate**, a predicate is intent written as SQL, and a comparator can only ever confirm that two files spell it the same way — if the model and the migration encode the same wrong predicate, parity passes and the constraint is still wrong.

  `tests/scripts/test_legacy_uniqueness_behaviour.py` proves each of the eleven non-PK rules twice. **REFUSES** asserts on `diag.constraint_name` rather than on the exception type — `media_items` carries three uniqueness rules, so a bare `pytest.raises(UniqueViolation)` would pass while a *neighbouring* rule did the refusing, and would keep passing if the rule under test were dropped entirely. **PERMITS** asserts the near-miss commits **and lands a row** (a `near_miss` that wrote nothing would satisfy a bare no-raise check forever), and it is the half that catches a rule constraining too much: 021's near-miss is a permanent lock *and* a temporary lock coexisting — literally the bug 021 fixed; 040's is two credentials differing only in `auth_method` — literally what 040 widened the key to allow; 015's is an Instagram token surviving an index scoped to `google_drive`; 013's is many rows sharing a NULL `instagram_media_id`, which unconditional would allow exactly one un-backfilled media item in the entire table.

  **The denominator is `pg_indexes` on the live schema, never a list in the file.** A count assertion would restate today's schema and pass by running the code it guards; instead the coverage test demands a case for whatever the catalog holds, so **a uniqueness rule added by a future migration fails this suite until someone states what it refuses and what it permits** — with the reverse direction checked too, since a case naming a dropped rule would otherwise keep "passing" against inserts that collide with nothing. Primary keys are excluded deliberately and *visibly*: a named constant with a stated reason, and a test proving that removing the exclusion changes the result. Scope is uniqueness and the bound is stated rather than implied — CHECK vocabularies are covered by #944's drift checks, and FK/NOT NULL have no intent layer between definition and behaviour for parity to miss.

  **30 tests.** The three self-contained controls are named for what they actually do — they mutate the schema *inside the test*, so they prove the corresponding `permits` assertion is sensitive to the property it is about; they do **not** test the migration files, and their earlier names (`test_undoing_021s_predicate_is_caught`) promised a regression they could not catch. **Real-migration coverage is carried by the ordinary near-miss tests and is measured, not asserted**: strip the predicate from `021`, drop `auth_method` from `040`'s key, or remove `015`'s `google_drive` scoping, and the matching `near_miss` test fails in each case. **That third row only became true in review** — 015's near-miss differed from its setup row in `service_name`, a KEY column, so the pair never collided predicate or no predicate and a real 015 regression was caught by **nothing**; the whole file stayed green. It now uses two rows identical on the key and excluded by the predicate. Separately, the primary-key exclusion has the control it needs: it was **claimed in this changelog, the PR body and the commit message before it existed** — run by hand during development and written up as though it had shipped — and now exists, verified by two mutants (dropping the exclusion, and pointing it at a suffix that matches nothing).

- **#790 M.1a: the pre-window profile battery, assembled — and run against production, where it moves three of the transform spec's five forks.** The M.1 transform spec's §5.1 already specifies this script (*"each is a plain SELECT over §4's stated predicates; the assembled script belongs beside `fc8_gate.py`"*); `scripts/m1_preflight.py` is that script, not a second enumeration — every one of its 54 checks carries the `spec` section it derives from, so the battery and the spec can be **diffed** rather than compared from memory. It is read-only (session set read-only besides), it rules nothing, and it re-implements no part of `fc8_gate.py` (#792), which is imported. G-PROFILE (§2.7) pairs every "impossible" edge with a pre-window profile and an in-file assertion; this is the first half, and it exists because there is no shadow phase — a mapping that cannot insert a row otherwise discovers that at 3e, inside the window, with the legacy system stood down.

  **What the production run moved.** §3.3's collapse counts — the ones the spec says are being chased — come back **`chats = 2, group_chats = 1`**, which resolves two forks in opposite directions: **Fork C collapses** (C1/C2/C3 coincide, the one-line ruling §3.3 describes) while **Fork D does not**, landing exactly on §3.4's named `chats > 1, group_chats = 1` case. **Fork E is live at 233** rows against a spec that sized it *"plausible but few"* and expected it might be moot — and **E1 is unavailable**, because the switch timeline it reuses has **0** source rows out of 7,496, which also strands all 1,586 `instagram_api` rows in the attribution gap. The largest finding is that **Fork D is not a cardinality question**: the §4.8 workspace-mismatch profile returns **1,095** history rows whose tenant is the DM-rooted chat while their media belongs to the group chat, spanning four months — under minting rule W those become intents whose composite tenant FK is inexpressible, and the same chat resolves at ladder rung 4 so W refuses its workspace anyway. D decides whether 1,095 rows of real posting history transform or quarantine.

  **Three exit codes that are not a pass.** `HALT` is the easy one; `ERROR` (a check could not be answered) and `INCOMPLETE` (a table in the database is claimed by no check) stay separate because *could not ask* and *asked and found trouble* have different remedies, and collapsing them fails toward "everything is fine". **The coverage denominator comes from the database, never from a list in the module** — which is how `posting_history_dedup_archive` (#941) surfaced unprompted on the first production run: created in production 2026-08-19, two days after the spec was written, absent from `02` §9's fourteen, and standing in front of `DROP SCHEMA legacy CASCADE`. A fourth state — *skipped because an optional table is genuinely absent* — is disclosed as `Skipped is not passed.` rather than folded into either side, and the existence test lives outside the SQL because PostgreSQL parses a whole statement before executing it, so a `to_regclass` guard inside it cannot help.

  **The owner ladder becomes one expression** (`scripts/m1_ladder.py`), consumed by the battery now and by the M1-02 transform later, because two copies of a four-rung precedence rule is how a profile blesses a chat the transform then quarantines. It does **not** resolve the rung-1/2 activity ambiguity — `04` L80 qualifies only rung 3 as "earliest *active*" while the next bullet drops `is_active=false` rows and the derived owner must land as a `workspace_members` row — so both readings are computed and a corpus on which they disagree is a blocker. **Rungs 1 and 2 have zero real rows behind them** (no production chat has an `owner` or `admin` membership), so the battery prints which rungs a corpus exercises on every run and the ladder's coverage is synthetic by necessity. Reachability is the weak version and is not what is asserted: every rung test seeds evidence for its own rung **and every rung beneath it**, because a fixture in which only rung 1 is possible says nothing about precedence — the half a wrong `ORDER BY` silently inverts. A **7-mutant battery** kills all seven, each by the test that should own it; one initially **survived**, exposing a hollow assertion (repetition cannot detect non-determinism when the plan is stable, and the `min()` check was passing on a coin flip), and its replacement seeds the larger id first so heap order and sort order disagree by construction.

  **Review found a check that could not fail, and the fix generalised it into nine that can.** `posting_history.status_outside_the_1to1_map` counted rows whose `status` is outside the five the transform maps 1:1 — and `check_history_status` admits *exactly those five*, so it returned 0 correctly and **could never return anything else**. Proven rather than argued: seeding a sixth value returns `CheckViolation`, not a query result. A green light nobody can make red, inside the instrument meant to certify M.1c–e. Cross-referencing every legacy CHECK against every value list in the file found **a second instance** (`user_interactions.interaction_type_outside_the_check_set`), and chasing *that* one surfaced a live application bug (#946: `onboarding_dropout` telemetry has never recorded a row, because a CHECK forbids the value and the `CheckViolation` is swallowed into a warning). **Deleting them would have lost a real concern** — the risk was never a stray row, which the constraint prevents, but a *migration* widening the vocabulary so the mapping silently stops being total. The row-count form cannot see that coming; a **vocabulary-drift** check sees it the moment the constraint moves. So the duplication becomes the point: the file states the vocabulary it assumes and the check asserts the database still agrees, the same diff-not-memory principle the `spec` field exists for, applied to a constraint. Nine constraints now carry it (status, posting method, lock reason, instance role, interaction type, onboarding step, queue status, user role, audit entity type), each counting three distinct drifts — widened, narrowed, and **absent**, which admits everything and would otherwise report a clean zero. Verified across both PostgreSQL versions in play, since PG 17 renders `pg_get_constraintdef` differently from PG 15 and the value extraction had to survive both.

  **Every check now has to prove it can fail, structurally rather than by sampling.** A reviewer seeded six deliberate faults; five fired and one could not. A sample of six found one problem in a population of twenty, so the answer is not to sample harder — `tests/scripts/test_m1_positive_controls.py` asserts that **every BLOCKER has a registered seed** and that the seed makes it fire, and the same one tier down for every COUNT. 56 parametrised positive controls plus two structural tests: 28 blockers fire, 28 counts move, each asserted **0 before and nonzero after** so the seed is what caused it. A check added later carries the same burden on the day it lands rather than waiting for the next review. `DISCLOSURE` checks are excluded and the harness says why in prose rather than leaving the gap silent — they assert nothing, and two are un-grouped aggregates that return a row on an empty table by construction.

  Also measured and reproducible from the same run: the **FC-8 zero-row gate is CLEAR** (both counts 0, `google_drive` only at live 4,554 / total 4,619, so **#793 stays unforced** on this corpus) and twelve further §5.1 profiles return zero — recorded as genuine passes rather than as silence. Point-in-time, not invariant: legacy is live until step 1, which is why G-PROFILE keeps the in-file arm.

- **#915 (PR-B of two, closes the issue): the checkpointed publish-pipeline executor body — the deepest increment in Phase L, and the last F/L gate before Phase M.** `run_publish_pipeline` drives a claimed `publish_pipeline` job through the `02` §5 transaction-per-checkpoint ladder (`none→transit_uploaded→container_created→container_ready→publish_called→effect_confirmed`), with every domain transaction re-CASing the lease (one `_leased_tx` prologue — a fenced worker can neither advance the ladder nor reach the provider work behind it), permits acquired on their own committed transactions via the L.3 rail, and the terminal transaction landing permit outcome + `posted` flip + `times_posted`/recent-lock/`last_posted_at` + job finalization together, then the best-effort inline FC-3.5 destroy. **The §4 flip integrates slice 1 verbatim**: PROCEED continues; DEFERRED reschedules to the account's next slot with the claim's attempt restored (deferral is normal operation — §4 names the approval-TTL reaper, not attempts, as the bound) and writes the codebase's first direct `cap_deferred` audit row; the (1,0) raise rolls the whole transaction back. **Error 9 is a cap, not a fault** — and since no `publishing→approved` edge exists in the seeded matrix, "same path as a local cap denial" is delivered as the defer MECHANICS: the permit resolves failed (a confirmed-safe failure, §6), the job reschedules to the next slot, the debit and key 4 stand, and the next run permits generation 2 — one provider call per authorization, counted. **Resume is proven from every checkpoint, two ways**: cold entry at each committed rung (parametrized; no upstream rung re-done) plus a kill at every provider seam with re-claimed resumes — including the gate's named case, kill-between-publish-permit-and-call landing `publishing_ambiguous` with ZERO calls ever placed under that permit. Ambiguity is TYPED: `MetaLostResponse` (deliberately not a MetaError — it is a lost response, not an answer) is the only exception the provider blocks catch; everything untyped propagates so a crash looks like a crash, with the resume protocol as the stated backstop for an adapter that forgets to wrap. The stub Meta adapter is the sandbox until M.3 (there is no shadow phase); the default-off §8 usage pre-check ships flag-at-the-executor-seam with its 5-minute per-`provider_account_ref` cache. **The gate caught two real design defects test-first**: a refund stamped after the terminal flip (the terminal-freeze trigger refuses it — refund now precedes the flip in-tx), and blanket exception handling around provider calls that would have swallowed crash-shaped exceptions. Mutation battery: 44 mutations across executor, jobs doors and seams, zero survivors — with one initial survivor exposing a real hole (a zombie worker could reach a genuine transit upload before its first fence) that became its own test, one inert mutant exposing a dead constant (deleted), and five sentinels re-proven against the post-review refactor including the consolidated lease predicate.
