- **#915 (PR-A of two): the FC-3 transit store — the workspace-signed Cloudinary layer the publish pipeline stages media through, previously absent (`cloud_storage.py` is the flat legacy uploader: global folder, public delivery, three FC-3 requirements short).** `TransitStore` delivers D28 as ratified: every upload is signed per-request from per-call credentials (no `cloudinary.config()` global, zero preset objects) with `folder=ws/{workspace_id}` (FC-3.1) and `type=authenticated` (FC-3.3); the folder interpolates the **parsed, canonical UUID**, never the raw string, so a non-canonical spelling cannot split one workspace across sibling folders — and D28's conditional ("escalate if signed-params do not deliver the isolation cleanly") is NOT triggered: the signature covers the folder and type parameters, so each authorization is scoped to exactly one upload in exactly one workspace prefix. Delivery URLs are signed and **non-expiring** per the amended FC-3.2 (D38) — no `auth_token`, no `expires_at`; the TTL is the asset's, which is why `destroy` passes `invalidate=True` (a CDN-cached copy surviving destruction would quietly extend the lifetime D38 caps). The FC-3.6 sweep gets its missing halves: a lister (ONE global paged walk over `ws/` per resource type, both types, full pagination, age-filtered client-side — verified against SDK 1.44 that `api.resources` cannot filter older-than server-side) and a deleter that **raises on a polite non-ok destroy** so `execute_reap_transit_assets` (merged at L.7, tested now for the first time) counts only completed destroys. Two review catches worth naming because each was a contract that read fine and was not: the deleter's docstring advertised a raise the code did not own (a `{"result": "error"}` answer was counted as reaped), and the strict single-format timestamp parse made the row-level fail-toward-stale rule **corpus-wide** — one benign provider format shift (fractional seconds) and every in-flight transit asset parses "stale" on the next tick, destroying mid-publish media; the parse is now general ISO-8601 and fail-toward-stale is reserved for the genuinely garbled row. Every SDK door carries the time bound, sourced from the egress floor's `upload` class rather than copied (the SDK is not behind the floor, so the bound rides here; a floor retune cannot silently orphan it). Proven by a 31-mutation battery with **zero survivors** — every asserted property reddens under its named mutation, map in the PR — plus a closed-set gate pinning the media-kind map to `ck_media_kind`. FC-3.5's inline destroy-on-post is the executor's terminal step and lands with PR-B (the pipeline body), which composes this store.

### Changed

- **#947: CI lint scope stops being an enumeration — ruff now runs over the repository, and `scripts/` and `setup.py` are gated for the first time.** The `Lint` job ran `ruff check src/ cli/ tests/`, so the nine Python files under `scripts/` — `migration_runner.py` (843 lines, the thing that executes the migrations), `advertised_ddl.py`, `tenancy_gate.py`, `fc8_gate.py`, `schema_parity.py`, `telegram_ratchet.py` among them — plus root `setup.py` had never been lint- or format-gated. Surfaced by rajan reviewing #944, which claimed ruff-clean on two new `scripts/` files against a green `Lint` check that had not opened either one. **A green check that did not examine what it appears to certify is worse than no check**, because it is read as evidence.

  **The fix is scope-by-container rather than a longer list, because the list is the defect.** #816 fixed this same gate one cycle ago by widening `src/ cli/` to `src/ cli/ tests/` — an enumeration extended, which left the mechanism that goes stale exactly where it was. It went stale again, and this repo has already written down why: *a list of names goes stale the next time one is added*. CI now runs `ruff check .` and `ruff format . --check`, and exclusions live in `ruff.toml` where the rest of the lint policy already is. A new top-level directory is gated on arrival instead of waiting to be noticed.

  **There were three allowlists, and two of them disagreed.** CI ran `src/ cli/ tests/`; `scripts/lint.sh` — the script the repo tells contributors to run before pushing — ran `cli/ src/`, narrower than CI and missing `tests/` that #816 had just added; `CLAUDE.md` documented a third copy. All three now name the repository, so there is nothing left to drift apart. Widening CI alone would have left a contributor running the documented pre-push script clean and still failing the gate.

  **`extend-exclude`, not `exclude` — measured rather than assumed.** Plain `exclude` REPLACES ruff built-in exclusions rather than adding to them, so it would enlist `.venv/`, `build/` and friends the moment scope became `.`. Driven directly: a vendored file carrying an unused import inside `.venv/` is found under `exclude` and clean under `extend-exclude`.

  **Markdown is excluded, and that is what the widening actually cost.** ruff 0.16 formats Python code blocks inside `.md`, so at repository root it considered 380 `.py` and 94 `.md`; seventeen files would have been reformatted and **every one of them was Markdown** — zero Python files were dirty. Gating them would fail a code lint on a prose edit, over snippets that are deliberately partial. With the exclusion the scope is exactly the 380 tracked Python files.

  **Green on arrival, on both the version that is installed and the version CI installs.** CI runs an unpinned `pip install ruff`, which resolves to 0.16.4 today; the local toolchain is 0.15.10. Both report rc 0 on `ruff check .` and `380 files already formatted` on `ruff format . --check`. There are no findings to clear, no ratchet, and nothing deferred to a cleanup pass.

  **The widened gate is proven to bite, with the control that makes the proof mean something.** A deliberate `F401` in `scripts/migration_runner.py`: old scope rc 0 `All checks passed!`, new scope rc 1. A deliberate formatting defect in `scripts/tenancy_gate.py`: old scope rc 0 `370 files already formatted`, new scope rc 1. Each step of the job is proven separately and each against the old scope going *green on the same violation* — without that control a red CI run shows only that something failed, not that the widening is what caught it. Both were then driven through real CI on this branch and reverted.

  **The file counts in the issue reconcile rather than conflict, and the tree moved while this was open.** Nine was correct for `main` when this was measured; eleven — the issue's figure — became correct the moment #944 landed, which it did mid-PR, adding `scripts/m1_ladder.py` and `scripts/m1_preflight.py`; the `12 files already formatted` in the issue body is those eleven plus `scripts/migrations/NOTE_waitlist_table.md`, which ruff counts because it formats Markdown. All three numbers were right about different trees, which is the argument for not writing any of them into a gate.

  **That merge is also the demonstration.** #944 is the PR whose two ungated `scripts/` files surfaced this issue. It merged between two CI runs on this branch, and because `pull_request` builds a merge commit, the widened gate picked both files up **with no edit to the scope** — the file count in CI went 380 to 385 on its own. Under an appended list they would have been gated only because someone remembered `scripts/` a week earlier; under a container they were gated on arrival. Both pass, so #944's self-reported *ruff clean* was true — it is now gated rather than asserted, which was the whole point.

  **One thing deliberately not bundled: `pip install ruff` is still unpinned.** `ruff.toml` pins the rule set precisely so lint policy is not decided by whichever version CI installed that day, but the tool itself floats — and ruff 0.16 adding Markdown formatting is that hazard already having happened, a capability change silently altering what `ruff format <path>` touches with no repo edit. It is the same defect family as this one and it is out of scope here; filed separately.

- **#787: window step 3b becomes executable under its own declared actor — a `SECURITY DEFINER` door owned by the database-owner role, ratified as option (d), and the migration gate's green now means the window WORKS.** `svc_migration` could not `ALTER` the owner-owned legacy tables and **no `GRANT` could make it able to**: `ALTER` requires table ownership or membership in the owning role and is not a grantable privilege. So the printed window died at 3b, one step before the schema move — and two of the eleven migration-gate assertions passed by asserting that failure. That was the honest way to hold a known refusal, but *"the gate is green"* and *"the window works"* had stopped being the same sentence, and a reader cannot tell them apart from a badge.

  The door scopes the elevation to **statements** rather than to a **role**: 050's two `ALTER`s live in a parameterless function owned by the owner actor, stood up at step 0 alongside the bootstrap, and `svc_migration` completes 3b holding neither ownership nor membership. Both seam assertions now assert a **green apply**, `D1_REFUSAL` is deleted, and the same door regressing surfaces as a red gate instead of a green one.

  **All three ruling conditions are gated rather than intended**, each with the positive control that makes it evidence: `REVOKE … FROM PUBLIC` is the *entire* access control (a new definer function has `proacl IS NULL`, which is EXECUTE to PUBLIC — the control drives a no-privilege role performing owner DDL through an otherwise identical door that skipped the revoke); the body must be **parameterless and free of dynamic SQL**, asserted as a census over `pg_proc` so it covers the door added next, with a control proving the detector fires *and* that the flagged shape really does let a no-rights role alter a table the author never named; and the door is **dropped in both step-8 stand-down variants**, the deliberate answer to its surviving an aborted window — an abort reaching neither variant leaves it standing, which is why it lives in its own schema, enumerable from the catalog and removed by one statement.

  Scoped to **050 and only 050** — the window's one legacy-DDL step. Migrations 046/047/049 carry legacy `ALTER`s too and stay owner-only: they are pre-window work the plan's gated apply already covers, two of them are read by the enum SSOT parity gate, and each additional door would be another owner-privileged surface with no window that calls it. **One cost, stated rather than buried:** 050 is now the only file in the legacy lineage that is not self-contained — a bare `psql -f` against a database without the door fails on an undefined function.

- **#912: the L.3 permit rail gains a call-counting stub that counts and a concurrency test that is concurrent — both built by `rajan` during the #904 review and recovered verbatim from a pre-compaction transcript after his worktree was gone.** Neither is a defect in L.3's behaviour; both replace tests that **describe more than they do**. `TestAtMostOnePublishCallAtEveryKillBoundary`'s docstring promised a stub recording every invocation, but `calls` was declared and asserted empty in two places and populated by nothing — so both assertions passed **by construction** and would have passed identically had the code called the provider a hundred times, on a rail whose entire purpose is at-most-one publish call. The replacement wires a real stub around the caller contract from `acquire_permit`'s own docstring (the provider call may only follow a returned, committed permit) and fires exactly once, only for the permit that committed, never for the fenced one. Both dead lines are **removed** rather than left beside the real one: two assertions where one is decorative means the next reader trusts the wrong one. The test named for two concurrent resolvers ran sequentially — there was no `asyncio.gather` anywhere in the file — and the replacement is a genuine race across separate connections on the same op from the same source state, one winner and one refusal every run; the sequential test is kept, being narrower rather than wrong. **Landed verified, because landing them unverified would be the same defect at one remove:** both pass on current main (19 for the file), and both BITE — removing the lease fence reddens the call-counting stub *alone*, and making the resolve CAS unconditional reddens the race *alone*, with no cross-firing, which is the discrimination distinguishing them from what they replace.


### Fixed

- **Two site links pointed at a Telegram handle we do not control (#1008)** — `waitlist-form.tsx` and `instances/page.tsx` hardcoded `t.me/storydump_bot`. The product's bot is **`storydump_app_bot`**, which is not a guess: it is the value `NEXT_PUBLIC_TELEGRAM_BOT_NAME` supplies to the Telegram Login Widget, read out of the live production bundle. `t.me/storydump_bot` is a live account under someone else's control — so the waitlist success panel was offering it to every signup as *"Join the Telegram community"*, and `/instances` was pointing every signed-in user with no instance at it through a primary button labelled *"Open Telegram Bot"*, which is the site's own instruction for how to start using the product.
  - **The handle is no longer written down anywhere.** Both links now come from `lib/telegram-bot.ts`, which reads the same env var the login widget does. Those two were the **only** hardcoded occurrences in the repo — `telegram-login-button.tsx` and the backend's `bot_username` already derived it and were already correct — so the defect was precisely that a literal had drifted from the configured value. Correcting the literals would have left that trap armed for the next person.
  - **`botUrl()` returns `null` when the variable is unset, and the call sites render nothing.** A best-effort `t.me/undefined` is the same shape as the bug — a link to a handle we do not control. A missing link is a nuisance; that is not.

- **#928: an unauthenticated user is no longer told the health card is broken — the failure branch is enumerated rather than a catch-all with named cases in front of it.** #926 made a 403 read as a refusal and left everything else as a fault, which closed #922 — but `_validate_admin` also raises **401**, so the exact defect #922 described survived one status over: a caller whose credential has expired was shown "Failed to load health data". Review found it by asking what else that validator raises.

  **Enumerating the whole surface turned up a fourth case and a body-shape bug.** Measured against the running endpoint rather than reasoned about: **401** (invalid credential), **403** (authenticated, not an operator), **422** (malformed `chat_id`), and **429** — the global `30/minute` limiter applies here, since the route carries no `@limiter.limit` of its own. slowapi's 429 body is `{"error": ...}`, **not** `{"detail": ...}`, so both `_api` and `_apiGet` — which read only `detail` — were surfacing a bare "Request failed" for it; they now read either key.

  Messages are split by **what the user can do differently**: 401 → reopen from Telegram; 403 → the refusal, by design; 429 → wait and retry. `_failureMessage` holds the enumeration because 401 and 429 mean the same thing on every card, while the 403 copy differs per card and is supplied by the caller. 422, 5xx and a network error carrying no status at all fall to the fault message **deliberately** — they are alike in the only way that matters here, in that the user cannot respond to them differently. That residual is the honest remainder of an enumeration, not a catch-all concealing named cases.

  Verified by clicking in every arm: 401, 403, 429, 422, 500 and 503 stubbed at the route, **plus 401 and 403 driven live against the real server** — the 401 by invalidating the credential at request time, after the page had loaded, which is the real-world shape (a session expiring while the dashboard sits open). The setup checklist survives all eight runs.

### Fixed

- **#905: an id prefix is matched literally — `%` and `_` were LIKE metacharacters, and it was TWO lookups, not one.** Both `get_by_id_prefix` implementations built `cast(col, String).like(f"{prefix}%")` from caller input, so a prefix carrying `_` (any single character) or `%` (any run) was interpreted as a **pattern** and silently widened the match set. **Not exploitable today, which is the reason to fix it:** both call sites sit behind an ownership gate that refuses however many rows matched — **the protection lives in a different component from the defect**, and any future caller not behind that gate inherits an unescaped pattern match with nothing in the signature to warn them. A thing that happens to be right is not a thing that must be right (#868's distinction). **The issue named the account lookup; a sweep found the same expression in `queue_repository`, and that one is the reachable instance** — its caller passes `SYSTEM_SCOPE` deliberately and checks ownership afterwards, so a fix patching only the named site would have left the live one. Both now route through one `id_prefix_matches` helper. **Escaping was considered and rejected**: `.startswith(prefix, autoescape=True)` is correct only while a keyword argument stays present, and an edit that drops it — or a new call site that never adds it — reintroduces the defect silently with the operator still reading fine. Comparing a fixed-length `left()` slice removes the pattern language from the expression entirely: there is no metacharacter to escape because nothing interprets one, and no argument to forget. **Born-red, and the discriminator cannot pass for the wrong reason**: a UUID contains only hex and dashes, so no real id contains `%` or `_` literally — a wildcard-bearing prefix therefore matches MORE than one row under the defect and ZERO under the fix, never the same answer. Measured against the reverted expression, `get_by_id_prefix('%')` returns a row. The suite also pins the escape character itself (an escaping implementation that forgot to escape its own escape would pass the wildcard cases and fail that one) and states, rather than changes, that an empty prefix still matches everything — this closes a metacharacter hole, not a missing-input check, and turning empty into "match nothing" would be a behaviour change hiding inside a security fix.

- **#922: the admin gate on `/system-status` now reads as a refusal instead of a malfunction.** After #898 gated deployment health to administrators, a non-admin expanding the System Status card saw **"Failed to load health data"** — a deliberate authorisation decision rendering as a fault. That costs twice: a support report sends someone chasing a defect that does not exist, and it trains users to ignore that line, so a *real* failure of the same card would hide behind it.

  **The gate is correct and unchanged; only the presentation moved.** A 403 now renders `Deployment health is available to administrators only.` — any other failure still renders `Failed to load health data`, which is the point: refusal and fault must be distinguishable, and replacing one blanket message with another would have missed it. Both `_api` and `_apiGet` now carry the HTTP status on the thrown error, so callers branch on the **status** rather than matching server copy, which would couple the UI to a message string.

  **Kept the card rather than hiding it for non-admins, and the surface decided it:** `_loadSystemStatus` calls `_renderSetupChecklist()` **before** the API request, from local state. The card's SETUP half — Instagram, Google Drive, Media Library, Posting, Delivery — is the tenant's *own* data and needs no admin rights. Hiding the card would have taken that from every non-admin, which is most users, to suppress one line. The collapsed badge is likewise unaffected: it is derived from setup state (`3/5 setup items configured`), not from the gated payload.

  Verified by clicking as an actual non-admin, with both controls run rather than assumed: **non-admin** → checklist intact, refusal line; **admin** (`users.role` promoted, then reverted) → full health section; **HTTP 500 and 503** → `Failed to load health data`, i.e. the fault path still says fault. The setup checklist survives all three.
- **#923: a LIVE cross-tenant disclosure found by classifying the 12, plus 2 dead methods deleted and the other 10 declared.** #924 fixed the mechanism; these were the instances it could then see, and the question for each was *why* it was unscoped rather than whether it was exploitable. **The one live defect:** `handle_account_remove_confirm` is a Telegram callback handler — `telegram_service.py:381` routes the `account_remove` prefix straight to it, so `account_id` arrives in callback data and is chosen by whoever taps. It read the account unscoped and rendered its display name and username into the confirmation card, while the **execute** step immediately below passes `chat_id` into `deactivate_account`, which enforces ownership and refuses. A guarded write behind an unguarded read is the existence oracle `get_owned`'s docstring warns about: the card disclosed an account the very next step would decline to touch. Sixth in the #891/#895/#898/#900/#901 family, reachable from inbound data. Fixed at the DOOR with `_require_account_ownership`, reusing the same not-found shape for a foreign id as for an invented one so the guard does not become the oracle it closes. **Born-red**: removing the guard reddens `test_a_foreign_account_is_not_disclosed_in_the_confirmation_card`, which asserts on the DISCLOSURE rather than the refusal — a test checking only "it answered not found" would pass while the card leaked. Paired with an owner-still-sees-their-card positive control, because a handler that refused everything would pass the first test and break the feature. **Two methods deleted rather than scoped:** `get_by_id_prefix` and `count_active` had zero production callers — the hits that looked like callers were `queue_repo` and `media_repo`, different classes with the same method names — so their tests went with them. **The other 10 are classified and say so in code, not just here** (navi's #919 point: "intentionally global" was legible to a developer reading the PR and invisible to the next reader): `get_all`/`get_all_active` are operator-only via `list_all_accounts_unscoped`; `get_by_username` *must* be deployment-wide or the duplicate check it serves cannot reject a duplicate; `get_by_instagram_id`/`get_by_meta_account_id` are OAuth identification, which runs before a tenant is known because it is the step that finds the tenant. **The gate does NOT cover this fix, stated plainly:** it evaluates the repository signature, so a door-level guard clears nothing — the burn-down went 12 → 10 only because two methods were deleted, and an entry leaves it only when the METHOD takes a tenant. Reading a stable count as "no progress" would be wrong and reading it as "10 defects" would be wronger; the gate comment now says so. **The `get_by_id_prefix` deletion also retargeted #925's tests rather than deleting them (#927 review):** its six behavioural tests proved #905's literal-matching fix against the account-repo copy — the unreachable one — while `queue_repository`, which that file's own docstring calls "the one reachable today", had only a source-grep. They now run against the live path, mutation-proven (reintroducing the LIKE pattern on the queue side reddens all seven). The cross-repository invariant keeps its universal safety negative and scopes its "routes through the helper" assertion to modules that actually perform a prefix lookup — written as an unconditional presence check, it read as the invariant but was narrower, and reddened when a module legitimately stopped having the lookup.

### Fixed

- **#908: the read-transaction leak fix (#907/#910) swept across the remaining ~30 `commit(); refresh()` sites — the idle-in-transaction class is now closed fleet-wide, not just at `create_run`.** #907 landed `BaseRepository.commit_and_refresh` (commit, refresh the row's server-set fields, and END the read transaction the refresh opens) and applied it at the single highest-value site; this sweeps the rest. **The primitive is leak-safe both ways (navi fuzzed it), so the residual risk of the SWEEP was BEHAVIOURAL, not resource** — a careless transform silently changing a control-flow path — which is why every site was read rather than sed-swapped. **32 pure adjacent `commit(); refresh(x)` sequences** across 11 repositories were transformed; **two sites were EXCLUDED and had their leak closed differently, because a blind swap would have changed behaviour:** `create_membership` guards its commit with a swallowed `IntegrityError` recovery that returns the existing row on a duplicate, and `commit_and_refresh` routes through the wrapper `commit()`, which records a circuit-breaker **failure** and logs on any commit error — so the swap would fault the breaker on what is a normal, expected dedup (the `except` would catch the error either way; the breaker pollution is the real reason); `set_mix` commits ONCE and refreshes in a loop, where the per-object primitive would re-commit per record. Both instead call `end_read_transaction()` directly — one commit, the reads closed, behaviour untouched. **Proven on real backend state, the #910 way:** `tests/integration/test_commit_refresh_sweep.py` establishes the pattern once on a representative swept site (`UserRepository.create`) — a `pg_stat_activity` probe asserts no backend is left idle-in-transaction on `users` after the call; born-red against the pre-sweep `commit(); refresh()` form and revert-demonstrated. **Collateral, stated:** unit tests that pinned exact commit counts were updated — a swept read-then-write site now commits three times (the read's `end_read_transaction`, the write, and the refresh's `end_read_transaction`) where it committed twice; the five non-swept read-then-write sites (deletes and refresh-less updates) are unchanged at two. `commit()`'s re-raise-on-failure preserves exception propagation at every pure site, unchanged from #907. Not a regression — the leak predates today (#907).

- **#896: the F.1 extinction gate keyed on parameter SPELLING, so any table whose tenancy lives in a JOIN passed vacuously — it now evaluates TENANCY, computed from the schema.** The old check matched the literal string `chat_settings_id` in a signature, which answers *"does any method default a parameter of that name to None?"* — not the question F.1 exists to answer. A table with no tenant parameter has nothing to match, so the gate reported green on a table it structurally could not evaluate; `instagram_accounts` was classified tenant-owned in the F.1 inventory (row 5, RF-G2) and sailed straight through, under a green gate, into five live defects (#891, #895, #898, #900, #901). **Two instances of that spelling defect, both mine, both surfaced by building the replacement:** `instagram_accounts` holds no tenant reference at all — the FK runs the other way, `chat_settings.active_instagram_account_id → instagram_accounts.id` — so no spelling could ever have caught it; and `onboarding_sessions` carries its tenancy as `pending_chat_settings_id`, which the old scanner **explicitly skipped**, because I wrote a `pending_` exemption to dodge a false positive and thereby exempted the column that carries the tenancy. The new rule reads the FK **target**, not the column name, so both classify correctly. Tenancy is **computed from the SQLAlchemy metadata** rather than hand-listed (a hand-list is the same spelling problem wearing a different hat, and it goes stale on the next table). **The honest half:** the schema finds the candidates but cannot resolve them — `chat_settings.active_instagram_account_id → instagram_accounts` and `chat_settings.user_id → users` are structurally identical edges while an account is *owned* by one tenant and a user is *shared* across many — so candidates are adjudicated explicitly and an **unadjudicated candidate FAILS** rather than defaulting to safe, since a default-to-global classifier would reproduce this issue's exact vacuous pass one layer up. **Proven on the real tree, not a synthetic string:** planting an unscoped `InstagramAccount` query in `membership_repository.py` (untouched, zero prior findings) fails the new gate **by name** — `{'membership_repository.py': ['planted_leak::InstagramAccount']}` — while the OLD gate passes 4/4 on the identical file. **No over-fire:** `users`, `service_runs` and `user_interactions` classify global, with a paired negative and a not-vacuous control on the classifier itself. **Coverage stated rather than implied:** branden measured that a fixed gate would have protected **2 of the 5**; the other three are an authorization bypass upstream of the repository layer, a presentation surface reading across tenants, and a cross-tenant write whose target was selected elsewhere — none visible to this gate. The sweep over the three repositories #896 named returns **zero**, and the honest reading is *not* "those three are safe" but that this gate's shape does not apply to them. It did find 12 unscoped methods in `InstagramAccountRepository`, pinned by equality so a thirteenth reddens, filed as **#923** rather than fixed here.

- **`## [Unreleased]` was restored to this file.** It was present through `ecccc23` and disappeared at `3f72ccd` (#917), almost certainly eaten by a conflict resolution. Worth more than a tidy-up: every changelog script that anchors on that heading — including the one I used minutes ago — performs a `str.replace` that silently matches nothing and reports success, so an entry is "written" and simply is not there. Mine was, and I only noticed because the commit showed one file instead of two.

### Added

- **L.5 slice 1 (#862): the §4 cap-ledger core — the atomic flip, refund, and the two resolve edges, with ZERO OVER-CAP proven under a genuine race.** `src/services/target/publish_cap.py`: the `approved → publishing` flip as ONE CTE-coupled statement (debit + state flip inseparable, so a zero-row flip can never leave a committed debit — the R2 leak), returning `(debited, flipped)` and resolving it to PROCEED `(1,1)` / DEFERRED `(0,0)` / raise-and-rollback `(1,0)`; `uq_publish_exclusive` (key 4) violation → defer; the cap refund targeting the RECORDED debit day; `resolve_retry` (refund the recorded day, re-enter `approved` debit-neutral with a bumped generation — the next flip re-debits the current day exactly once) and `resolve_cancel` (retains the debit by omission — a `review_required` intent may have published, so refunding risks under-counting toward over-posting). All service-layer raw SQL on the L.1 doctrine (the DB is the authority: the four `post_intents` CHECKs, `ck_dpc_nonneg`, the transition trigger, and key 4 enforce). **Gate** (`tests/scripts/test_publish_cap_gate.py`, 12 real-PG tests): the isolation level asserted (READ COMMITTED); **the R2 zero-over-cap race is genuine** — `asyncio.gather` over N tasks each on its own async connection debiting ONE bucket, exactly the cap succeeds and the ledger never exceeds it; every refusal named (constraint / raised type) with a rowcount positive control; `uq_publish_exclusive` and `ck_dpc_nonneg` each proven load-bearing by dropping them; and pass-5's resolve-retry (refund + re-debit-exactly-once) and resolve-cancel (debit retained). This is 4 of L.5's 6 gate items (the concurrency-critical, product-depends-on-it heart); the checkpointed pipeline executor body (FC-3 transit, container-id-before-call, resume-from-checkpoint, error-9 — items 1/2/6) is slice 2, #915. Runs against stub/sandbox only; nothing reaches a real Meta endpoint.

### Security

- **#900 / #898 / #901: the read/write-asymmetry hardening sweep — one PR, five fixes, every one born-red.** They share a shape that decided the fixes rather than only the tests: **each leaks through a path that PRECEDES OR ACCOMPANIES a correctly-enforced refusal.** The enforcement was never missing; it simply was not the only thing that spoke. That is why reviewing the mutation path alone reads clean, and why the original was found by clicking a control the list had offered. So each fix silences the *other speaker* instead of adding a second refusal. **#900 (the severity outlier — the only WRITE)**: `POST /api/onboarding/add-account` resolved an account by `meta_account_id` **deployment-wide** and then wrote to it, so a caller holding valid Meta credentials for another tenant's account could re-stamp that row to their own chat. Credential possession was mitigated; tenancy was not. Now routed through `_require_account_ownership` — the same derivation Switch/Remove enforce, not a new one — and answering with the **same generic 400 as bad credentials**, so the new gate does not become the existence oracle it closes (pinned by a test that compares both refusals byte-for-byte). **#898**: `/system-status` returned deployment-wide health to any authenticated tenant, including `media_pool`'s category name and item count belonging to whichever tenant owned them — tenant *content* metadata in a stranger's dashboard, one tap from the home screen. Its sibling `/analytics/service-health` sits in the same file already gated with `_validate_admin`, whose docstring states the intent exactly; this endpoint was missed when that gate was applied. **The leak grew today for a benign reason worth recording: #906 fixed the health check so it stops raising and starts returning data, and this endpoint is an ungated consumer of exactly that data — fixing the crash gave the leak more to leak.** Consequence named rather than discovered: the card is now admin-only, so non-admin tenants receive 403 there; a tenant-scoped subset of the checks would keep it and is the better product answer, but that is a product decision and is deliberately not made in a security fix. **#901**: `get_account_by_id_prefix` resolved any tenant's account from 8 hex characters ahead of a mutation door that would correctly refuse — an enumeration oracle, and the reason the Telegram remove-confirm dialog could render a foreign display name and handle; scoping the LOOKUP closes both, and an invented prefix now answers identically to a foreign one. `_validate_new_account`'s error text embedded a foreign `display_name` (error messages are a disclosure surface routinely exempted from review attention); the caller's own id is still echoed, because removing that too would make the message useless without making it safer. `count_active_accounts` was deployment-wide while shaping tenant UI, and now answers the same set the caller's own list shows. **The ownership predicate inside `InstagramAccountRepository` is extracted to one `_ownership_predicate`** so the listing and any future per-account check cannot answer differently — `get_owned`'s own docstring warns that the read and write sides must agree or the list becomes the oracle the mutation door refuses to be. A third spelling was drafted and **deleted**: `_account_owned_by_chat` already answers the single-account question, and adding another was the exact divergence that docstring warns about. **FC-2 ratchet baseline moves by exactly two entries, set-diff reviewed and named** — `count_active_accounts` and `get_account_by_id_prefix` now take a chat id, which grows the F.6 chat-keyed surface. Taking `chat_settings` instead would avoid that and match `list_accounts`, but only 3 of the 7 call sites have it resolved, so the alternative adds a lookup at 4 sites inside a security fix; the two entries retire with the rest of the family at M.3. **Relation to #896, since it is the root cause and is deliberately not fixed here:** a corrected F.1 gate that understood derived ownership would protect **two** of these five — the id-prefix lookup and the count, both repository-shaped. It would **not** protect the other three: #900's gap is an authorization step between a legitimate deployment-wide lookup and a write, #898's endpoint returns no tenant rows for a repository gate to see, and an error string is not a query. Those three need their own assertions regardless of #896.


### Fixed

- **#918: the dashboard no longer renders already-posted items in the future — timestamps leaving the query layer now carry an offset.** Recent Activity showed `posted · in 3h` for a post made three hours *ago*, with the collapsed summary reading `Last post: in 3h`; the status label and the timestamp contradicted each other in the same row. `posting_history.posted_at` and `posting_queue.scheduled_for` are `timestamp without time zone`, and the query layer serialised them with a bare `.isoformat()`, emitting no offset. ECMA-262 parses an offset-less date-time as **local**, so `new Date()` shifted every value by the viewer's UTC offset and `_formatRelativeTime` took its future branch for anything younger than that offset. Six serialisation sites fixed via the existing `ensure_utc` — `posted_at` (history rows), `scheduled_for` ×2 (queue rows), and `last_post_at` ×3 (queue detail and setup state).

  **A second, quieter defect in the same block is fixed with it:** `_check_activity` computed `datetime.now(timezone.utc) - recent_posts[0].posted_at`, aware minus naive, inside a broad `except Exception` that logged at debug level. `last_post_at` was assigned *before* the raise, so the card kept showing a last-post time while **`posting_active` silently stayed `False` for any tenant that had ever posted** — a swallowed exception presenting as data rather than an error. Measured before the fix: a 20-minute-old post yielded `posting_active: False`; after: `True`.

  **Scope is the rendering layer, and the bound is stated rather than implied.** The sweep covered every `new Date(...)` in `app.js` — 8 call sites, of which 5 parse a server value and 3 take no argument — traced each to its serialiser. Five further sites share the shape (`dashboard_media_queries.py:56,65`, `dashboard.py:507,542`, `dashboard_instance_queries.py:49`) but sit on endpoints `app.js` never calls (verified: zero references each), so they are outside this layer and are recorded on #918 rather than changed here. The Telegram surface renders one timestamp (`telegram_commands.py:243`) and is a different display surface, not covered.

  **The `posting_active` half now has regression cover, and the except is a stated decision rather than an inherited one** (review finding on the first pass). Reverting the `setup_state_service.py` fix originally left **all 16 tests in that file green — including `test_posting_active`**, because its mock supplied an already-*aware* `posted_at`: precisely the input that does not trigger the defect. The test named for the behaviour could not detect that behaviour breaking, which is the same class as the bug itself one layer up — the flag was silently False because a broad `except` swallowed the error, and the test was silently green because the fixture sidestepped it. That fixture now carries the **naive** value a `timestamp without time zone` column actually returns, and three further tests cover the block directly. The `except` is split rather than merely widened-with-a-comment: an `SQLAlchemyError` is the *expected* failure at this boundary and keeps its debug-level, degrade-don't-break handling, while anything else now goes through `logger.exception`. **Breadth was never the problem — silence was**; a swallowed programming error presenting as data is what made the original invisible. Both halves are independently born-red: removing the coercion reddens 3 tests, removing the except split reddens the visibility test.

  **Tests pin the process timezone rather than inheriting it**, across UTC, `America/New_York`, `Asia/Tokyo` and `America/Los_Angeles` — inheritance is what let this survive, since on a machine whose zone matches the database session's the two errors cancel and the rendered value looks correct. Reverting the fix turns the **contract** tests red in all four zones, and the **symptom** test red only in the two west-of-UTC zones, which is exactly right: local-parsing pushes a timestamp forward, so only there does a past post land in the future. A viewer in Tokyo saw wrong times but never future ones.

### Added

- **L.8 (#865): webhook ingress and idempotent admission — the same delivery arriving TWICE AT ONCE produces one admission, proven with a genuine race rather than a retry.** Every other Phase L increment controls its own entry; this one does not, so idempotency is the increment. `src/services/target/webhook_ingress.py` admits over `command_dedup` (merged in `056`) with a **single atomic `INSERT … ON CONFLICT`** rather than a SELECT-then-INSERT: a retry arrives after the first attempt finished so any check works, but two simultaneous deliveries both run the check before either writes, which is exactly the case a check-then-act gets wrong. Three outcomes, and the middle one is deliberately **not an absence**: admitted; **replay** (same key, same fingerprint) raised as a NAMED refusal carrying both obligations — acknowledge it, do not execute it — because `rowcount` cannot discriminate a winner from a loser (#883) and a boolean carries neither obligation; and **conflict** (same key, different fingerprint) refused outright, never swallowed as a replay, since a reused idempotency token with new content is a caller bug or an attack and silently dropping it is the failure that is invisible *because* it looks like successful deduplication. Fingerprints normalize (sorted keys), so a provider reordering JSON between deliveries still hashes the same — without that, every redelivery would fail closed, which is correct in direction and an outage in practice. **The race is proven structurally, not asserted:** both coroutines open their own connection, BEGIN, and then block at a two-party rendezvous before touching the table, so a sequentially-executed version cannot get past the first wait — the test fails by HANGING rather than by quietly degrading into a sequential run that still passes (`asyncio.Event` pairs, not `Barrier`, which is 3.11-only while CI is 3.10). **Discrimination measured, and the obvious claim corrected:** mutating `admit()` to check-then-act does NOT double-admit — the primary key stops that regardless of the code — so a test asserting "the naive version admits both" would pass for the wrong reason. What the naive version actually loses is the NAMED refusal: both racers pass their SELECT, and the loser surfaces a raw `UniqueViolationError` at the top of a webhook handler, indistinguishable from a real fault and therefore retried. Both race tests redden under that mutant with exactly that exception. Also verified rather than assumed: an ABORTED winner does not poison the key (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` makes the loser wait on the tuple, and Postgres lets its insert proceed when the winner rolls back), the gate's literal 200 replayed callbacks yield exactly one admission and 199 named replays, and the secret-token check is constant-time and refuses **everything** when unconfigured — the direction matters, since an adapter that accepted everything on misconfiguration would pass a happy-path test and be an open webhook in production. Isolation asserted `read committed` on BOTH the sync path and the asyncpg path the race actually uses, since it is the latter whose level decides whether the race means anything. **#903 is answered rather than assumed:** it does not affect admission correctness — idempotency lives at admission, upstream of any job, so a reaped sender re-claims work whose *effect* is governed by L.4's CAS and cannot turn one admission into two — but it does touch the ack-SLO clause, because a slow worker is exactly when the lease boundary bites. #903 stays open; no composition root exists yet and this increment does not create one.

### Added

- **L.7 (#864): scheduler-as-clock, the dispatcher, and the recurring system jobs — and the explicit answer to whether L.4's argument extends to recurring work.** It does not, and the shape difference is the point. L.4 (#897) established that the outbox needs no guard of its own because `uq_jobs_serialized_lease` admits one live sender and **holding the lease is the proof the predecessor does not** — but that argument is about who *executes*. Recurring work has a second edge a one-shot delivery does not: it must be **scheduled again**, and the lease says nothing about that. `fn_clock_tick`'s recurring leg guards on `IF NOT EXISTS (… state IN ('ready','leased'))`, a **snapshot read**, and the index that rescues `fn_claim_job` does not rescue it — `uq_jobs_serialized_lease` is partial on `state = 'leased'` while the double-insert lands at `state = 'ready'`. **Measured, not argued: two concurrent ticks both insert, and the gate drives that with real connections.** A clock that can double-fire is the same defect class as an outbox that can double-send, so the protection is a *different* mechanism — **advisory-lock election**, which makes the concurrency impossible rather than refusing a loser. `plan_slot` is the one edge that also carries an L.4-shaped guard, and the contrast is instructive: its idempotency lives on the **effect** rather than the job (`uq_intent_slot`, key 1), so a duplicate `plan_slot` execution mints no second intent. The election protects the scheduling edge, key 1 protects the minting edge, and neither covers the other. **Clock skew is decided rather than omitted:** every scheduling decision reads the DATABASE clock — `fn_clock_tick` takes no timestamp parameter, asserted in the gate against `pg_get_function_arguments` — so a host whose wall clock jumps after a reboot changes nothing about what is due, which matters on an RTC-less Pi with real skew history; the loop's own pacing is `asyncio.sleep`, monotonic by construction. Database-clock skew is a deployment property no application code can see from inside and stays with S. `src/services/target/scheduler.py` ships `ClockElection` (session-scoped `pg_try_advisory_lock` — try rather than block so a loser never queues, session rather than transaction so a SIGKILLed clock's election dies with its connection), `tick` over the `059` door, `execute_plan_slot`, `execute_reap_expired`, `execute_reap_transit_assets` and the `Clock` loop. Losing the election deliberately does **not** move `consecutive_failures` — on a three-replica deployment two processes lose every interval, and a supervisor restarting them for it would be the defect, the same rule L.4's poller applies to a paced tick. **The gate is 15 real-Postgres tests** holding the house standard: isolation asserted, key 1 refusing **by name** on `uq_intent_slot` with a rowcount positive control, and every guard proven load-bearing by removal. **Three findings the mutation battery produced against my own claims rather than review**: the wrapper's insert budget was untested because the storm test drove the raw door (a wrapper parameter nothing exercises is a parameter in name only — now driven through `tick()` too); the first kill test disposed an engine instead of killing a backend, which is a graceful close the pool may defer and proves nothing about SIGKILL (now `pg_terminate_backend`, issued as the same role because the owner lacks `pg_signal_backend`); and the EXPLAIN clauses are scoped to what a gate-sized table can show — that the predicate is index-servable rather than that the planner picks a named index, after the reap leg picked `uq_intent_live_subject` and demonstrated exactly why the stronger claim would have been asserting a fixture artifact. Production plan choice is an S.1 measurement and is not claimed.

- **L.6 (#863): Instagram Login OAuth, target-side — a PORT of the proven flow onto `oauth_states` and `oauth_credentials`, with every divergence named.** A working flow already runs in production (PRs #341/#378, a real account connected), so `src/services/target/ig_login_oauth.py` moves it rather than re-deriving it, and the module docstring lists where the target differs **on purpose**: state lives in a row consumed by a one-shot CAS rather than a signed self-describing token (replay protection has to be a fact about storage — a stateless token cannot be single-use); the TTL is `05`'s **900s** seam, not legacy's 600; the credential is a row under the MultiFernet ring `main` already ships under `ENCRYPTION_KEYS` (`07` §3 keeps the shipped env name deliberately); and **refresh drops the host branch**. That last one carries the legacy lesson rather than discarding it: `token_refresh.py` had to branch because `graph.instagram.com` takes `grant_type=ig_refresh_token` + the token alone while the FB host needs `fb_exchange_token` + client id/secret, and sending IG params to the FB host produced Meta error 101. Under FC-7 that host is unreachable — `ck_credentials_provider` ships with **no** `fb_login_legacy` value, verified against the shipped constraint rather than taken from the issue — so the branch is structurally unnecessary, not forgotten. Reconnect is **"last issued wins", enforced in the ISSUE transaction**, because `07` §2 records that the pass-2 "last consumed wins" claim was false: independently issued rows never consumed one another, so both callbacks could land. `reap_expired` gains the `oauth_states` class, bounded, reaping expired **and** consumed rows while leaving live ones. **A real bug was caught by a test asserting the consequence rather than the exception:** the fail-closed path flips the credential `expired` and the account `reauth_required`, but the flip originally rode on the caller's transaction and was rolled back with the raise — leaving the credential `active` while nothing could read it. It now commits before raising; mutation-proven, with the mutant verified live first. 21 tests, all against a real database: isolation asserted `read committed`; replay, expiry, unknown-state and cross-workspace each refused **by name** and each with a *different* reason, since an operator reading a log needs to tell a re-submission from a slow user from a break-in; a rowcount positive control before any refusal; a guard-drop proving `ck_oauth_state_context` is load-bearing; ciphertext asserted absent from both the column-adjacent assertions and the exception text (`07` §§3,5). **Two clauses of the gate are explicitly NOT closed here and are labelled rather than implied:** "a fresh Professional account connects with zero Facebook surface" is unprovable for an arbitrary account until #410 (Meta App Review) passes — an external gate with a human owner — and D31's other two halves, the dispatcher minting no further intents and the publish pipeline recording the fault, are L.7's and L.5's.

### Fixed

- **#909: `get_recent_posts` compared an aware cutoff against a naive column and returned SILENTLY WRONG results — and it was fifteen sites, not one.** `PostingHistory.posted_at` is a naive `DateTime`; comparing it to `datetime.now(timezone.utc) - timedelta(...)` makes Postgres cast the **parameter** using the session's `TimeZone`, so the window shifts by that offset. Right on a UTC connection, wrong on any other, with a believable number either way — the reason this outranked the two louder instances in its family (#906), which raised `TypeError` and reported themselves. **The reported instance was one of fifteen**, found by an AST sweep over every `Compare` whose one side is a `Column(DateTime)` attribute: 14 in `history_repository.py` and 1 in `media_repository.py` (`count_dead_content_by_category`) — a different file, which is the fourth instance the sweep was asked to look for. Four more sites took the cutoff as a **caller-supplied parameter**, so awareness was the caller's; three are now coerced at the method boundary (`since = naive_utc(since)`), which is a fix no caller can undo, and the fourth turned out to be a local that was already naive. **`ensure_utc` does not fit here and a second convention was not introduced:** `ensure_utc` makes a naive value aware and passes an already-aware one straight through, so applying it to an aware `since` is a **no-op that leaves the defect in place** — the column is the naive side, and it is not the side Python can coerce. The mirror `naive_utc()` lands beside it in the same module as the other boundary of the same "stored values are UTC" convention, and it converts to UTC **before** dropping tzinfo, because dropping it from a non-UTC offset without converting is the same silent shift one layer down. **Proven session-timezone-dependent both ways, with the timezone PINNED rather than inherited** — a test that takes the server default passes on CI's `postgres:15` (`Etc/UTC`) and would have gone on passing while the bug shipped. Each case sets `TimeZone` explicitly, asserts it took, and runs on the repository's own session (its `db` is a read-only property, so a separately-created session would be pinned while the query ran elsewhere). Measured with the fix reverted: a 90-minute-old row matches a **1-hour** window under `America/New_York` and does not under `UTC`. A third test isolates the mechanism — an aware parameter's cast shifts with the session by ≥1h while a naive one does not — so a future reader need not re-derive that it is the parameter Postgres casts, not the column. **A standing static gate ships with the fix** (`test_naive_column_comparison_gate.py`): it re-runs the sweep and fails on a sixteenth instance, because the defect changes no behaviour on a UTC session and a new one would otherwise ship green through every existing test. It carries its own positive controls (the analyser must still find ≥30 naive columns and examine ≥30 comparison sites, or a models refactor would make it pass vacuously), discloses `UNKNOWN` caller-supplied sites rather than failing them, and pins that count so a new one gets looked at. Mutation-verified in both directions: reverting one of the fifteen reddens the gate, and reverting `naive_utc` to a pass-through reddens the timezone test on `America/New_York` alone. Two defects in this change's own work were caught by its own tooling — `ruff` found an automated coercion inserted before the local it referenced, and that misfire exposed an analyser gap (`.replace(tzinfo=None)` was not recognised as naive) which is now closed.


### Fixed

- **OUTAGE RISK #907: `track_execution` no longer parks a connection idle-in-transaction for the full duration of every tracked call.** `BaseService.track_execution` wraps every tracked service call app-wide, and its first act — `ServiceRunRepository.create_run` — did `add → commit → refresh`; the `refresh()` SELECT opened a read transaction nothing ended, so the connection sat **idle-in-transaction** from `create_run` until `complete_run`'s commit — the whole call. Survivable while the pool could grow; an outage risk the moment L.0 pinned `max_overflow=0` + `pool_timeout=3.0` (zero burst room, callers fail after 3s once the pool saturates). The constants are right; the leak is what made them dangerous. Fixed with a `BaseRepository.commit_and_refresh(obj)` primitive (commit, refresh the persisted row's server-set fields, END the read transaction the refresh opens) applied at `create_run` — the single highest-value site, on the universal path. Proven the way it was diagnosed: a `pg_stat_activity` test asserts **zero idle-in-transaction backends attributable to a tracked call**, born-red against main (backend state, not a call-count), plus a mid-call assertion for the full-duration window. The ~30 other `commit(); refresh()` sites carry the same leak and follow as a per-site-verified sweep (#908) — not blanket-transformed here because at least one is a refresh-after-rollback variant `commit_and_refresh` would break. Not a regression: `service_run_repository.py` is byte-identical across #853; this predates today.
- **SECURITY #895: the Telegram `sap:`/`btp:` short-prefix callbacks are tenant-scoped — an auth-bypass that disclosed foreign tenant POST CONTENT, live on a surface with real users.** These callbacks carry an 8-char queue-id PREFIX, not a full UUID; the callback ownership gate resolves with exact-UUID `get_by_id`, so a prefix returned `None`, `row is not None` was False, and — worse — the actions were absent from `_QUEUE_ID_ACTIONS` entirely, so neither membership nor ownership ran. The handler then read the prefix cross-tenant with `SYSTEM_SCOPE` and rebuilt another tenant's media caption into the caller's chat. Found in the #891 audit; ari ranked it ahead of #891 because unlike that dashboard (never clickable until this week) the Telegram keyboards were never CSP-blocked. Fixed with the #891 lesson — ONE ownership rule, two consumers: `telegram_utils.caller_may_act_on_queue_row` (owned-OR-NULL, so legacy single-tenant cards still work) is applied by the gate for full-UUID actions AND by the `sap:`/`btp:` handlers for their prefix, scoped to the caller's tenant at the door that reads the content; `sap`/`btp` join `_QUEUE_ID_ACTIONS` for membership. Born-red at the callback door with two committed tenants (the unrelated tenant's tap rebuilds nothing, shows neutral "not found"), an agreement invariant pinning the handler's ruling to the gate's rule (owner / foreign / legacy-NULL / absent), and the non-member-refused case; reverted → all red naming the disclosure → restored. The remaining audit siblings (#891 items 2–5) are the separate hardening sweep; the root-cause gate defect is #896.

- **#899: the queue and media-sync health checks no longer raise `TypeError` on a naive timestamp — the dashboard stops rendering a raw Python exception at it.** `_check_queue` computed `datetime.now(timezone.utc) - oldest_pending.created_at`, and `PostingQueue.created_at` defaults to `datetime.utcnow` (naive) on the app's own write path (`queue_repository.create`), so aware-minus-naive raised. **Broken across exactly the band its own constant calls normal:** `_check_queue` returns early above `QUEUE_BACKLOG_THRESHOLD`, and skips the age branch entirely at zero, so the crash occupied the **1–10** range — the range documented as *"JIT queue is 0-5 items; 10+ indicates a problem."* Measured all three states before the fix: 0 pending healthy, 3 pending `TypeError`, 62 pending correct backlog verdict. The failure is caught, so it degraded rather than 500'd, which is why it went unnoticed — the card simply showed `❌ Queue — Queue check error: can't subtract offset-naive and offset-aware datetimes`. **The same mismatch was present a second time in the same file** and is fixed with it: `_check_media_sync` parses `ServiceRun.completed_at` (also a naive column) back through `datetime.fromisoformat` after an `.isoformat()` round-trip and subtracted that from an aware now — confirmed live by driving the real method, which returned `Sync check error: can't subtract offset-naive and offset-aware datetimes`. Both sites now use the existing `ensure_utc` helper, which was written for precisely this convention (naive columns storing UTC) and passes aware values through untouched.

  **Why the existing tests could not see it, which is the durable half.** `test_check_queue_healthy` set `get_oldest_pending` to `None`, so the age branch never ran; `test_check_queue_stale_items` built its item with `datetime.now(timezone.utc) - timedelta(hours=8)` — an **aware** value, the opposite tz-ness from what the database actually stores. **The mock was more correct than production, so the branch stayed green on a path that raised for every real caller.** The new coverage therefore builds a real `PostingQueue` and lets the model's own default supply the timestamp (asserting it is naive, so an aware fixture can never quietly re-hide this), and drives `_check_media_sync` itself rather than calling `ensure_utc` directly — a test that called the helper would pass with or without the fix. Parameterised across the whole 0 → past-threshold range, because the defect lived only in the band between the two ends. Reverting the fix alone turns all **8** new tests red with the field's exact `TypeError`; the three boundary controls (0, 11, 62) stay green in **both** states, which is what pins the defect as band-limited rather than universal.
- **SECURITY #891: the accounts list door is tenant-scoped — no longer the existence oracle the mutation door refuses to be.** `GET /api/onboarding/accounts`, the Telegram accounts display, and `auto_select_account_if_single` listed **every active Instagram account on the deployment** (names, handles, UUIDs): `list_accounts()` → `get_all_active()` carried no tenant filter while Switch/Remove refused non-owners — isolation enforced on writes, disclosed on reads; the unscoped list also fed a write (a new tenant on a one-account deployment auto-selected the FOREIGN account as its active pointer). The list now uses `InstagramAccountRepository.get_owned` — the SAME derivation `_require_account_ownership` enforces (active pointer | chat-stamped `api_tokens` | unstamped-legacy for the env chat only), pushed down as a set-returning predicate because `instagram_accounts` has no tenant column; the deployment-wide read survives only as `list_all_accounts_unscoped()` (operator CLI). Born-red at the door with real tenants + an agreement test pinning list-membership to the mutation door's ruling. How it survived F.1 (cited): the table was classified tenant-owned but F.1's gate keys on the `chat_settings_id` parameter spelling and this tenancy lives in a JOIN — passed vacuously. Wider read/write-asymmetry siblings inventoried on #891, not batch-fixed here.
- **#867: the implicit `ADMIN_TELEGRAM_CHAT_ID` fallbacks are retired — an absent chat is now a caller bug, never a grant of the admin tenant.** F.1 made the fail-open `if chat_settings_id:` pattern extinct at the repository layer; an implicit ADMIN default is that same shape wearing a default, and it is worse in one respect — it **grants privilege on missing input silently**, with no branch for a reader to notice. All 8 sites are converted: the chat parameter is REQUIRED, and the one operator edge that relied on the default (`storydump-cli backfill-instagram`) names `settings.ADMIN_TELEGRAM_CHAT_ID` at its own call site and gains a `--chat-id` flag, so the grant is visible in a diff and overridable instead of implied inside the service. **The count is 8, not the 9 recorded during the #866 review, and not the 8 the issue body lists — measured, with the predicate stated:** an assignment, inside a function declaring a chat-shaped parameter, whose value is or falls back to `settings.ADMIN_TELEGRAM_CHAT_ID`. The same predicate returns the identical 8 at #866's merge commit and at today's `main`, so nothing moved in between; the set is `posting.py::send_gdrive_auth_alert`, `instagram_api.py::{post_story,get_content_publishing_limit}`, `instagram_backfill.py::backfill`, and `instagram_credentials.py::{is_configured,validate_instagram_account_id,get_account_info,safety_check_before_post}`. **Three sites carried no in-code justification, not the two previously identified** — the third is `posting.py::send_gdrive_auth_alert`, the only one outside the instagram tier, whose docstring documented its gating, its state and its bot and never mentioned that an absent id redirected a tenant's Google Drive reconnect alert to the admin chat. **Exactly one fallback branch was reachable from a live caller** (`backfill`, via a CLI command that passed no chat id); the other seven are reached either not at all — `is_configured` and `validate_instagram_account_id` are called only through `instagram_api` wrappers that nothing in `src/` or `cli/` calls — or only by callers that already pass an explicit id, which includes every inbound Telegram path (`telegram_autopost.py` reaches three of these functions and always names `ctx.chat_id`). So no production behaviour changes; the branches are gone so they cannot start mattering. A structural gate, `tests/scripts/test_no_implicit_admin_fallback.py`, now fails on reintroduction — the hazard is a shape, and a new function written next week with the same default would pass every behavioural test in the suite and be caught only there. It matches four shapes — assignment, the `or` form, the **ternary**, and a **parameter default** — and the last two were added on review, which is the point of the gate rather than a footnote to it: **the ternary is exactly the idiom this PR's own CLI fix uses**, so the mechanism written to stop this class recurring had a blind spot shaped precisely like the code shipped beside it, and the next person to write one would most plausibly copy that idiom. Both added shapes are proven by planting the hazard in a real service module and watching the gate fire — the parameter-default run passed on the first attempt because the mutant never landed, so the mutant is verified live before the result is read. Widening to the ternary also makes the gate match the legitimate operator edge, and it should: structurally that line **is** the hazard and what makes it safe is context no parser can see. So the context is declared rather than guessed — a site opts out with `# admin-grant-ok:` and a reason on the same line, making `grep` the standing inventory of every deliberate admin grant, the move F.1 made with `SYSTEM_SCOPE`. The allowlist is asserted by **equality** (F.6 ratchet's rule), so a second sanctioned grant arrives as a visible line in a diff. Positive controls throughout, including a paired negative for the marker and one asserting a marker on the wrong line does not suppress. **The F.6 ratchet baseline moves 114 → 116 in this diff and both entries are deliberate**, caught by the gate rather than by review: `instagram_api.py::validate_instagram_account_id` gains the tenant parameter it should always have had, and `cli/commands/backfill.py::backfill_instagram` gains `--chat-id` — a new chat-keyed *operator edge*, which is the sanctioned shape the F.3 door family entered the set as. Equality-not-ceiling is what forced both into a reviewable line instead of absorbing them as headroom.

### Added

- **L.4 (#861): the channel outbox and its sender — and the answer to the hazard this increment was assigned for.** `channel_outbox` carries no unique index and no lease token (#886, from DDL inspection; the #887 ruling was explicit that the argument making `jobs` safe does **not** transfer, because `jobs` has both and this table has neither). An outbox exists to make a send happen exactly once, so #883's failure — two writers both told they won — reads here as the machine believing it sent something it did not, or sending twice while believing it sent once. **It needs no guard of its own, and the reason is a mechanism rather than the table's class:** exactly-once rests on two database-enforced facts, neither on `channel_outbox` — (1) the sender is the `deliver_outbox` job keyed `tg:<binding_id>`, so `uq_jobs_serialized_lease` admits ONE live sender per chat writer-independently, the same partial unique index that made `jobs` safe, reused rather than re-derived; (2) every write OUT of `sending` is a CAS on the state it leaves, so a loser matches zero rows — a predicate miss on a column the winner changed, the discrimination #883's `WHERE id = :id` could not make. Fact 1 removes concurrent senders; fact 2 fences the case fact 1 cannot see, a partitioned predecessor whose lease expired but whose process still runs. **Two claims were corrected by the mutation battery rather than by review**, and both were mine: entering `sending` is single-winner by `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, not by the outer `AND state = 'pending'` — removing that clause leaves the gate green because a committed row fails the subquery's own filter and an uncommitted one is locked, so it is kept as labelled defense-in-depth rather than credited as the guard; and pacing-before-claim is a preference, not a guard, because a paced call raises before committing and the caller's rollback un-claims anything it took. **No migration: adding a send token would be a second lease token for one delivery**, and `02` §6 gives the job execution state and this table the delivery record. `src/services/target/outbox.py` ships `enqueue`, `claim_next`, `mark_sent`/`mark_ambiguous` through one `_leave_sending` CAS (one spelling, so the fence cannot be present on some edges and absent on others), `recover_stranded`, `resolve_ambiguous`, `supersede_all`, `deliver` and `OutboxPoller`. **Stranded rows are recovered by whoever holds the binding's lease** — there is no reaper leg for the outbox and it needs none, because holding the lease is itself the proof the previous holder does not. Recovery never blind-retries: it goes through `ambiguous`, since whether the send left is exactly what nobody knows (R8 — Telegram has no read-back for a lost `sendMessage`). The `02` §6 per-kind policy is implemented and bounded: `notification`/`ack` retry **exactly once** then fail, `approval_prompt`/`invitation` resend at any attempt count, and `supersede_all` retires every live card while queueing one `prompt_supersede` per **known** ref — a lost ref is still retired and ages out under R6. Pacing consumes both `02` §6 scopes (`tg_chat` on the binding, `tg_global` on `''`) in the same transaction as the send they admit, and over budget is a **defer** that leaves the row `pending`, not a failure — H5 slip-a-slot. `OutboxPoller` is the `run_at` polling loop that replaces the struck Redis wake-up (TT:P0-07), at the `05` 2 s cadence with every number a parameter; a paced tick deliberately does not move `consecutive_failures`, because a chat at its budget is working and a supervisor restarting the sender for it would be the defect. **The gate is 15 real-Postgres tests as the real `svc_worker` login**, holding the house standard: the isolation level is asserted rather than assumed (19 of L.2's tests still passed at REPEATABLE READ, so a concurrency test at the wrong level proves nothing); the second sender is refused **by name** on `uq_jobs_serialized_lease` with the loser asserted to have blocked mid-flight; every refusal carries a rowcount positive control; and each guard is proven load-bearing by removal — dropping the index makes both senders lease, and removing either CAS clause makes the same statement land. Two things the writing measured rather than assumed: the shipped increment-and-check idiom does **not** deny at `limit=0` (its `WHERE rc.count < :limit` guards only the ON CONFLICT branch, and the first hit of a window is an INSERT), so the pacing test spends the budget with a real send; and `async with` resolves `__aenter__` on the **type**, so the poller's first test factory patched an instance attribute that was silently ignored — the loop drained nothing while every assertion still ran.

- **L.3 (#860): the provider-operations permit rail and the `reconcile_ambiguous` executor — `ig` only, and at-most-once survives because a call is authorized exactly once, not because a stale call is physically prevented.** `src/services/target/provider_ops.py` implements `02` §6 verbatim: the permit insert and a strengthened lease CAS (`state = 'leased' AND locked_until > now()`, `FOR SHARE`, not the pass-2 id+token form that passed a lease expired without reassignment) in ONE transaction, the provider call only after it commits, and `publish` advancing `publish_step` in the same transaction so `ck_ambiguous_called` is exact. Resume DIVERGES by blast radius: `container_create` marks the op `failed`/`lost_response`, bumps `generation` and permits a fresh create — an orphaned container is inert, so a recoverable effect must never park the pipeline — while `publish` **never re-calls**, marking the op `ambiguous` and the intent `publishing_ambiguous`, with `generation` never bumping out of ambiguity, which is the structural half of at-most-once (a new generation would mint a new business key and make a second call permittable at all). `src/services/target/reconciler.py` ships the executor with `05`'s ladder (60s → 5m → 30m → 2h, capped at container expiry; asserted at 10–25 polls against ~1,440 at a flat poll) and both evidence modes, because the gate requires a test per mode: container-verdict terminalizes from the authoritative value, evidence-capture proves the machine parks `review_required` with the captured trail on the **same** `PUBLISHED` value instead of self-terminalizing. `FINISHED` is non-terminal in both and has its own test — after `publish_called` it means *ready to be published*, so classifying it authoritative-negative would terminalize a still-live publish as a definite non-event. Every verdict also terminalizes the `provider_operations` row it judged, or `ix_ops_retire` never drains. **The `provider_operations` transition-guard question was decided by measurement, not by inheriting L.2's `jobs` argument:** the table carries zero non-internal triggers (measured on the replayed schema, not read off the DDL), and two concurrent resolvers targeting the *same* destination state at asserted `read committed` return rowcounts 0 and 1 — so a CAS discriminates and #883 does not reproduce, because #883 was a trigger keyed on *did the value change* (which skips a same-state write) while a CAS keyed on *is the value still `permitted`* matches nothing once the row has moved. The residual difference is stated rather than smoothed over: `jobs`' `ready → leased` is a unique index, so a writer who forgets the guard still hits it, whereas here a forgetful writer gets `rowcount = 1` and no error — equivalent in effect, not in robustness. No trigger is added (Phase L is the service layer over Phase F's schema); instead the predicate is made structurally unavoidable, with one function owning every state advance. Two things the build itself found: `ck_posted_complete` refused the reconciler's first `posted` verdict because the `api` route demands `ig_container_id` **and** `publish_step = 'effect_confirmed'` — a bare state flip would have left a posted row that cannot say what it posted, and the constraint gets the credit; and the permit insert is deliberately written **without** `ON CONFLICT`, because that clause resolves its constraint at parse time and would have made the guard-drop test — which drops `uq_ops_business_key` to prove it is load-bearing — impossible to run at all. 43 tests: 26 on the evidence contract, 17 against a real database with the isolation level asserted, a rowcount positive control before every refusal, and named refusals throughout, since rowcount cannot discriminate a winner from a loser.

### Added

- **L.2 (#859): the jobs service — claim, heartbeat, and lease-token CAS finalization over the merged doors, plus the one durable counter idiom.** The schema and both `SECURITY DEFINER` doors landed in Phase F (`056`/`059`); this is the service layer and the gate proofs, on the L.1 doctrine that the database is the authority. `src/services/target/jobs.py`: `claim_job` wraps `fn_claim_job`, commits the lease (an uncommitted lease exists to nobody), and converts a lost serialization race into a clean rolled-back retry — the retry needs no bookkeeping because the winner's committed lease trips the claim query's own `NOT EXISTS`; `finalize_job` is the `02` §5 CAS plus the state predicate that keeping the token requires (without it a double-finalize would match the terminal row and report `rows=1` — the #883 phantom-win one table over), raising typed `JobFenced` inside the CALLER's transaction; `LeaseHeartbeat` is the one independent asyncio beat task per worker (`fn_extend_leases`, own connection per beat, short-count tripwire, `consecutive_failures` as the missed-two-beats observable; escalation/liveness/pool shape stated as composition-root concerns). `src/services/target/rate_counters.py`: the `02` §6 increment-and-check UPSERT, written once — the `WHERE rc.count < :limit` IS the guard, deliberately service-side and compared against the CURRENT limit (`04`'s "frozen limit" phrasing contradicts `02` §6's "deliberately NOT frozen"; `02` is the domain authority, flagged on #859). **The gate battery** (`tests/scripts/test_jobs_lease_gate.py`, 21 real-PG tests as the real `svc_worker` login, every #883 lesson applied): the isolation level itself is pinned (READ COMMITTED); the serialization race is driven DETERMINISTICALLY through the #883 window — the loser blocks on the winner's uncommitted index entry, is REFUSED by `uq_jobs_serialized_lease` by name at commit, then retries clean; kill/expiry/resume proves one live owner throughout and the fence both directions including double-finalize; the pass-3 invariants each carry a rowcount positive control AND the drop-the-guard proof; the counter denies at the limit under six real concurrent incrementers, its drop-proof running the same statement without its WHERE. Verified per the #883 follow-on rather than assumed: `jobs` has NO transition guard — the gate pins that absence as a tripwire; the safety is carried per-edge by mechanism (a writer-independent unique index entering `leased`; a CAS that can only mislead a stale owner about its own staleness leaving it) and deliberately does NOT extend to tables lacking both, per the #887 ruling. Every `05` number arrives as a parameter.

- **#878: the Schedule card expands, and shows the upcoming slots that `/analytics/schedule-preview` has been computing for nobody.** Schedule was the only non-expandable card on the dashboard and schedule-preview was a working, tenant-scoped, tested endpoint with zero callers — the two halves of one feature that shipped apart. Deliberately **1 of the 10** orphaned analytics endpoints: schedule preview is the only one that is not degenerate at low volume, since it says something useful with a single post where the rest need a content corpus first, and building analytics UI against unmeasured production volume is how you ship something that looks broken. Slots reuse the queue card's row shape rather than introducing a second list idiom — both cards answer "what goes out next", so absolute time left, relative time right, category as the secondary line, and an imminent slot reads "in 2h" exactly as a queued item does. Times render in **UTC** because the card's own summary line one row above states UTC and a viewer-local rendering would contradict the line directly above it. A cached preview is dropped when cadence, posting window or pause state changes — all three are inputs to the slot computation — hooked only into the two paths that leave home rendered, since the Edit path already resets card state via `_populateHome`. **Not verifiable by clicking, and this is disclosed rather than discovered later: #879 (CSP blocks all 55 inline `onclick` handlers) makes every card on the dashboard unclickable, pre-existing and repo-wide;** this feature is correct and inert until that is resolved, and was verified by invoking the function the blocked handler would have called.
