- **#878: `service-health` is wired to the CLI — the detector we already had, built and unplugged, now runnable by a human or a cron.** Mason's product read established that the ten unreferenced analytics endpoints are the closed backend children of three still-open compound plays (#150/#187/#188), and recommended wiring two; this is the first, and it goes to the CLI rather than the dashboard because there is no admin surface in the dashboard at all. **The value claim was measured before the wiring, not after:** the justification was that this view would have caught the Sync Media Now outage, so both arms were run against a live database — the TRUE pre-fix `media_sync.py` (checked out from `f6ff75c^`), called in the routes' exact argument shape, raises `TenantContextError` at `_build_db_lookups` *inside* the `track_execution` block and reports `MediaSyncService … error_rate=1.00`, against `0.00` on fixed main and a `total_calls=0` control before each. It would have been a flat 1.00, not a subtle drift. **It also cleared by about four lines** — the rebind that caused the bug sits above the `with`, and a raise there writes nothing — which is a fact about that bug, not a property of the instrument, and is why the three limits are stated in the shipped module docstring rather than only in review: only work inside `BaseService.track_execution` is visible (verified as the sole `create_run` caller, with the scheduler's `record_run` periodic marker excluded from the aggregate by name); **a run killed mid-flight stays `running`, still counts as a call, and therefore pulls the error rate DOWN** — measured, one honest failure reads `1.00` and a single stuck run alongside it takes the same service to `0.50`, so a hard crash makes a service look healthier; and a service with zero calls does not appear at all, so silence is not health. The second gets its own derived `Unresolved` column (`call_count - success_count - failure_count` — no endpoint change, the aggregate already returns every term) and the third an explicit "absence of telemetry, not a clean bill of health" message. `--fail-over RATE` is the **only** path to a non-zero exit, which is what separates a report from a detector; it has no default on purpose, because a guessed threshold either pages on noise or stays silent through an outage. `--json` keeps the payload parseable (the breach banner is suppressed there) and `--hours` passes through. Endpoint, repository and query are untouched. Two defects were caught in the tests rather than shipped: the integration pair was order-dependent (`route_repos_to_test_db` rebinds the session factory but does not roll back, and `--fail-over` aggregates globally — measured, the healthy control read `0.75` and exited 1 when it ran second) and now truncates `service_runs` per test, verified green in both orders; and a paired negative would have been vacuous because rich hard-wraps at terminal width, so it passed whether the text was absent **or merely split across lines** — both matchers now collapse whitespace.

### Fixed

- **#883: the loser of a concurrent intent transition is now REFUSED instead of being told it won.** `trg_intent_guard` compares only under `NEW.state IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.state`, so a same-state write skipped every check and succeeded as a no-op. That is inert in the ledger — no audit row, `entered_state_at` unmoved — and **not** inert to the caller. Measured on the shipped trigger against live Postgres: two writers take the same edge on one intent; the loser blocks on the row lock, then under READ COMMITTED re-evaluates against the winner's committed row and is told it succeeded with `rowcount = 1`, identical to the winner. Two callers believe they transitioned; one did, and the audit ledger correctly shows one row. **`rowcount` cannot separate them**, so no service-side check recovers the distinction, and `transition()` raising or not is the caller's only success signal. Migration `061` adds a second trigger rather than editing the first, because a `BEFORE UPDATE FOR EACH ROW` trigger cannot tell "SET state to the same value" from "did not touch state" — both present as `NEW.state = OLD.state`, which is exactly why the existing guard is written with `IS DISTINCT FROM`. `BEFORE UPDATE OF state` fires on the column being **named in the SET list** whatever its value; checkpoint updates never name `state` and so never fire it (asserted in both directions). Firing order is load-bearing and named in the file: Postgres fires `BEFORE` row triggers in name order and `tg_intent_guard` sorts first, so a terminal row still reports terminal immutability rather than this rule. **The regression test is the deliverable as much as the trigger** — `test_the_LOSER_of_a_concurrent_transition_is_REFUSED_not_told_it_won` drives two real connections, asserts the loser genuinely blocked (a race that never contended proves nothing), asserts the session is READ COMMITTED (at a higher level the loser already gets a serialization error and the trigger is never reached), and was **born red** against the pre-`061` trigger with the defect verbatim: `the LOSER of a concurrent transition was told it succeeded (rowcount=1)`. The #875 test that asserted the old no-op behaviour is rewritten to the property that now holds, keeping its ledger-integrity assertions. **Raising the transaction isolation level would also close this and is deliberately not done here** — it is a fleet-wide change to every transaction L.0 opens, with its own retry semantics, and wants its own decision. **Scope stated rather than implied:** this closes it for `post_intents.state`, the only column in the target lineage carrying a transition-legality guard; `jobs.state`, `channel_outbox.state` and `provider_operations.state` carry value-set CHECKs and no transition guard at all, so they have no version of this defect and no protection against the same caller-facing outcome either — `jobs.state -> 'leased'` is a lease claim and belongs to L.2's claim protocol.

- **`/analytics/schedule-preview` emitted a timestamp no browser could parse.** `slot_time` was `next_time.isoformat() + "Z"` on a timezone-aware datetime, so it carried an offset **and** a `Z` (`2026-08-19T14:45:24.488783+00:00Z`) and evaluated to `Invalid Date` in every browser — unparseable by the only kind of consumer the endpoint has. The same file already used plain `.isoformat()` for `posted_at` 290 lines above, and this was the single instance of the `+ "Z"` form in `src/`, so it was a local slip rather than a convention. Fixed at the source rather than shimmed in the frontend, and free to fix precisely because the endpoint had **zero consumers** at the time — the wiring above adds the first.

### Fixed

- **#868: the F.6 ratchet's adapter exemption read a module's IMPORTS instead of its NAME, and had been under-counting the chat-id axis by 25 functions. `chat_id_functions_outside_adapters` 89 → 114 is the fix working, not a regression — do not correct it back down.** `is_telegram_coupled()` exempted a module if it (a) imported the `telegram` SDK or (b) imported anything whose dotted path contained "telegram". Both are facts about what a module happens to import for any reason at all — catching an SDK exception type, making an outbound `Bot` call, pulling in a shared UI constant — and none of those is adapter-hood. Because an exempted module's chat-id functions leave the counted set with it, a loose predicate does not merely mislabel a module, **it blinds the axis**: the gate ran, passed, and published a number about a set 25 functions narrower than the one it named. Every reading anyone took from "89" — including the F.3 conversion that moved the baseline — covered less than it appeared to. Nothing in F.3 is wrong because of this (the 25 predate it and its own conversions are independently verified); the ratchet's headline simply understated reality the whole time. The predicate is now a fact about the module's own declared name — stem exactly `telegram` or starting `telegram_` — and takes no `tree` parameter, so there is no longer an argument through which an import could be consulted again. Stem rather than raw path-substring also closes a collision class preemptively (`nontelegram_helper.py` matched the old form); the two agree at 16 on today's tree, so that tightening is stated as preemptive rather than evidenced by the count. **Eleven modules were wrongly exempt, not the two named when the issue was opened**, each traced individually: `scheduler.py` (4 functions revealed), `google_drive_oauth.py` (8), `oauth_service.py` (4), `instagram_login_oauth.py` (4), `health_check.py` (2), `loops/scheduler_loop.py` (1), `posting.py` (1), `start_command_router.py` (1), plus `main.py`, `queue_reap.py` and `resilience.py` which are inert (no chat-id-shaped functions of their own). `start_command_router.py` is the one that could plausibly have been a real adapter and was checked rather than assumed — `telegram_commands.py` is what is registered against the dispatcher and delegates to it, so it is one hop downstream of the edge, and its `_handle_group_start` calls F.3's sanctioned `provision()` door; its new visibility in the tracked set is the gate working, not a false positive to re-exempt. All four axes move and all four are disclosed: `telegram_modules` 27 → 16, `core_telegram_modules` 22 → 15, `chat_id_functions_outside_adapters` 89 → 114, `provider_account_ref_log_sites` 0 → 0. **No function left the axis** — the change is pure addition, so no coverage was traded for the count. All 16 surviving adapter modules were verified genuine `telegram_*`-named modules. The now-orphaned `imported_names()` helper is deleted rather than left as a loaded gun beside a predicate whose whole point is that imports do not decide adapter-hood. The module docstring is rewritten in the same commit (it is stored in the baseline JSON as `predicate` and drift-tested), including two corrections it needed on its own account: its illustrative "a substring predicate misses `telegram_operation_state.py`" claim was **false when written** — the word is in that file's line-1 docstring, and was at install — and its "45 functions inside adapters" figure is now 19 of 133, both re-measured rather than re-asserted. Diagnosis and design are rajan's, posted in full on the issue; implemented here.

### Fixed

- **#877: the dashboard media count is source-agnostic — the Media Library card can no longer contradict itself on screen.** `_check_media` asked the repository for one hardcoded source type (`"google_drive"`), so a local or upload library rendered an "Empty" badge, "Media Library — Not indexed" in System Status, and a 1/5 setup counter directly above its own category list (which was always tenant-wide). The count now uses `MediaRepository.count_active` — the same universe the card body renders — and is no longer gated on a configured folder, because uploads exist without one: an upload-only tenant's library now reads indexed with its real count. Both new tests were demonstrated red against the reverted fix (`0 == 3`, `0 == 2` at the count assertions) before landing green. Sibling sweep of the hardcoded-source class across `src/` + `cli/`: every other `"google_drive"` literal is a legitimate service identifier, provider-dispatch branch, validation list, or CLI choice — no unfixed same-class sibling found; noted for the record that `media_items.source_type` stores three values (`local`, `google_drive`, `upload`) while `chat_settings.media_source_type` validates two, which is consistent (different fields) but worth knowing when reading queries.

- **#879: the dashboard is clickable again — CSP was blocking every one of its 63 inline event handlers, so the Mini App rendered perfectly and did nothing.** `script-src 'self' https://telegram.org` carries no `'unsafe-inline'`, so the browser refused to execute inline `on*` attributes, and the app's entire interaction surface was inline: 55 `onclick` + 5 `onchange` in `index.html`, plus 3 more `app.js` injects at runtime into the account rows. The only `addEventListener` was `DOMContentLoaded` — which is why the page loaded and rendered correctly while every card, both OAuth buttons, the wizard navigation, the settings steppers, sync and indexing were inert. **Not introduced by #458**: before that commit there was no `script-src` at all, so `default-src 'self'` already governed scripts and inline handlers were already dead; #458 fixed the external SDK load it set out to fix and its title ("so Mini App is interactive") overstates the outcome. Fixed by **converting the handlers to delegated listeners**, not by relaxing the header — `'unsafe-inline'` would have traded a real security property for one line on a surface with zero users, and this file already interpolates into a quoted attribute using `_escapeHtml`, which is `textContent`-based and does not encode quotes (not currently exploitable, since the only value there is a server-generated UUID, but the strict policy is the net under that shape). Markup now declares `data-action` with JSON `data-args`, so argument **types survive the DOM** — `selectWindow(9, 21)` stays numbers, `adjustSetting('posts_per_day', 1)` stays a string then a number. Dispatch is an explicit **allowlist**, never `App[name]`, so a DOM-supplied string cannot reach an arbitrary method. The five Quick Controls switches declare `data-event="change"` because they are `<input>` inside `<label>`: a click-only delegation fires on the click *and* the change it triggers, toggling twice and netting to no change at all. Also adds `_escapeAttr` for attribute-context interpolation, since the newly-written `data-args` had no business using the text-context escaper. **Proved by real clicks in real Chromium with controls, because a programmatic drive reports success either way — that is precisely how the defect went unnoticed, including in the audit that filed #877/#878.** Same harness against `main`: every card, real clicks, all `expanded=False`; against this branch, all `expanded=True`, 13/13 — including the Schedule card, whose handler arrived from #881 *while this branch was in review* and was caught by review counting `main` independently rather than trusting this branch's own "zero remaining" report. That is the same silent-miss class this change exists to prevent, arriving through PR interleaving instead of a script bug, and it is why the gate is an independent enumeration of the post-rebase tree rather than the conversion script's exit code. Two negative controls: a `data-action` absent from the allowlist stays inert, and a re-introduced inline `onclick` is **still dead** on the branch — the CSP header is byte-identical in both runs, so the pass cannot have come from quietly relaxing it.
- **#874: the #873 route test now exercises the real `sync()` — or rather, its replacement does; the no-op is gone.** The predecessor asserted `sync`'s call arguments into a wholesale mock of the very service #872's bug lived in, so reverting the bug left it green — a test named for behavior it never exercised, reading as protection. The replacement runs the route against the REAL `MediaSyncService` (the service suite's own stubbed-`__init__` idiom supplies the mock floor one layer BELOW the defect, never above it) and asserts the boundary-resolved tenant at the repository seam. Demonstrated, not asserted: with the #872 discard reverted locally the test fails at exactly that assertion (`Expected: ('local', 'cs-test-1') / Actual: ('local', None)`), then greens with the fix restored — the evidence the wholesale-mock version structurally could not produce, and the check mutation testing cannot substitute for (a test with no coupling to the mutated layer never moves).

- **#872: `MediaSyncService.sync()` no longer discards its own `chat_settings_id` — "Sync Media Now" and onboarding indexing work again.** The tuple-unpack from `_resolve_source_config` rebound the parameter, so the tenant every route explicitly passed was silently replaced by the resolver's owner — `None` whenever source overrides were given, which is exactly how the routes call. Under the fail-closed repositories an explicit `None` scope raises, so every dashboard/onboarding sync 500'd. Pre-existing shape (present at the F.3 base; found by review on #866). The durable half is the test: the new service-level test asserts the property at the repository seam — the explicitly-passed tenant is the id every media mutation runs under, in the exact argument shape the routes use — and the route test now asserts `sync`'s call **arguments**, not merely its invocation, because a wholesale service mock sat exactly on top of the discard and made "2864 passed" structurally silent about it.

### Changed

- **F.3 conversion (#842): chat→tenant resolution now has ONE door and ONE policy — a missing tenant is a typed refusal, never a silent default, a bare None, a 404-vs-400 coin flip, or a phantom tenant.** The five divergent missing-tenant behaviors the burn-down inventory named (silent admin-default, boolean False, HTTPException 404, ValueError, auto-provision) are gone, plus the five more the conversion sweep found (None propagated as tenant scope at 7 sites — the "None means everything" fail-open one layer above where F.1 retired it; two silent bare returns; a None credentials contract; three divergent user-bail strings; a bespoke conflict type). Concretely: `TenantResolutionError` moves to `src/exceptions/tenancy.py` — the shared contract type of the two resolution tiers that never call each other — and `SettingsService.resolve_chat_settings_id` / `require_settings` are the legacy door (refuse `unknown_binding`, never mint; at M.3 the internals swap to the target resolver with no edge change). `get_settings` is **de-minted**: `create_if_missing` is removed, `get_settings_if_exists` is folded in, and minting is only ever the explicit `provision()` at the two sanctioned onboarding doors (group /start first contact; group linking) — the old default meant any dashboard read of an unknown chat id minted a live tenant that `get_or_create` marked `onboarding_completed=True`, i.e. straight into the scheduler. The web boundary resolves ONCE: `_validate_request` refuses unknown chats with the same 403 as a membership denial (no chat-existence oracle), hands `chat_settings_id` to routes, and the entire dashboard chain (facade + history/media/queue query classes, 27 functions) is re-keyed to the tenant id; `is_active_member` is keyed by resolved tenant id, so its False-on-unknown-chat case dissolved rather than being excepted. The scheduler's absent-chat→ADMIN-tenant default is removed outright (CLI names the admin chat at its own call sites); `get_queue_preview`'s unknown-chat unscoped-preview fail-open is gone with its re-key. Edge idioms are stated where refusals land: web 403 (plus an app-level handler so no refusal can 500), Telegram "run /start first" bails, job-boundary log-and-skip, idempotent teardown/ensure no-ops, and two named deliberate exceptions (the account-ownership not-found shape, which must stay indistinguishable from a non-owner probe; the instagram-tier documented ADMIN-chat CLI fallbacks, tracked for their own retirement). F.6 ratchet baseline moves in this diff, set-diff reviewed: the dashboard chain and membership leave `chat_id_functions_outside_adapters`; the door family (`resolve_chat_settings_id`, `require_settings`, `provision`) enters it as the one sanctioned chat-keyed surface pre-M.3.

- **F.3 (#842): neutral tenant resolution — the ONE resolver, born target-side.** `src/services/target/tenant_resolution.py`: inbound identity (chat binding | web session) resolves once to `01` §1's triple — (`user_id`, `workspace_id`, `channel_binding_id`) — and chat ids die at the boundary (the F.6 rule's semantic half, enforced at birth by an AST gate over the target service tier with its own mutation control). Fail-closed with a closed refusal vocabulary, never a default; the central authorization gate (`workspace_members` role check, one place) validates a CLAIMED workspace under its own `SET LOCAL` tenant context, so a false claim reads empty and refuses under RLS — and the claim provably dies with the transaction. **Relation to F.1 stated, not implied:** separate by design — F.1's chokepoint guards the legacy repository layer (chat_settings_id, dies at M.3); F.3 mints target identity (workspaces.id) for the L.0 unit of work; they share the refusal contract, not code. **Privilege reality measured in the tests** (14, against the real replayed schema, subject-gated per the #852 lesson): the web half runs end-to-end as `svc_ingress` today (auth-plane sessions + claim-gated membership); the chat half as `svc_ingress` fail-closes on a binding that provably exists, because the printed §7 policies give ingress no pre-context binding read — filed as decision fork #854 (resolver-door lean), pinned green so a sanctioned path reddens it deliberately. **Zero behavior change by construction: the diff only adds** — the legacy resolvers (settings/dashboard chat→chat_settings paths) are untouched and legacy-lifetime-bounded.

- **Repository session state now lives in ONE module-level ContextVar (copy-on-write mapping, per-instance sentinel keys) instead of two ContextVars minted per repository construction** — the per-instance vars accumulated forever in the thread's Context (measured: one 36-test file grew it 1 → 73 entries; a full suite, thousands), and at that scale CPython 3.10's HAMT collision defect surfaces as `TypeError: unhashable type: 'hamt_bitmap_node'` — the failure that turned main red at `test_queue_repository` setup while 3.11 (rewritten HAMT) stayed green. Task-copy semantics are unchanged: a spawned task's first write replaces the mapping in its own context only, nothing is mutated in place, sentinel keys are immune to id reuse, and `None` deletes the entry. Post-fix growth: 1 → 2 entries across 102 tests. No test changed.

- **F.4 (#751): the RLS runtime harness — all 58 policies EXERCISED as the declared runtime logins, each with a named disposition, none merely asserted present.** `tests/scripts/test_rls_runtime_harness.py` (100 tests) replays the full advertised stream under the declared actors and drives it as `svc_worker` AND `svc_ingress`: a 17-table tenant-isolation matrix per login, read AND write (a generic self-assign UPDATE exercises USING and WITH CHECK without per-table payloads; governance writes set the actor GUCs the way every runtime unit of work does), every zero paired with a positive on its own axis; `jobs`' deliberate absent-context exception (`OR workspace_id IS NULL`) pinned against a seeded system row instead of accidentally satisfied; cross-tenant INSERT refusals matched to the RLS message so a lost grant cannot impersonate the policy; all nine doors driven per real signatures — armed where the world permits (claim + lease-extend, the quarantine-skip negative, an enqueuing clock tick, retention reaping a year-old system job, the reaper expiring exactly the two overdue intents, invitation admitting a member) and pinned to exact empty shapes where not (`fetchall` is never None, so is-not-None asserts nothing — three such tautologies were found by review and removed); per-door EXECUTE census subtracting only that door's own owner; login DELETE censused to zero across the schema with live probes as the reality check; auth-plane sweep bounded on the SAME table (expired session gone, live session survives) plus the deliberate `service_tokens` asymmetry; `SET ROLE` refused everywhere with the bootstrap's two documented grants pinned exactly; zero-NULL gates with the presence half asserted. **The completeness oracle is the policy census itself**: every `pg_policies` row at (policy, table, cmd, roles) grain, asserted equal to the catalog (mutation-proven — removing a census row reddens naming the drift), with a disposition per row (16 matrix / 29 door / 5 auth / machinery / read-only / insert-pair) whose split is pinned — so a smuggled permissive policy on an already-covered table is a diff, not a silent pass. The transaction-reuse leakage class (4 tests, measured SQLSTATEs, including the silent SET-LOCAL-reverts-to-previous-tenant case the spec missed) is recovered from the parked `feat/751-f4-transaction-reuse-leakage` branch; mason's #789 minors land with it (owner-side control; the FORCE mutant report). The parent-chain seeder and the full-schema stand-up are promoted to `tests/scripts/conftest.py` as the one spelling. Out of CI reach and stated: the runtime-env credential-locality clause is a deployment property for the M-phase ops gate.


- **F.1 build (#841): the repository layer's tenant access is fail-closed — "None means everything" is extinct; deliberate cross-tenant access is now the explicit, greppable `SYSTEM_SCOPE`.** Every tenant-scoped repository method (media, history, lock, queue, category-mix, token, audit — 78 methods) now REQUIRES tenant context: the owning `chat_settings_id`, or the `SYSTEM_SCOPE` marker from `src/repositories/tenant_scope.py` for sanctioned system paths (worker loops, maintenance sweeps, health checks, the CLI — the same internal path `_write_allowed` always documented). Omission is a `TypeError` at the call; explicit `None` raises `TenantContextError` at the boundary, before any SQL. **Zero behavior change by construction:** all 78 previously-omitting call sites across `src/`+`cli/` were converted to explicit `SYSTEM_SCOPE` (which is falsy, so every pre-existing filter branch treats it exactly as it treated omission), and row-construction sites write ownership through `tenant_value()` (system scope stamps NULL, as omission always did). The four F.1 gate obligations are pinned in `tests/src/repositories/test_f1_fail_closed.py`: boundary failure per repo, cross-tenant reads return zero rows on a real database, Class-3 (user-plane) repositories REJECT tenant context, and the fail-open signature pattern is extinct via a scanner proven able to fail on a reintroduced instance. `grep -rn SYSTEM_SCOPE src/ cli/` is now the standing inventory of cross-tenant access — the #841 burn-down list for converting system paths to real tenant scoping, one reviewable behavior change at a time (telegram handler paths and the OAuth token upsert are the named first candidates).

### Added

- **L.1 — the intent ledger gate (#858)** — `src/services/target/intent_ledger.py` plus 23 tests in `tests/scripts/test_intent_ledger_gate.py`, all driving **raw SQL against the shipped triggers**. No migration: Phase F landed every table, trigger and seed in `055`, verified as zero files touched under `scripts/migrations/`.
  - **The trigger is the authority, so the tests bypass the service.** `04` §L.1's gate says so outright, and a suite routed through the service would prove only that the *service* refuses — the weaker claim, and the one that stays green while the database guarantee rots. Every refusal here is issued from a plain psycopg2 connection.
  - **The service deliberately validates nothing.** It issues the UPDATE and lets `trg_intent_guard` decide; `legal_transitions` reads `post_intent_transitions` and is never consulted before a write. A Python copy of the edge set would be a second authority that drifts, and every service-routed test would keep passing against the copy. A test deletes an edge and asserts the service reports the smaller set, so the read is proven live rather than memoised.
  - **Two vacuous tests were caught before they were written, both by the same control.** A first probe asserted "an actor-less mutation on each of the five governance tables raises" and appeared to pass for all five — `channel_bindings` and `oauth_credentials` were not raising at all, because the shared seed chain creates no rows there, so the UPDATE matched nothing and the row-level trigger never fired. Separately, `seed_workspace_chain` leaves `autocommit` OFF, so every intent minted afterwards sat uncommitted and invisible to the asserting connection — again 0 rows, again no trigger, again a green refusal test that refused nothing. **Both surfaced only because each refusal is paired with a positive control that asserts `rowcount >= 1`.**
  - **Every guard is proven load-bearing by removal.** `TestTheGuardsAreLoadBearing` drops each trigger and asserts the refusal disappears — transition guard (illegal edge *and* terminal immutability), audit trigger (actor-less state change), insert guard (born-scheduled), governance trigger. A refusal that came from an adjacent cause — a constraint, a missing row, a typo'd column — would survive the DROP and be caught here. This is the structural answer to the failure mode that mutation testing could not catch, where the test had mocked away the code under test.
  - **"Double transition" is asserted as the property that actually holds, not the phrasing.** Measured: a same-state write **succeeds** — the guard only compares when `NEW.state IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.state`. What is true, and is asserted, is that it produces **no audit row** and does **not** advance `entered_state_at`, so it cannot forge a second ledger entry; and that replaying a completed transition *is* refused, because after A→B the edge no longer matches. Rejecting the repeat is structural, not a check.
  - **The edge table and the completeness CHECKs are two independent rules, and both are asserted.** Six legal edges are refused outright if the update does not also carry what the destination requires (`ck_publishing_debited`, `ck_posted_complete`, `ck_ambiguous_called`). So the suite proves each edge lands *with* its companion columns and is still refused *without* them — the shape L.5's flip transaction has to satisfy.
  - The transition matrix is derived at runtime from `post_intent_transitions` and `ck_intent_state`, never written down here, with a floor assertion (≥13 states, ≥27 edges, >50 illegal pairs examined) so a failed derivation cannot present as a clean pass.

- **L.0 — the transaction substrate and egress floor (#857)** — `src/services/target/unit_of_work.py` and `src/services/target/egress.py`, with 39 tests covering all five gate clauses. **This is the neck of Phase L**: L.1 and L.2 are serial behind it and the four-wide fan-out is behind L.2.
  - **`max_overflow` is pinned to 0 — read from the `05` seam, not chosen.** `05`'s tasks-vs-connections paragraph carries the arithmetic (`3×(10+0) + 2×(10+0) = 50`) and the R4 rationale. It is deliberately **not** read from `settings.DB_MAX_OVERFLOW`, which is `20` on this repo — exactly R4's finding, since that silently makes the true ceiling `(10+20)×5 = 150`. A test asserts the two still differ, so if someone later wires the seam to the config the assertion tells them rather than passing quietly. The legacy sync engine still reads the setting and is **left alone** — retiring it is M.3's.
  - **The UoW is unconstructible without a tenant**, not merely refusing on use: nothing can hold one and reach a query. `02` §7 RLS reads `app.tenant_id`, so a tenant-less UoW would be a widened query waiting for a caller — the failure F.1 retired at the repository layer, closed here at the transaction layer. **No `SYSTEM_SCOPE` analogue exists and a test asserts its absence**: `04` §L.0 says system operations go through the `02` §7 doors, so a privileged UoW variant would bypass the audited path.
  - **GUC hygiene proven under pool reuse, on a single-slot pool.** `SET LOCAL` dies with the transaction, so the next borrower cannot inherit another tenant's scope — asserted by alternating two tenants through a `pool_size=1` engine and then reading `app.tenant_id` from a plain connection off the same slot. A large pool would hand each UoW a fresh connection and the gate would pass without exercising reuse at all.
  - **Transaction-per-checkpoint is enforced, not documented** (`02` §5). A ContextVar marks an open transaction and the egress point refuses any provider call while it is set — the check lives at egress because that is the one place every provider call passes, and the alternative is a rule each adapter must remember. The flag is reset from its token in `finally`, never by assignment: a leaked flag would make every later call in the task raise, a wrong-way failure that looks like the discipline working.
  - **The four floor proofs are each a PAIR, and each was mutation-verified against the implementation.** Guard disabled → the proof fails; restored → green. Observed, per guard: unbounding the per-attempt timeout reddens the hang proof; removing the absolute-budget check reddens the retry-storm proof; removing the cap comparison reddens the oversize proof; removing the private-address check reddens six SSRF cases; removing the cross-host check reddens the redirect proof. **Each mutation named only its own proof — no cascades**, so the proofs are coupled to the guards rather than to each other.
  - **SSRF refuses three independent ways**, because each catches what the others cannot: a closed host allowlist (a denylist cannot enumerate the internet), an address-class check over **every** resolved address (checking only the first is the DNS-rebinding shape — pinned by a test where a host resolves to one public and one private address), and outright refusal of cross-host redirects with same-host hops **re-validated from scratch** (a redirect is an attacker-controlled URL, so validating only the original would make the allowlist decorative). The metadata address `169.254.169.254` is in the parametrised refusal set.
  - **The retry budget is ONE absolute wall-clock budget, not per-attempt.** A per-attempt timeout times N attempts is not a bound a caller can reason about; it is how a "5 second timeout" becomes a 40-second stall. The proof asserts a storm stops with retries still nominally available, and its paired negative shows that with the budget disabled the same storm runs to the attempt count.
  - **No migration.** Phase F already landed every table, trigger and door Phase L names (`052`–`060`), and L.0 adds no DDL — verified as zero files touched under `scripts/migrations/`.
  - **Review round (navi, egress floor; branden, substrate) — six findings, folded into one revision.** Fixed here: the byte cap now **streams and abandons the read** past the cap (it was post-hoc — `client.request()` materialises the whole body, so the previous check reported a memory DoS rather than preventing one, and was untestable by construction under `MockTransport`; a chunk-counting stream now proves early abort); `pool_timeout` is pinned to a derived **3.0 s** seam (with `max_overflow=0` the pool cannot burst, so the timeout *is* the saturation policy — SQLAlchemy's 30 s default is pile-up rather than `01` H5's slip-a-slot, and it exceeded the egress floor's own budget, so the substrate could outlast its callers); `pool_recycle=300` carried across from the legacy engine; `pool_size` pinned too, since half a pinned inequality is not pinned. The `test_the_seam_is_NOT_read_from_settings` gate **inverted when the defect it guards was fixed** — it asserted `settings.DB_MAX_OVERFLOW != SEAM`, so remediating R4 by setting the config to 0 turned it red; replaced with the behavioural form (drive the setting to any value, assert the engine still shows the literal), verified green at `DB_MAX_OVERFLOW=0`. Whitespace tenant ids now refused. Added the composed-path test (real UoW + real `egress.request`, which had zero coverage while both halves were green), a task-spawned-inside-the-transaction test, and the 3-hop redirect-chain regression test navi verified by hand but found unasserted. Correction: the SSRF mutation reddens **8** tests, not the "six" originally claimed.
  - **Deferred visibly to S.3 (#871): the SSRF resolve/connect TOCTOU.** `validate_target` checks every resolved address, but httpx re-resolves at connect time and exposes no resolver hook, so the check proves the hostname resolved public *at that moment*, not that the connection went there. Closing it needs a custom transport that pins the IP while keeping TLS SNI and verification correct — materially larger than the other findings, and getting it wrong is worse than the gap. Exploitability is bounded by the allowlist being six closed high-reputation provider hostnames. **The module docstring now names the gap and states that the allowlist — not the address check — is the load-bearing control**, and the plan doc's S.3 entry names the carried item. The deferral's real cost is written down: do not widen that allowlist before #871 lands.
  - New runtime dependency: `asyncpg==0.31.0`, SQLAlchemy's async Postgres driver. The legacy sync engine keeps `psycopg2`; the two coexist until M.3. **CI is the arbiter on Python 3.10** — this change uses ContextVars and the repo has had a leak that only tripped there. Locally the token-reset semantics were checked under a real 3.10 interpreter (nesting and default-restore identical), but the suite itself ran on 3.11.

- **F.6 — the FC-2 Telegram-reference ratchet lands in CI, baseline re-measured at install (#843)** — `scripts/telegram_ratchet.py` (stdlib-only, the `fc8_gate.py` precedent), its committed baseline, 22 tests, and a dedicated `FC-2 Telegram ratchet` CI job.
  - **The predicate is executable code and the count is derived from it**, which is F.1 §4's prescription verbatim: *"commit the predicate as executable code and derive the count from it, so the baseline and the gate cannot disagree."* The baseline file stores **sets, never counts** — every number reported is `len()` of a stored set, because a count stored beside its set is a second source of truth that can drift from it. A test asserts no scalar is stored beside a list, and another asserts the committed predicate text still **equals** the module docstring it came from.
  - **It counts the thing, not the string — measured, not asserted.** On the tree at install the substring predicate returns **72** modules where structural AST import-resolution returns **27**: 46 phantom entries, i.e. ~45 modules of headroom in which a genuine new coupling could land without reddening. That is the F.1 `SYSTEM_SCOPE` defect at larger scale. It also fails the *other* way, which matters more: `src/services/core/telegram_operation_state.py` is a real Telegram module containing no literal "telegram", so a substring predicate **misses** it. Both directions are pinned by tests.
  - **EQUALITY, not ceiling** — following the ruling on the F.1 ratchet, for the same reason: a ceiling accrues headroom as the burn-down retires sites, silently pre-buying unreviewed access for a future addition, while equality forces every delta into the same reviewable diff. Shrink-only then falls out of equality plus review rather than needing its own mechanism. A test drives a **shrink** red to prove it.
  - **Baseline measured at install, with its predicate: 27 / 22 / 115 / 0.** Telegram-coupled modules **27**; the FC-2 core-services segment **22** (clause 3 requires it to reach empty); chat-id-accepting functions outside adapters **115**; `provider_account_ref` in logging call sites **0**. The plan's 75/76 were not adopted — they do not reproduce, and F.1 §4 showed the axis yields 18/64/70/72 depending on predicate.
  - **Only one of the four is pass/fail, and that is a finding rather than a design preference.** The `07` §5 hygiene axis is already at zero, so it is enforced as a hard rule. The other three are violated today *by the plan's own account* — FC-2 clause 3 says the core segment must **reach** empty, which presupposes it has not — so shipping them as pass/fail would be red on arrival, and narrowing them until they passed would be the "blocks nothing or blocks everything" failure §4 names. They ratchet.
  - **The chat-id rule exempts allowlisted adapters, deliberately.** 45 of the 160 chat-id-accepting functions live inside Telegram adapters, where taking a chat id is FC-2 working as intended (adapters at the edges). Counting them would make the rule fire on the correct design; the rule is that *non-adapter* code must not take Telegram ids, leaving 115.
  - **Red-test demos, live on the real tree and under CI's own interpreter.** All four axes were driven red and restored green: a new core Telegram module (+1 on two axes), a chat-id parameter outside an adapter, `provider_account_ref` reaching a log call, and a **shrink**. Plus 22 unit tests including parameter-evasion cases (positional-only, keyword-only, async, camelCase) and an unparsable-module disclosure, since a syntax error would otherwise read as a clean shrink.
  - **Verified against Python 3.10, not just local 3.11.** CI runs 3.10 and local-green is not evidence for an environment-conditional class, so the gate was executed under a real 3.10.20 interpreter and its `--json` measurement diffed against 3.11's: **byte-identical**. `ast` shape differences between the two would have shown here.

- **F.2.9 — 07's auth plane lands as migration 060, and F.2 IS COMPLETE (#806)** — three tables (`session_tokens`, `oauth_states`, `service_tokens`) with their touch triggers, RLS, two grants and five policies: advertised-stream statements 241..256, the final sixteen. Arm (b) reports `ok=True vacuous=False f2_count=257` — **the target lineage now EQUALS the 257-statement stream rather than prefixing it; 0 remaining.** 26 tables / 26 RLS / 58 policies, exactly split §4's endpoint.
  - **Per-file breakdown, the complete lineage:** 052 → 2 `[0..1]`, 053 → 20 `[2..21]`, 054 → 23 `[22..44]`, 055 → 32 `[45..76]`, 056 → 17 `[77..93]`, 057 → 32 `[94..125]`, 058 → 76 `[126..201]`, 059 → 39 `[202..240]`, 060 → 16 `[241..256]`. Sum 257.
  - **THE CROSSING, measured to the statement.** `02-domain-model.md` contributes indices 0..240 and `07-security-model.md` begins at 241, so all 16 of this file's statements come from `07` and **none** from `02`. `07` numbers its own sections from §1, so the four preserved `-- [§n ...]` markers do not continue `02`'s numbering.
  - **These three tables arrive WITH their RLS and policies**, unlike every `02` increment — the split's "#746's original property for free". Measured consequence, from the squash-proof probe: `tenancy_violations` is **0 at prefix 241 and 0 at 257**, so the auth plane adds three tables without ever reopening the window that F.2.2–F.2.6 held open.
  - **Before/after reproduces from one command**, the technique carried forward from F.2.8 and independent of arm (b) because it reads the stream rather than the files: prefix 241 → `tables=23 rls=23 policies=53`; prefix 257 → `tables=26 rls=26 policies=58`. Snippet in the PR body.
  - **The `07` marker says "6 policies" and there are FIVE — recorded, not corrected.** That text lives inside a manifest-hashed block, so editing it would break the sha256 bijection. Five is confirmed three independent ways: the segment carries 5 `CREATE POLICY`, the whole-stream delta is 58 − 53 = 5, and `07`'s raw block text contains 5. The likely origin of "6" is an assumed 3-ingress + 3-sweep symmetry: `service_tokens` gets `p_auth_ingress_svctok` but **no sweep policy**. That asymmetry is deliberate and internally consistent — the maintenance `GRANT` likewise covers only `session_tokens, oauth_states`, so grant and policy agree that service tokens are not swept. A postcondition pins both halves.
  - **`oauth_states` has no surrogate key** — its primary key is `state` itself, the 128-bit urlsafe random value the OAuth round-trip carries. `reconnect_target` is a bare `UUID` with no FK, because the plan constrains it to no table. Its `ck_oauth_state_context` is a CASE expression rather than a conjunction: `signin` carries neither id, `link` carries a user only, everything else carries both.
  - **Models return to scope** (3 tables): `src/models/target/auth_plane.py`, registered in `__init__.py`. Parity passed first run.
  - **The GRANT blind spot is live (2 grants) and was hand-covered with DISCRIMINATING mutations.** Both redden the same 7 lane tests — expected, since they sit in one file — but each raises a distinct message naming the specific failing SQL, which is the property that matters: dropping the `NOT` yields `...expected true: SELECT has_table_privilege('svc_maintenance','session_tokens','DELETE') AND has_table_privilege('svc_maintenance','service_tokens','DELETE')`, while the policy-count mutation yields `...expected true: SELECT count(*) = 4 FROM pg_policies ... LIKE 'p_auth_%'`. The cross-increment-index blind spot is not exercised — zero `CREATE INDEX`.

- **F.2.8 — 02 §7-DDL's nine SECURITY DEFINER doors land as migration 059 (#806)** — ten `CREATE FUNCTION`, nine `ALTER … OWNER TO`, nine `REVOKE ALL ON FUNCTION`, nine `GRANT EXECUTE`, and the one-statement `GRANT CREATE` / `REVOKE CREATE` bracket: advertised-stream statements 202..240, in stream order. Arm (b) reports `ok=True vacuous=False f2_count=241`. **16 statements remain — F.2.9 alone.**
  - **Per-file breakdown:** 052 → 2 `[0..1]`, 053 → 20 `[2..21]`, 054 → 23 `[22..44]`, 055 → 32 `[45..76]`, 056 → 17 `[77..93]`, 057 → 32 `[94..125]`, 058 → 76 `[126..201]`, 059 → 39 `[202..240]`. Sum 241 = `f2_count`.
  - **Does NOT cross into `07`, and it is the last increment that does not.** `02-domain-model.md` contributes stream indices 0..240 and `07-security-model.md` begins at 241, so this file ends exactly ONE statement short of the boundary. F.2.9 is the crossing, and the auth plane's shape differs — three tables arriving *with* their RLS and policies, unlike every `02` table increment.
  - **Ten `CREATE FUNCTION`, nine doors, and that is not a discrepancy.** `fn_next_slot` is a pure `STABLE` helper called by `fn_clock_tick` — not `SECURITY DEFINER`, so it correctly receives no owner change and no `EXECUTE` grant. Anyone counting functions to check the split's "nine" will find ten; the nine are those carrying the three-statement cycle. A postcondition pins `fn_next_slot` as **not** `prosecdef`, so the distinction is asserted rather than only explained.
  - **The before/after is reproducible from a single command, which is deliberately better than commit ordering.** The prior increment's measurement could not be checked from a squashed history without rebuilding it. This one derives BOTH arms from stream prefixes in one run and depends on no git ref at all, so it survives the squash: prefix 202 → `doors=0 owners=0 grant_execute=0`; prefix 241 → `doors=9 owners=4 grant_execute=9`. The exact snippet is in the PR body.
  - **Four owners for nine doors — `svc_claim`, `svc_clock`, `svc_maintenance`, `svc_membership` — cross-checked against an independently written list.** `DOOR_OWNER_ROLES` in `tests/scripts/test_advertised_ddl_replay.py` names exactly those four, derived separately from this migration.
  - **The transient CREATE bracket is this file's sharpest detail.** `ALTER FUNCTION … OWNER TO` requires the executor to be a member of the new owning role AND the new owner to hold CREATE on the schema — membership alone fails with "permission denied for schema public" (pass 7, R6's P0, empirically forced). The schema owner grants CREATE, the doors are installed and re-owned, and the grant is revoked as the section's last statement. The bracket opens and closes inside this one file.
  - **The GRANT blind spot is LIVE in this increment (20 `GRANT`/`REVOKE`), so it was covered by hand.** Table-level parity cannot see a grant. Two mutations, each turning **7 lane tests red** and green on revert: inverting the CREATE-bracket check (proving the privilege really is absent, not merely unasserted) and pointing the `EXECUTE` probe at `svc_membership` instead of the actual grantee `svc_worker`. The cross-increment-index blind spot is **not** exercised — zero `CREATE INDEX`, by count.
  - **One postcondition is a negative, deliberately, and it is a different category from the ones 053 banned.** That rule forbids asserting the absence of state a LATER increment adds. The CREATE-bracket check asserts the TERMINAL state this file itself establishes with its own last statement; no later increment re-grants CREATE on public to a door owner, and `advertised_ddl_replay` asserts the same absence independently. A door owner holding CREATE afterwards means this migration applied only partially — exactly what an adoption probe should catch.
  - **No target models, structurally:** `CREATE FUNCTION`, `ALTER … OWNER TO`, `REVOKE` and `GRANT EXECUTE` have no declarative SQLAlchemy representation, so `create_all` emits none of them and there is nothing to mirror. Zero relations created.
  - A postcondition initially named `fn_claim_job(text,text,interval)`; the real signature is the 4-arg `(text, text, interval, int)` the `GRANT` itself uses. Caught before commit by reading the emitted SQL — a wrong signature raises rather than silently passing.

- **F.2.7 — 02 §7-DDL's row-level security lands as migration 058, and the tenancy gate closes (#806)** — 23 `ALTER TABLE ... ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` and 53 `CREATE POLICY`: advertised-stream statements 126..201, in stream order. Arm (b) reports `ok=True vacuous=False f2_count=202`; 55 stream statements remain.
  - **The gate closing is measured, not claimed.** `tenancy_violations` over the stream's own prefix reports **17 violations at index 126** (through F.2.6) and **0 at index 202** (through this file), with the tenant-keyed count unchanged at 17 — so the change is RLS arriving, not the population moving. Taken on the plan side *before* the migration was written, and confirmed afterwards against the replayed catalog.
  - **Per-file breakdown:** 052 → 2 `[0..1]`, 053 → 20 `[2..21]`, 054 → 23 `[22..44]`, 055 → 32 `[45..76]`, 056 → 17 `[77..93]`, 057 → 32 `[94..125]`, 058 → 76 `[126..201]`. Sum 202 = `f2_count`.
  - **Does NOT cross into `07`, measured.** `02-domain-model.md` contributes stream indices 0..240 and `07-security-model.md` begins at 241, so 126..201 is entirely `02` §7-DDL. The auth plane arrives at F.2.9.
  - **No target models, for a structural reason rather than an empirical one.** `ALTER TABLE ... ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` and `CREATE POLICY` have no declarative SQLAlchemy representation — `create_all` cannot emit them under any model definition — so there is nothing a model could mirror. The 23 tables were modelled in F.2.2–F.2.5; this increment creates zero relations.
  - **Seventeen of the 53 policies are GENERATED, not literal**, which matters to anyone diffing this file against `02` by eye: `expand_policies` materialises the two pattern lists the §7-DDL block declares — `p_tenant` across 14 tenant-plane tables and `p_user_plane` across 3 user-plane ones — so they appear in the stream, and here, without appearing verbatim in the plan's prose.
  - **An expiring pin fired exactly as designed, and its side prediction was wrong.** The lane carried an assertion pinning the OLD invariant shape as red through F.2.2–F.2.6, with its own expiry: *"if RLS has now landed, the mid-stream window has closed and this pin should go with it."* The **instruction held**; the **prediction did not** — it expected the crossing increment to turn the messages from "RLS is not enabled" to "no policy", but F.2.7 lands the 23 `ENABLE` and the 53 `CREATE POLICY` together, so no table is ever left enabled-but-unpolicied and the list goes straight to empty (0 observed, not 17 reworded). Recorded in place, because the next tripwire will be read the same way.
  - **The pin was replaced by the invariant, not by nothing.** `tenancy_violations(sig) == []` is now asserted with a positive control (a non-empty tenant-keyed set), so the closing is observed rather than merely un-flagged. The probe comparison downstream was rebased onto that captured baseline rather than a hardcoded `[]`, keeping it relative.
  - **Both known gate boundaries hand-checked and absent:** 058 contains zero `CREATE INDEX` (the cross-increment-index gap) and zero `GRANT`/`REVOKE` (the grant gap), verified by count. RLS and policies are themselves invisible to table-level parity, but unlike grants they *are* covered — by `tenancy_gate` and by `advertised_ddl_replay`'s pattern-list assertion. Postconditions were mutation-checked anyway: an unsatisfiable policy floor turned **7 lane tests red**, reverting restored green. Counts use `>=` because F.2.9 adds 3 more RLS tables and 5 more policies (split §4: 23/23/53 here, 26/26/58 there).

- **F.2.6 — 02 §7-DDL's grant matrix and the `archive` schema land as migration 057 (#806)** — thirty grants/revokes, `CREATE SCHEMA archive` and its grant: advertised-stream statements 94..125, in stream order. Arm (b) reports `ok=True vacuous=False f2_count=126`; 131 stream statements remain.
  - **Per-file breakdown, so `vacuous` is re-derivable rather than trusted:** 052 → 2 `[0..1]`, 053 → 20 `[2..21]`, 054 → 23 `[22..44]`, 055 → 32 `[45..76]`, 056 → 17 `[77..93]`, 057 → 32 `[94..125]`. Sum 126 = `f2_count`.
  - **First increment with NO target models, which follows from the parity rule rather than excepting it.** Since F.2.2 an increment has been migration PLUS models, because lane parity compares the replayed `public` against `create_all` and a table on one side only is drift. This one creates ZERO relations — nothing for `create_all` to render, so no model to write. Verified by running: parity passes with `src/models/target/` untouched.
  - **Still sourced from `02`, measured rather than assumed.** `02-domain-model.md` contributes stream indices 0..240 and `07-security-model.md` begins at 241, so 94..125 is `02` §7-DDL. What changes at this increment is the *character* of the source — security objects rather than table sections — not the document. `07`'s three auth-plane tables arrive at F.2.9.
  - **The plan's block marker is wider than the file, and is kept verbatim anyway.** It reads "§7-DDL grants + RLS enablement + policies"; only the grants are here, since RLS and the 53 policies are statements 126..201 (F.2.7). The marker is the plan's, not a description of this increment's boundary.
  - **A measured premise in the lane suite expired here, exactly where its own comment said it would.** `test_the_legacy_bound_is_load_bearing_not_tidy` ran both arms unbootstrapped on the stated grounds that "no migration and no `setup_database.sql` references a service role" — 057's grant matrix names all six, so the unbounded arm failed with `role "svc_ingress" does not exist`. The note anticipated this ("that is where the first policy-carrying table will need it"); grants arrived before policies. The unbounded arm now runs on `bootstrapped_db`; the bounded arm stays roleless, because it stops below the move, never reaches 057, and keeping it roleless is what preserves the contrast the test draws.
  - **The postconditions carry more weight in this increment than in any before it, and were mutation-checked rather than assumed.** Parity is table-level and a GRANT is not a relation, so nothing else in the suite independently verifies the matrix applied. Falsifying one postcondition (`has_table_privilege('svc_clock','jobs','INSERT')` → `'TRUNCATE'`) turned **7 lane tests red**; reverting restored green. Five probes cover a schema, a schema privilege, a table privilege, a role-specific grant and a column-level grant.
  - **`navi`'s cross-increment-index gap is not exercised here** — 057 contains zero `CREATE INDEX` statements, verified by count. Nothing to hand-check.
  - **Roles are not created by this file** and the plan is emphatic (pass 7 R6's P0; D40): the stream runs as `svc_migration`, which must not hold role DDL's cluster privileges, so the seven service roles come from the `04` window-bootstrap. The tenancy gate is still red after this file — grants narrow verbs, RLS narrows rows, and F.2.7 is what closes it.

- **F.2.5 — 02 §5 and §6's execution machinery lands as migration 056 (#806)** — five tables (`jobs`, `channel_outbox`, `provider_operations`, `command_dedup`, `rate_counters`), three touch triggers and nine indexes: advertised-stream statements 77..93, in stream order. Arm (b) reports `ok=True vacuous=False f2_count=94`. **This completes `02`'s 23 tables** — split §4's endpoint for the table increments; 163 stream statements remain, and the character of the work changes from here (F.2.6 grants, F.2.7 RLS and policies, F.2.8 doors).
  - **Per-file breakdown, so `vacuous` is re-derivable rather than trusted:** 052 → 2 `[0..1]`, 053 → 20 `[2..21]`, 054 → 23 `[22..44]`, 055 → 32 `[45..76]`, 056 → 17 `[77..93]`. Sum 94 = `f2_count`, and a non-empty F.2 side is what makes `vacuous=False` mean something.
  - **Numbered 056, verified rather than assumed** — checked against the corpus *and* against every open PR touching `scripts/migrations/`. Only #840 claims a number (054, for a legacy-lineage change), and it collides with the merged 054 independently of this file; `discover_migrations` hard-fails on duplicates, so no collision can land silently.
  - **It spans two plan sections, and keeps the plan's own markers.** §5 is `jobs`; §6 is the outbound-effects pair plus dedup and rate counters. Four `-- [§n ...]` markers survive verbatim in the body. A first draft replaced them with one invented marker — caught before commit, because inventing a section number is exactly the authored content the file header disclaims.

On the models side it therefore had to be declared on `PostIntent` in `intent_ledger.py` — `create_all` renders a table's indexes from its own `__table_args__`, so that is the only place it can come from. Marked there as belonging to this increment. Same shape as 055's five `tg_audit_*` triggers landing on 053's and 054's tables.
  - **`jobs.workspace_id` is nullable, alone among the increment's tenant columns**, and `ck_jobs_system_kinds` is what makes that safe: an equivalence asserting no-workspace exactly when the kind is a system kind, so a tenant-scoped job cannot lose its tenant and a sweep cannot acquire one.
  - **Two `intent_id` columns carry no foreign key** — `channel_outbox.intent_id` (nullable) and `provider_operations.intent_id` (not). Both are evidence that an effect was attempted and must survive the intent being reaped. The plan declares no FK on either; none is invented.
  - **`command_dedup` and `rate_counters` carry no `updated_at`** and no touch trigger — write-once coordination records, so `timestamps()` is deliberately not uniform across the module. `jobs.payload` uses the backslash-escaped colon for its `'{"v":1}'` default, the 053 trap the parity gate structurally cannot see.
  - **Correction to 055's description, no functional impact.** Migration 055's header and its CHANGELOG entry call it "02 §3's intent ledger". It also carries all of plan §4 — the transitions table, the 27-row seed and the guard/audit triggers — as the stream's own `-- [§4 ...]` marker shows. The SQL, the models and every gate are unaffected; only the prose under-describes the increment.

- **F.2.4 — 02 §3's intent ledger lands as migration 055 (#806)** — five tables (`category_post_case_mix`, `post_intents`, `audit_events`, `daily_post_counts`, `post_intent_transitions`), 27 seeded legal-edge rows, four trigger functions and eleven triggers: advertised-stream statements 45..76, in stream order. Arm (b) reports `ok=True vacuous=False f2_count=77`. The lineage now holds 18 tables, which is what the ratified split's §4 predicts for this increment; 180 stream statements remain.
  - **Numbered 055, not 054.** 054 is F.2.3 (merged, #844). PR #840 also proposes a 054, for a LEGACY-lineage change, and is blocked pending a lineage ruling — it collides with the merged 054 independently of this file and must renumber either way. `discover_migrations` hard-fails on duplicates, so no collision can land silently.
  - **The file body is GENERATED FROM THE STREAM, not transcribed**, the rule 053 set and 054 kept: statements 45..76 were extracted through the repo's own extractor and the file asserted at write time to normalize back to exactly those statements. Only the comment header is hand-written, and the normalizer strips it.
  - **This increment carries DATA, which the three before it did not.** Statement 64 seeds `post_intent_transitions` with 27 legal edges. They are not fixture rows: `trg_intent_guard` reads the table on every UPDATE, so an unseeded schema rejects every transition while still replaying green — the pass-5/R4 finding the seed closes. `advertised_ddl_replay` asserts one legal and one illegal transition against these rows, so the matrix text and the seeds cannot drift.
  - **It also attaches triggers to earlier increments' tables.** The five `tg_audit_*` governance triggers land on `workspaces`, `workspace_members`, `channel_bindings` (053) and `ig_accounts`, `oauth_credentials` (054). That is the stream's order rather than a liberty: `audit_events` must exist before anything writes to it, which is why the reshape moved it into this increment (split §3).
  - **`audit_events.workspace_id` carries no foreign key, deliberately** — the one tenant column in the increment that is a bare `UUID NOT NULL`, because an audit row must outlive the workspace it describes and a CASCADE would delete exactly the evidence of the deletion.
  - **`post_intents`' two composite FKs carry no `ON DELETE`, unlike every composite FK in 054.** Under default `NO ACTION` a delete that would strand a live intent is refused rather than silently cascading the ledger away. `daily_post_counts` does cascade, because a count is derived rather than evidentiary. The asymmetry is the plan's and is mirrored exactly.
  - **The 053 JSONB-default trap, avoided rather than rediscovered.** `post_intents.attempts_by_step` defaults to `'{"v":1}'`; written naively as `text("'{\"v\":1}'")` SQLAlchemy reads `:1` as a bind parameter and emits `DEFAULT '{"v"NULL}'`, so the table will not create. The parity gate never compares defaults, so this fails at `create_all` rather than as drift. Backslash-escaped, as `channel_bindings.settings` already is.
  - **Models land in the same increment**, per the scoping rule F.2.3 established and navi reproduced: `src/models/target/intent_ledger.py`, registered in `__init__.py`. Three of the five tables have no `updated_at` — `audit_events` and `post_intent_transitions` are §0 insert-only classes with no touch trigger — so `timestamps()` is deliberately not uniform across the module.
  - **Postconditions scoped to objects 055 itself creates, none asserting absence.** The seed probe names ONE edge rather than counting rows, because a later increment may legitimately add an edge and a count would then read the migration unapplied.

- **F.2.3 — 02 §2's accounts, sources and media tables land as migration 054 (#806)** — six of them (`ig_accounts`, `provider_quarantine`, `media_sources`, `oauth_credentials`, `media_items`, `post_locks`) with their six touch triggers and eleven partial/plain indexes: advertised-stream statements 22..44, in stream order, after 053's 2..21 and 052's 0..1. Arm (b) reports `ok=True vacuous=False f2_count=45`. The lineage now holds 13 tables, which is what the ratified split's §4 table predicts for this increment.
  - **The file body is GENERATED FROM THE STREAM, not transcribed** — the same rule 053 set. Statements 22..44 were extracted through the repo's own extractor and the resulting file asserted, at write time, to normalize back to exactly those statements. The one hand-edit is the leading comment block, which the normalizer strips. Edit the plan and the manifest ratchet; never this file alone.
  - **NO POLICIES AND NO RLS**, per Fork 1 ruling (a) and the ratified split §3: F.2.2–F.2.5 land tables, triggers and indexes, and RLS plus policies for all of them land together in F.2.7. All six tables here are tenant-keyed and carry no policy until then; the prefix-aware lane check accounts for it on both sides.
  - **Target models land in the same increment** — `src/models/target/accounts_sources_media.py` mirrors 054 on `TargetBase`, registered in `src/models/target/__init__.py`. Not a stylistic choice: lane parity compares the replayed `public` against `create_all`, so a migration-side table with no model behind it IS the drift it reports. Verified by running — `test_lane_parity_holds_against_the_target_models` fails with the migration alone and passes once the models land.
  - **Composite foreign keys are this increment's shape.** Four of the six FKs are composite `(workspace_id, <id>)` references rather than single-column ones — `oauth_credentials` to `ig_accounts` and `media_sources`, `media_items` to `media_sources`, `post_locks` to `media_items` and `ig_accounts`. That is the plan's tenant-integrity device: a composite FK makes a cross-workspace row unrepresentable in the schema rather than merely forbidden by policy, which is why each parent carries a `UNIQUE (workspace_id, id)` existing solely as their target. A single-column rendering would still produce a working schema and would be a red parity test.
  - **`provider_quarantine` is the one table with no surrogate key** — its PK is the composite `(workspace_id, scope_ref)`, so it does not call `pk()`, and its `workspace_id` is both tenant key and half the primary key.
  - **`post_locks.created_by_intent_id` carries no foreign key, deliberately.** `post_intents` lands in F.2.4, so a reference to it here would not be a stream prefix; the plan declares none and the model does not invent one.
  - **Postconditions are scoped to objects 054 itself creates and none asserts absence** — the rule 053 established after a review catch. `migration_runner.py` derives a file's permanent adoption probe from these lines, so a postcondition asserting the absence of state a later increment adds would read unapplied at M.3 on a database plainly holding all six tables. F.2.7 enables RLS on these tables and attaches their policies, and neither may retroactively un-apply this migration.

- **The M.2 rehearsal spec (#790) — `documentation/planning/2026-08-17-m2-rehearsal-spec/README.md`: the window rehearsal made executable as a protocol, with its preconditions stated as measurements rather than assumptions.** Planning artifact only. Five legs (clean end-to-end with per-step assertions; rollback-and-re-entry with the D41 ledger-truth checks; abandon with the printed capability gate; guard-refusal with byte-identical catalog-snapshot proofs; the `05` §DR first-drill obligations — RTO datum and the once-exercised tenant-level extraction), the Neon managed-role **verdict checklist** (ten executable probes derived from the bootstrap and the pass-8/9 D40 amendments, each with its stock-PG expectation and divergence route — the verdict is the branch's to give, not this spec's), and the smoke battery scoped to what the parity-bar doc proves the target *is* today (DB-plane instruments; the app-plane bar is M.3's precondition, its two open forks adjacent-not-inherited).
  - **Nothing here assumes access:** production is measured unreachable from the fleet (stale Neon creds, unauthorized Railway, no `DATABASE_URL`), so the branch, the owner-role session, and repeat-until-clean re-branching are named provisioning asks (P1), not presumptions. The owner-session ask names its acquisition mechanism (review round: the obvious console path — a credential reset on the role production authenticates as — is prohibited in so many words; the preferred path is the inherited credential against the branch endpoint, verified read-only at first contact, with the fallback's branch-locality proof placed on the provisioner).
  - **Fork A is inherited at 3e and made cheap instead of expensive:** a quarantine fire mid-rehearsal is specified as the fork surfacing (halt, export evidence read-only, file on #790 — never hand-repair the branch as if A3 were ruled), with per-option deltas for what each A ruling changes in the rehearsal.
  - Gate honesty carried over from M.1: every clause of the `04` L91 gate is specified, none executable until the named preconditions clear — stated with owners rather than papered over. No new forks surfaced; five [SD-M2-n] spec-tier decisions registered for review. — #796 removed the broad `except` that let a named tenant's OAuth failure fall through to the deployment-wide service account; the review stated its bound rather than implying it: fail-closed confirmed at 1 of 3 callers by inspection, the other two left open. #819 covered two (`telegram_autopost.py`, `telegram_notification.py`). This is the third.
  - **Tests only. No source change — the property already held.** `_auto_approve_instagram` has no `except` around its body at all; the `finally` guards only its own Cloudinary cleanup (`except Exception: pass`, scoped to that one call). A credential-resolution failure from `get_provider_for_media_item` was never inside that guard, so it already propagated. That is the expected outcome of closing a verification gap, not a defect narrowly avoided — reported as a finding rather than framed as a fix.
  - **Confirmed, not assumed.** Written against current `main` first: both new tests pass unmutated. Then mutation-checked — reintroducing a generic `except Exception: return None` around the body reddens both with the exact `DID NOT RAISE` signature, and a third, added positive-control test (a legitimate call, full success path) stays green under the same mutation, proving the tests discriminate rather than merely being strict.
  - **Injected at the same seam #819 used, for the same reason.** `get_provider_for_media_item` is patched with a `side_effect` — the point a tenant with broken OAuth actually reaches before any provider exists, not `provider.download_file`, which fires only after resolution already succeeded.
  - **Anti-vacuity, matching #819's pattern.** Every test asserts the mock was actually called, and with this tenant's `telegram_chat_id` — a fail-closed assertion that never reaches resolution passes for free against a method that bails out earlier (e.g. at the safety check).
  - **Both directions, same reason #819 gave.** A non-auth `ValueError` also escapes, unmarked as a control — this caller's fail-closed property comes from having no handler at all, not from special-casing auth, and asserting only the auth case would leave a narrower future catch green.
  - **#797 stays open — not closed by this PR.** `_auto_approve_instagram` propagates cleanly, confirmed above, but it has exactly one call site (`scheduler.py:652-663`) and that caller wraps it in a generic `except Exception as e:  # noqa: BLE001`. A raised auth error and a benign `return None` converge on the identical outcome — `instagram_story_id = None`, a warning log, the row released for retry. The method fails closed; the system does not. #797's original framing (this caller swallows the error generically) was right about the *caller*, not the *callee* this PR pins — both readings are correct, about different properties. Found in review by virgil; the trace is on #797.

### Security

- **The settings boundary now redacts BOTH ways a settings load can fail, not just the one we noticed (#780)** — `Settings.__init__` caught `pydantic.ValidationError` and nothing else. `pydantic_settings` resolves raw values from env/dotenv/secrets in a SOURCE layer that runs *before* pydantic validates anything, and on failure raises its own `SettingsError` — a `ValueError`, unrelated to `ValidationError` by inheritance, so `except ValidationError` structurally could not see it. Both exits now converge on the same redacted, unchained `SettingsError`.
  - **It is a LEAK, not just a bypass, and that is a correction to how this was previously recorded.** The escaping error's own message names a field and a source class and quotes no value — which is why it read as merely structural. But it is chained `from e`, and for a complex field that `e` is a `json.JSONDecodeError` carrying the **entire undecoded input on `.doc`, untruncated**. Measured: a plain `pytest --showlocals` on the escaping path printed a synthetic credential **nine times**; the same invocation on the already-fixed `ValidationError` path printed it **zero** times. That positive control is what makes the nine attributable to the missing catch rather than to `--showlocals` being generally leaky. It is also strictly worse than the #775 defect this work began with, where pydantic's own ~22-character truncation at least bounded the disclosure.
  - **Severing the chain is the load-bearing half.** Redacting the message alone would accomplish nothing here, because the value was never in the message. The raise happens outside the `except` block for the same reason #777 does it: `from None` only sets `__suppress_context__`, leaving the `JSONDecodeError` — and its `.doc` — on `__context__` for any logger, debugger or traceback renderer to reach. After the fix both `__cause__` and `__context__` are `None`.
  - **The field name is verified against the model before being emitted, never merely extracted.** Reading anything out of a third-party message is the discipline `_redact` already refuses for `msg`, and the refusal earns its keep: `pydantic_settings`' CLI source formats its message as `f'Parsing error encountered for {field_name}: {e}'`, interpolating an underlying exception that may quote its input. That source is unused here, but "the message happens to be safe in the sources we happen to use" is a property of the installed release, not of the library. The emitted line is therefore provably one of two things — a declared field name, or `<unknown>`.
  - **The import is aliased, and the collision is not hypothetical**: `pydantic_settings` ships a `SettingsError` and so does `src/config/settings.py`. An unqualified import would shadow one or the other by import order, in the one module where the difference between them is the entire subject.
  - **Dormant as declared, and measured rather than assumed** — 42 fields, zero complex-typed, zero aliases, no `env_prefix`. The trigger is a **required** `list`/`dict`/nested-model field; an `Optional`-wrapped one degrades safely to the redacted validation path (pydantic-settings sets `allow_parse_failure` for union-wrapped complex fields), which a positive control pins so the fix is not over-scoped to "any complex field". Verified on the installed pydantic-settings **2.14.0**, not inherited from a note written against 2.14.2.
  - **Tests fail against the unfixed code, and for the right reason.** Captured before the fix: 3 failed / 9 passed, the three failing on `pydantic_settings.exceptions.SettingsError` escaping `Settings.__init__` — not on a fixture that happened to omit the credential. The defect is reached through a **subclass of the real `Settings`**, which inherits the real boundary, so a dormant path is exercised against production code without adding a complex field to shipped configuration.
  - **Six mutants, all killed, and one of them only after a test was added for it.** Removing the catch, chaining `from exc`, using `from None`, swallowing silently and dropping the field-name check each redden. Passing the library's message straight through **survived all twelve original tests** — the direct unit test of the redactor drives it by hand, so it proves the helper correct and says nothing about whether the boundary calls it, and the library's current message quotes no value so no leak assertion fires. Killed by asserting the emitted **vocabulary** is this project's, which is what makes "no value can reach this string" a property of our code rather than a lucky fact about the installed release.
  - **The leak detector itself had to be strengthened first, and this is the part worth carrying forward.** The existing `_render` helper walked the cause chain using `str()` and `repr()` — and `JSONDecodeError`'s `str()` is just `"Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)"`. Pointed at an exception holding the whole credential on `.doc`, it reported **zero leaked characters**. A leak test built on it would have gone green against a live disclosure. It now dumps each exception's attribute state as well.

- **A second, LIVE leak of the entire `.env` file, found while fixing the dormant one — the boundary now ends in a default-deny rung rather than a list of classes (#831)** — a single non-UTF-8 byte in `.env` (a latin-1 character in a password, a pasted smart quote) raises `UnicodeDecodeError` while `DotEnvSettingsSource` is being **constructed**, before any source is called. pydantic-settings' own `except Exception -> SettingsError` funnel lives *inside* the call, so it never sees it either, and neither enumerated class matches. It escaped `Settings.__init__` and, because `src/config/settings.py` builds a `Settings` at module scope, escaped **module import**.
  - **Not dormant, unlike #780.** No subclass, no complex field, no alias — the shipped 42-field configuration. Where #780 needs someone to add a required `list`/`dict` field first, this needs one mistyped byte in a file operators edit by hand.
  - **The payload is the whole file.** Measured on a 163-byte fixture: `UnicodeDecodeError.object` carried all 163 bytes and **three of three** synthetic credentials, while `str()` and the rendered traceback carried **zero**. Same blind spot as `.doc`, one level up and much larger — and the same reason a message-only redactor cannot see either.
  - **`ValueError` is the width, deliberately not `Exception`.** It is the library's own altitude: `SettingsError` subclasses it and `sources/base.py` catches it, and every credential-carrying escape found here is one. `except Exception` would also swallow genuine programming errors and report them with no traceback (the raise happens outside the handler) — trading a credential leak for a debugging cliff. `TypeError` and `AttributeError` still propagate intact, pinned by a test.
  - **This changes which clause carries the security property, and the mutation matrix says so plainly.** With the tail rung in place, deleting the `SettingsError` clause no longer reddens the leak assertions — a source error simply falls through and is redacted anyway. That clause now buys **diagnosability** (it names the failing field; the tail rung can only name the failure class), and it is still pinned by two tests. The guarantee itself rests on the rung.
  - **Eight mutants, all killed**, including the two on the new rung: removing it, and having it echo the exception instead of its class name. Tests captured RED against the two-clause version first — 2 failed, on `UnicodeDecodeError` escaping — and each carries a premise test asserting the payload is genuinely still reachable, so neither can quietly become vacuous.

### Fixed

- **The M.2 spec's SD-M2-3 safety claim no longer wears a citation its source does not support (#790 follow-up, #835 review)** — the row cited "#787's measurement" for production having no `svc_*` roles, but #787 enumerated schemas and tables, never `pg_roles`; a safety claim with a citation reads as verified, which is exactly what makes a mis-citation worse than no citation. The claim is load-bearing for the hazard contrast and is kept — restated as what it is (an inference from program state: no window has ever opened, and the bootstrap is the only printed creator of the six service roles other than `svc_migration` — which `04` §0.2 prints as a 0.2 **pre-window** creation whose production execution is open from here), and converted into a measurement of the session's own catalog at the one moment a session exists: P1.b's read-only first-contact assertion gains one SELECT of `pg_roles`, read before P5 ever runs, with its three readings routed explicitly (fresh world — P5 runs; the documented 0.2-Login world — recorded, P5 proceeds on the inherited role; an upstream bootstrap — halt) — the reviewer's preferred placement, riding an existing step rather than becoming a new one.
